#10543. AMD VP and chief Zen architect rebuke claims that Nvidia and Arm made CPUs for agentic AI — ‘We didn’t magically know four years ago that agentic was going to be a thing’

AMD’s new Venice chips aren’t agentic CPUs, but they’re the best CPUs for agentic AI, says AMD.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Two prominent voices within AMD agree that the company didn’t design its new Zen 6 family, codenamed Venice, explicitly for agentic AI in a clear shot at Nvidia and Arm, who’ve both described their latest data center CPUs as “agentic” in nature. In separate talks, AMD’s chief Zen architect Mike Clark and corporate VP Madhu Rangarajan said that the company didn’t predict the agentic wave when Zen 6 development started.

We just wrapped up testing AMD's Venice CPUs, which you can read more about in our Zen 6 benchmarking article.

CPU development cycles are measured in years, and given that the agentic frenzy around CPUs hit around March of this year, it’s unreasonable to expect that anyone has developed silicon specifically for agentic AI, much less a general-purpose data center CPU. And still, Nvidia claims its Vera CPU was “purpose-built” for agentic AI, while Arm says its AGI CPU was “designed for the demands of agentic AI.”

“We think that the EPYC 9006 CPU is the best CPU for the agentic era, and spoiler alert, we didn’t magically know four years ago that agentic was going to be a thing and purpose-built something for agentic,” said Rangarajan in a session with media. “The reality is an agentic workflow is a diverse set of tasks… and in order to execute those tasks well, you need compute that is able to handle a diverse set of workloads with a full portfolio.”

Introducing the Zen 6 microarchitecture, Mike Clark agreed with the sentiment: “I’m not going to mislead and act like I knew agentic computing was coming, or we knew it, but we did strongly believe that the CPU would always have a place in the software ecosystem of AI… And as agentic [has] blown up, we [are] able to now deploy that for agentic compute workloads.”

As AMD rolls out its next-generation microarchitecture with Venice CPUs, it’s targeting the messaging from Arm and Nvidia, which have posed the most direct threat to AMD’s continued rise in the data center on the back of agentic workloads. And indeed, there are performance vectors where Nvidia’s Vera looks strong for agentic workloads, as you can see in our Nvidia Vera testing. AMD’s argument, however, is that agentic AI calls for a broad range of CPUs just as general-purpose workloads do, pushing back on the single-SKU approach of Nvidia and Arm.

Our testing of two AMD Venice chips supports that argument, as well, with the dense EPYC 9996 with its 256 cores and 512 threads offering peak throughput performance, while the frequency-optimized EPYC 9686F goes toe-to-toe with Vera in the role of an AI host node. AMD is launching two additional ranges next year, with Venice-X targeting HPC with its 3D-stack cache, and Verano going directly after Vera as an AI host node with an LPDDR5X memory system.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Although the workload characteristics of agentic AI are still in the process of being defined, we have a better understanding of how those loops work now than we did at the beginning of the year. And like all complex topics, the answer to what is important in a CPU for agentic AI is the always-disappointing “it depends.”

As we break down in our early testing procedure for agentic AI, the workload characteristics and demand on the CPU/memory are defined by what the agent is doing, and in the data center, what several agents are doing at high concurrency when sandboxed. A coding agent will generally call more tools that are taxing on the CPU than a web browsing agent, for example, while an agent that queries a database may call for strong memory performance.

Of course, there are some vectors of performance that can’t be addressed with different SKUs (such as that strong memory performance). However, the potential diversity of agentic workloads certainly makes an argument for CPU variety. At a most basic level, an agentic loop allows the LLM to understand functions you’ve defined, and then pass arguments to those functions to execute them. We’re talking about a workload as broad as what you can define in a function in languages like Python and Rust.

#10541. This new qubit could be 100 times less error-prone in superfluid quantum computer breakthrough

Researchers at the University of Surrey have proposed a new kind of qubit that could help address one of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing: keeping error rates low as systems become larger.

The concept uses superfluid helium, a form of helium with unusual quantum properties, to create a qubit that may be less sensitive to some of the disturbances that affect today's leading quantum computers.

Why Quantum Computers Struggle With Errors

Quantum computers rely on qubits to store and process information in ways that conventional computers cannot. Many current systems use superconducting circuits, but these are highly sensitive to electromagnetic noise and stray electrical charges - similar to the static electricity that can make hair cling to a balloon.

Even very small disturbances can disrupt the fragile quantum information stored in a qubit. As more qubits are added, controlling those errors becomes increasingly difficult, making scalability one of the central challenges in quantum computing.

In a study published in npj Quantum Information, researchers from Surrey's Quantum Sciences Group describe a different approach built around superfluid helium-3 - an unusual form of liquid helium that can flow without friction.

Their proposed device, called the Superfluid Helium Oscillator Quantum (SHOQ) device, would use charge-neutral superfluid helium. Because the helium carries no electric charge, the design could be naturally protected from certain types of electromagnetic noise.

A Qubit With Much Lower Predicted Error Rates

According to the researchers, this is the first reported design for a qubit based on a superfluid. Their calculations suggest the SHOQ device could have error rates about 100 times lower than conventional superconducting qubits.

Dr. Priya Sharma, Daphne Jackson Fellow in Hybrid Quantum Systems at the University of Surrey's School of Mathematics and Physics, and lead author of the study, said:

"We are not the first to think about the individual components behind this idea, but what we have done for the first time is bring them together in a microfluidic device and work out the specific details that could enable the device to function as a qubit.

"The maths tells us that it should work. We have taken what we already know about superfluid helium and quantum technologies and turned that into an educated design, with the parameters and specifications needed to build one. The next step is to make a prototype and put those predictions to the test."

Rather than replacing existing quantum computing hardware, the researchers say the SHOQ device could potentially be integrated with superconducting quantum technology.

That could allow different types of qubits to perform different jobs within the same system, depending on their strengths.

One longer-term possibility is to use the SHOQ device as a form of quantum memory. In that setup, a superfluid based qubit could store quantum information while other hardware carries out calculations.

Dr. Eran Ginossar, Associate Professor at the University of Surrey's Department of Physics and Advanced Technology Institute, and co-author of the study, said:

"We don't necessarily need one type of qubit to do everything. Combining different quantum technologies could allow us to take advantage of the strengths of each.

"Superfluid helium gives us a fundamentally different type of quantum hardware to explore. If the predicted performance can be demonstrated experimentally, it could eventually work alongside existing superconducting technologies as part of a larger quantum system."

The team now plans to build a prototype to find out whether the predicted performance can be reproduced in a real device. That effort is being supported by an IAA Commercialisation Fellowship awarded to Dr. Priya Sharma.

The SHOQ device would need to operate at extremely low temperatures, but researchers have already reached the necessary conditions in previous experiments involving superfluid helium-3.

The project was led by the University of Surrey in collaboration with Professor Jens Koch at Northwestern University in the United States. Koch was one of the researchers involved in developing the transmon - a superconducting qubit design that is now widely used in quantum computing.

Materials provided by University of Surrey. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10539. Samsung’s next cheap phone will have a much bigger battery than the Galaxy S26 Ultra

According to a new report, Samsung’s upcoming mid-range smartphone is getting a big battery upgrade that outdoes that company’s current $1,400 flagship.

GalaxyClub reports that the Samsung Galaxy A38 will ship with a 6,000 mAh battery. That’s a 20% jump over Samsung’s typical 5,000 mAh batteries that have been in Galaxy A series phones for the past few years, and is also a 20% jump from the company’s current flagship Galaxy S26 Ultra.

Compared to the likes of many other Android phones on the international market, 6,000 mAh isn’t huge. Honor, for example, launched new phones with 10,000 mAh batteries just a couple of weeks ago. But in the context of Samsung, this is a welcome step forward. The company finally embraced silicon-carbon in its latest foldables and, while it’s unclear if that’s what we’re seeing here, it’s very obvious that Samsung is making a push to increase its battery capacity moving forward.

Based on the Galaxy A37’s launch date, the Galaxy A38 is likely to make its debut in early 2027.

Galaxy S27 is rumored to make some minor battery upgrades as well.

#10536. Scientists recreate a bizarre quantum effect that stops a particle cold

A particle does not have to pass through a magnetic field to be influenced by it. That surprising feature of quantum physics has now been recreated by researchers at the University of Oxford, who used a hybrid quantum computer to demonstrate how an invisible magnetic influence can bring a simulated particle's movement to a complete stop.

The experiment reproduced a phenomenon known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect, in which a charged particle traveling around a region containing magnetic flux experiences a measurable change in its quantum state, even though its path never takes it through the magnetic field itself.

The Oxford team achieved this using a quantum system that combines qubits, the basic units of quantum information, with quantum oscillators, which represent vibrating motion. Their findings, published in Nature Physics, demonstrate how this combination could help scientists investigate fundamental interactions between matter and fields that are increasingly difficult to simulate using conventional computers.

A Strange Quantum Effect Predicted in 1959

In everyday physics, a charged particle responds to the electric and magnetic fields it encounters. Quantum mechanics, however, reveals that particles can behave in ways that have no straightforward counterpart in our ordinary experience.

In 1959, physicists Yakir Aharonov and David Bohm predicted that a charged particle could be affected by magnetic flux even if it traveled entirely outside the region containing the magnetic field. Rather than experiencing a conventional magnetic force along its path, the particle would undergo a change in its quantum phase.

Quantum phase describes an aspect of a particle's wave behavior that influences how different possible paths interfere with one another. Although the phase itself is not directly visible, differences in phase can produce measurable effects.

The prediction became known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect and was eventually verified in experiments involving real electrons. It remains one of the clearest demonstrations that the quantum world operates according to rules that go beyond the familiar laws of classical physics.

Researchers are now exploring how this phenomenon behaves in more complex theoretical systems known as lattice gauge theories.

These theories describe how matter interacts with fields associated with fundamental forces. They represent space as a grid, with matter located at individual points and fields associated with the connections between them. Such models are particularly important in particle physics and high-energy physics, where scientists study the basic particles and interactions that make up the universe.

The challenge is that the mathematics becomes extraordinarily demanding as these systems grow more complicated. Even powerful conventional computers can struggle to calculate how all the interacting components evolve.

Quantum computers offer a different approach. Instead of attempting to calculate every aspect of a quantum system using classical computing methods, scientists can construct another quantum system that follows the same underlying rules. They can then observe its behavior directly, an approach known as quantum simulation.

How Scientists Built the Quantum Experiment

The Oxford project began in 2022, when lead author Dr. Sebastian Saner, Dr. Oana Bazavan, and their colleagues started developing a way to simulate lattice gauge theories using a hybrid quantum system. They collaborated with Dr. Alejandro Bermudez of the Instituto de Física Teórica in Madrid.

Their experimental design relied on two different quantum components that performed separate functions.

The first consisted of qubits encoded in the internal states of trapped ions, which are electrically charged atoms held in place using electromagnetic fields. These qubits represented the gauge fields in the theoretical model.

The second component used quantum oscillators, created by the vibrations of those same trapped ions. These oscillators represented matter.

By combining the two, the researchers constructed a simple loop that served as a basic building block of a lattice gauge theory. Two oscillators represented matter at two different locations, while two qubits represented the fields connecting those locations.

Although the arrangement was small, it was sufficient to reproduce an important interaction between matter and magnetic flux.

Quantum Entanglement Creates a Surprising Solution

The researchers next placed the two qubits into an entangled quantum state. Entanglement is a phenomenon in which quantum systems become linked in ways that cannot be fully explained by classical physics.

Within the team's simulated lattice gauge theory, this entangled state represented magnetic flux passing through the center of the loop.

Initially, the researchers had planned to introduce the magnetic flux as a fixed background condition. However, creating the necessary interaction would have required capabilities that their quantum hardware did not possess.

Encoding the flux directly into the qubits provided a practical alternative. More importantly, it allowed the magnetic flux to become part of the evolving quantum system rather than remaining an unchanging external influence.

"For us, the exciting step was to encode the magnetic flux in a gauge field that was itself dynamical," says Dr. Saner. "Rather than having matter evolve in a fixed background, the matter and gauge field become part of the same quantum dynamics."

What began as a technical workaround ultimately enabled the researchers to explore a more interesting version of the phenomenon, one in which matter and the field could participate together in the quantum dynamics.

A Quantum Effect That Stops Particle Motion

With their simulated loop in place, the scientists examined how a matter particle moved between locations through a process known as quantum tunneling.

Quantum tunneling allows particles to move between states in ways that would not be possible under ordinary classical rules.

When the researchers ran their simulation without magnetic flux, the particle could tunnel freely around the loop.

Introducing the simulated flux produced a strikingly different result.

The particle had two possible paths around the loop, but the magnetic flux changed their relative quantum phases. As a result, the two paths interfered destructively, meaning their quantum contributions canceled each other out.

This interference completely suppressed the tunneling, leaving the simulated particle frozen in its original state.

The outcome demonstrated the Aharonov-Bohm effect within a lattice gauge theory containing a dynamical field. Rather than simply reproducing the familiar behavior of a particle moving around a fixed magnetic influence, the experiment showed how the phenomenon can emerge when matter and the field are both represented by interacting quantum components.

A New Tool for Exploring Fundamental Physics

The Oxford findings were published alongside a related study by researchers at the University of Maryland, led by Professor Norbert Linke.

The Maryland team also used a hybrid quantum system, but their experiment focused on simulating the Yukawa potential, a mathematical description of an interaction relevant to nuclear and particle physics.

Although the two research groups developed their methods independently, they later coordinated the submission of their studies to Nature Physics.

Together, the experiments demonstrate how hybrid quantum computers could become valuable tools for investigating fundamental interactions that are difficult to reproduce using classical simulations.

By combining different types of quantum components, researchers can build systems in which matter and fields are represented in ways that more closely reflect the physics they want to study.

The Oxford experiment provides an example of that potential. A phenomenon first predicted in 1959 and later observed with electrons has now been recreated in a quantum simulator where the field itself participates in the dynamics, offering scientists another way to explore the unusual rules governing the quantum world.

Materials provided by University of Oxford. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10534. Android Auto’s latest Gemini failing keeps asking ‘who do you want to call’

The switch to Gemini on Android Auto has resulted in some good functionality add-ons, but also some very noticeable slip-ups. The latest in the bunch is a problem where Gemini won’t recognize names when you’re trying to make a call or send a text via voice commands on Android Auto.

A trending issue right now on Google’s forums, Android Auto users say that Gemini gets caught in a loop of asking who it is that you want to call or send a message to. You can say the name repeatedly, but Gemini fails to finish the command and just starts over.

The issue isn’t isolated to any particular phone or Android Auto build, as is often the case with Gemini bugs, and started showing up early in October, with more reports showing up recently. Some users also report issues with Android Auto automatically answering calls, but that’s much more isolated.

Google is constantly rolling out new updates for Android Auto and Gemini, so it’s likely only a matter of time until this one is fixed. If you’re running into this problem, let us know in the comments below.

#10532. Retrying a send is harder than sending

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every integration that moves something out of your system has the same weak point, and it is

not the happy path. It is the second attempt.

Your code posts a document to a provider. The connection drops before the response arrives. You now hold no job id, no confirmation and no idea whether anything left the building. The timeout told you about your network. It told you nothing about theirs.

So you retry, and you either send the thing twice or you never send it at all. Pick one, because

without help from the API you cannot have both.

The first instinct is a client-side guard. Hash the payload, keep a table of what you have already

That works until the duplicate attempt comes from a different process, a different container, or a

queue worker that started after a deploy. Your table is local state defending against a distributed

The second instinct is to ask the provider whether the job exists. That means listing recent jobs

and matching on something, usually the recipient and a timestamp window. It is a search, and

searches have false positives. Two legitimate sends to the same recipient a minute apart look exactly like one send and one retry.

The third instinct is to make the operation naturally idempotent. That works beautifully for a database upsert and fails completely for anything that leaves your control. A document that has been transmitted cannot be un-transmitted by a second call.

An idempotency key, generated by the caller, attached to the request, and honoured by the

The contract is simple. The caller invents a unique string per logical operation. Every attempt

carries the same string. The first request to arrive does the work and records the result against that key. Every later request carrying that string gets the recorded result back instead of doing the work again.

The important part is where the state lives. It is on the server, which is the only place that knows

whether the job exists. Your retry loop stops needing to be clever. It just needs to reuse the key.

Stripe popularised this pattern and the reason it spread is that the alternative is unbounded.

Without it, every caller writes their own deduplication, badly, and the failure only shows up under load.

Document and messaging APIs are where it matters most, because the side effect is visible to

someone outside your company. Fax for Agents is a current example of the pattern taken

seriously. It requires an Idempotency-Key on every mutating call, so a retry hands back the

same job instead of creating a second one.

Idempotency solves the duplicate. It does not solve the other half, which is that you still do not know what happened.

Most integrations model this as a boolean. Sent, or not sent. That is wrong, and you discover it

the first time a transmission half completes.

A useful state machine for a send has more room in it. Fax for Agents publishes six states:

queued, active, partial, failed, unknown and delivered. Two of those are the interesting ones.

unknown means the provider has not said. Not failed, not delivered. The correct response to

unknown is to wait. A retry loop that treats unknown as failure needs rewriting, because that is

how you produce duplicates even with idempotency in place.

partial means some of it arrived. Resending the whole document would deliver the first pages

twice, so partial is a state that must never be retransmitted automatically. It needs a person, or a

And delivered should be the narrowest word in the vocabulary. In that API it is written only when

the provider's evidence for the attempt supports it, never when the request was merely

accepted. The gap between "we accepted your job" and "it arrived" is where most false

Four questions, answerable from the documentation before you write a line of code.

Does it accept a caller-supplied idempotency string on every mutating call, and for how long

How many terminal states does a job have? If the answer is two, the API is hiding something

Is there a state meaning "we do not know yet", and does the documentation say what you should do when you see it?

When a webhook arrives, can you verify it came from them? Signed delivery with an event id is

the difference between a webhook handler and an open endpoint.

None of that is exotic. It is the difference between an integration that degrades and one that

#10530. Physicists discover “astonishing” property of light that defies expectations

Scientists have discovered a surprising ability of light that allows it to develop spinning patterns as it moves through empty space, without relying on special lenses, mirrors, or materials. The finding reveals that light possesses a hidden geometric structure that can influence its behavior in ways researchers previously thought were difficult to achieve.

The discovery comes from an international team led by researchers at the University of East Anglia (UEA) in the United Kingdom, working with scientists in South Africa. Their experiments demonstrate that carefully prepared light can be controlled through its own internal geometry, offering a new approach to manipulating how light spins and twists.

The findings challenge long-standing assumptions about how different properties of light interact. They could eventually lead to more sensitive medical tests, faster and more secure communications, and advanced quantum technologies that use light to transmit and protect information.

Why the Direction of Light's Spin Matters

One of the most intriguing aspects of the discovery involves chirality, a scientific term describing the difference between left-handed and right-handed structures.

Human hands provide a familiar example. Although they are mirror images of each other, a left hand cannot be perfectly superimposed on a right hand. Many molecules have a similar property, including compounds used in medicines. Two versions of the same molecule can have nearly identical chemical structures yet produce very different effects in the body.

Scientists can distinguish these molecular forms by studying how they interact with light that has a particular rotational behavior. Traditionally, producing and controlling such light has often involved specialized materials, carefully designed surfaces, or powerful lenses that focus beams extremely tightly.

The research demonstrates that appropriately prepared light can develop this behavior naturally as it travels, without requiring those additional optical components.

"Our work shows that light can naturally develop this handed behavior all on its own," said Dr. Kayn Forbes from UEA's School of Chemistry, Pharmacy and Pharmacology.

"You just have to prepare it in the right way.

"Most people think of light as traveling in straight lines. But scientists can also create structured light -- light whose brightness, shape, and direction are carefully arranged.

"One extreme example is light that twists as it travels, forming a corkscrew shape known as an optical vortex. Each twist can carry information, making this kind of light valuable for high-speed internet, secure communications, and advanced sensors.

"Light can also spin as it travels, depending on how it is polarized. This spin can be left-handed or right-handed -- another form of chirality."

Scientists Discover How Light Develops a Hidden Spin

Light can exhibit different kinds of rotational behavior. An optical vortex, for example, has a wave pattern that twists around its direction of travel, much like a corkscrew. Light also has a property known as polarization, which describes how its electric field oscillates. Certain polarization patterns produce a form of rotation called spin.

Physicists have long studied the relationship between these two properties. However, the interactions responsible for the newly observed effect were generally considered extremely weak and difficult to detect without specialized experimental conditions.

The researchers found that the situation changes when a beam of light is prepared in a particular balanced configuration.

Under those conditions, the beam can initially have no spin, yet distinct regions with spinning behavior emerge as it travels through free space. The effect does not require the light to pass through engineered materials or additional optical devices.

"It starts off with no spin at all," explained MSc student Light Mkhumbuza, who carried out key experiments. "But as the beam travels forward, spinning regions appear and separate out -- almost as if the spin was hiding and then revealed itself."

Rather than creating spin through an external optical component, the researchers demonstrated how the beam's existing internal structure can determine where spinning regions develop during propagation.

To explain this unexpected behavior, the researchers turned to topology, a mathematical field concerned with properties that remain unchanged when objects are continuously stretched, bent, or reshaped.

Dr. Isaac Nape, from the University of the Witwatersrand in Johannesburg, South Africa, offered a simple way to understand the concept.

"To explain it, imagine a mug and a doughnut," he said. "You can morph one into the other without tearing it, because they both have one hole. That hole is a topological feature."

The researchers found that structured light possesses its own topological characteristics, encoded in the arrangement of its polarization.

This underlying pattern acts like a mathematical fingerprint. Even as the light beam changes shape while traveling, its topological structure remains, influencing how different regions of the beam evolve.

As a result, the arrangement of the light itself can determine where its left-handed and right-handed spinning patterns become visible.

The discovery gives scientists a way to guide this behavior by designing the beam's initial structure, rather than depending on specialized materials to produce the desired effect.

"This gives us a completely new tuning knob for light. By adjusting its topology, we can decide how and where chirality appears," said Dr. Nape.

Potential Breakthroughs in Medicine and Communications

The ability to control light through its geometry could eventually make several important optical technologies simpler, more affordable, and more versatile.

One particularly promising application involves medical diagnostics and pharmaceutical research. Because many biologically important molecules have left-handed and right-handed forms, carefully structured light could help scientists identify these differences with greater sensitivity. Such tools could be valuable for evaluating drug safety and detecting biological markers associated with disease.

The same principles could support smaller optical sensors capable of identifying chemicals or biological substances without requiring large, sophisticated laboratory instruments.

Communications technology could also benefit. By encoding information into different twisting and spinning patterns, researchers may be able to increase the amount of data carried by individual light beams. This could contribute to faster optical communications, improved information security, and future quantum networks.

Other potential uses include manipulating microscopic particles, cells, and molecules with light, as well as developing more resilient quantum systems. Topological properties may help protect delicate quantum information against certain forms of noise and interference.

"The implications are wide-ranging," said Dr. Forbes. "This work could lead to simpler and more sensitive medical tests, especially in drug development.

"It could also be used to pack more information into laser beams -- boosting data capacity for communications, including future quantum networks.

"And because the effect doesn't rely on fragile materials or precision-engineered surfaces, it could be easier and cheaper to use in real-world technologies.

"This research could lay the foundations for a new generation of light-based technologies, by showing that light's behavior can be controlled using its own internal geometry," he added.

These applications remain possibilities for future development, but the underlying discovery provides researchers with another way to design and control the behavior of light.

A Hidden Property of Light Finally Revealed

For decades, scientists have relied on increasingly sophisticated optical equipment to produce and manipulate unusual forms of light. The findings suggest that some of these behaviors can instead arise from carefully designed structures already encoded within a light beam.

By showing how topology governs the emergence of spinning regions during propagation, the team has revealed a previously underappreciated connection between light's geometry and its physical behavior.

The discovery also illustrates how much remains to be learned about something as familiar and extensively studied as light.

"For something so familiar, light is proving to be far richer, stranger and more powerful than anyone imagined," said Dr. Forbes.

"And astonishingly, this new behavior has been there all along -- just waiting to be seen."

The research, 'Topological Control of Chirality and Spin with Structured Light', was published in the journal Light: Science & Applications.

Materials provided by University of East Anglia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10529. Microsoft lobbies against potential ban on Chinese optical networking gear for AI data centers as FCC mulls restricting imports

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The supply of this tiny module (or lack thereof) could derail the hundreds of massive data centers planned in the U.S.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Microsoft, the tech giant that is building 35 data centers around the U.S. and has plans for 134 more, has reportedly met with the White House Office of Science and Technology Policy and the National Security Council as the FCC is considering an import ban on Chinese optical transceivers. According to Reuters, the company, alongside the Information Technology Industry Council (ITI), is lobbying against the move.

“It really threatens to arguably grind (the data center rollout) to a halt,” ITI Executive Vice President of Policy and General Counsel John Miller said. He also added, “Those chips, without optical transceivers, literally could just sit there waiting around unutilized until you actually have the transceivers to essentially connect them together,” referring to the AI chips that data centers use.

Optical transceivers are tiny modules plugged into hardware that convert their electrical signals into light signals, allowing data to be transmitted as light pulses across fiber optics. Data centers increasingly use optical transceivers and fiber optics to transmit data between AI GPUs and server racks, as they’re faster, offer lower latency, require less power, and generate less heat compared to traditional copper connections. However, Chinese companies currently supply 66% of the global market, meaning banning them from supplying data centers in the U.S. would more than halve the available supply, thereby exacerbating the various shortages that have been pummeling the industry.

However, there are concerns that some of these transceivers could be compromised by Beijing and be used to steal data, install malware, or remotely disrupt American data centers, which is why the FCC is considering the restriction, just like how it banned Huawei from the U.S. telecommunications network. But unlike Huawei, which had many established competitors in the telecommunications hardware industry, only two U.S.-based companies produce optical transceivers that data centers need — Coherent and Lumentum. While these firms could produce optical transceivers to compete with Chinese offerings, they simply do not have the scale to make up for the shortfall if the FCC bans Chinese imports today.

One compromise floated by the Trump administration is that it would only ban Chinese optical transceivers capable of hitting 3.2 terabits per second. While this technology already exists, its production isn’t expected to expand until 2028, with installation not beginning until 2029. This should give American producers time to ramp up their own output, although the suggestion is still under consideration.

#10526. DroidDeck review: This could change how you game on your phone [Video]

The gaming landscape has changed drastically over the past year to the point where you’re able to effectively turn a capable phone into a fully fledged Steam Deck with projects like DroidDeck.

If you’ve been living under a rock for a bit, all you need to know is that it’s now technically possible to run full PC games directly on a high-end Android smartphone without relying on cloud streaming or rooting your device.

In effect, this turns a recent, compatible (more on that in a second) flagship phone into a de facto Steam Deck. I am still trying to wrap my head around how well it actually works, but we’ll try to give you a fair summation here with all the pros and cons.

A quick preface: I am a sucker for mobile gaming (I easily have over 30 hours clocked in Balatro across my devices), though the most taxing things I usually run are retro emulators. Being able to play full x86 PC titles locally on the device you already carry everywhere is a completely different beast – and why I am so excited by this project as a Steam Deck aficionado.

DroidDeck is a project that allows you to launch Valve’s Steam Big Picture interface natively on many recent Snapdragon-powered Android phones. It comes complete with the all-important Proton translation that has been so game-changing for Linux gaming, and it also includes custom graphics drivers for mobile devices.

What makes DroidDeck so approachable is that you do not need to mess around with terminal commands or complex emulation configurations. No piracy or ROMs required. It boots directly into the familiar Steam interface, allowing you to download and store titles locally on your internal storage, completely legally.

It’s very simple to get started; you simply grab the APK file from the official DroidDeck GitHub page. Install, sign in to your Steam account, and you’re 95% of the way to gaming.

During the initial DroidDeck setup, there is one critical step you cannot skip: select the appropriate Proton translation layer and enable the custom Mesa Turnip drivers in the application settings. This unlocks proper 3D acceleration on Snapdragon Adreno GPUs. If you boot up a game and cannot figure out why it is failing to launch, check that driver setting first. It was an issue I initially ran into, but I found out it was essential after some digging around in forums and Reddit posts.

Once you log into your Steam account, your library works exactly like it does on a Steam Deck.

You get full access to the store, automatic cloud saves plus backups, and even Steam Workshop support. When I booted up Brotato, all of my modded Workshop content carried over seamlessly. Better yet, because the games are installed locally, the entire experience works fully offline. Services like Nvidia GeForce NOW are great when you have a rock-solid connection, but because you’re running locally rather than streaming, DroidDeck makes PC gaming on your phone viable for a commute or a flight where you just want to play games unhindered.

Some of this may sound vaguely familiar if you have heard of another similar project called GameNative. It differs by acting as its own custom Android launcher rather than booting directly into Valve’s official Steam Big Picture interface. It is also built on top of existing open-source tools like Pluvia and Winlator; GameNative feels much more like a traditional mobile app that pulls in your libraries from Steam, Epic Games, GOG, and Amazon. Although you can add games from those platforms on DroidDeck if you really want to.

It has been around a bit longer and automatically applies community-shared configurations to get games running out of the box, though it relies on separate background containers per title rather than giving you that unified, console-like Steam Deck environment that makes DroidDeck feel like a better implementation strictly for gaming, in my opinion.

Just a quick cursory glance at Reddit of people using both, and it seems most people are having a better time with this implementation.

There is some good news and bad news with compatibility.

According to the official GitHub documentation, DroidDeck requires an Adreno 730 GPU or newer. That is the graphics chip paired with the Snapdragon 8 Gen 1, a processor that is roughly four years old now, meaning millions of phones out in the wild technically meet the minimum spec. Sadly, that list does not include any recent Google Pixel device. You could try to run on the beta track, but it’s not recommended.

Minimum specs and enjoyable specs are two very different things. Because DroidDeck is actively translating x86 PC instructions to run on ARM architecture, there is plenty of overhead. For a smooth experience right out of the gate, I recommend starting at a processor one generation newer than the Snapdragon 8 Gen 1.

If you want to pick up an older, cheaper flagship specifically to tinker with this, the OnePlus 12 is in a great sweet spot because it’s cheaper, has a great screen, and plenty of power. Make sure whatever device you use has at least 12GB of RAM (16GB is even better) and fast UFS 4.0 storage to help with in-game asset loading.

For my testing, I have been using a Galaxy S25 Ultra with DroidDeck installed for about 4 or 5 weeks so far. Talking about phones for my day job puts me in the privileged position of having last year’s flagship sitting around as a backup device, and its hardware is ideal for this. Modern flagship displays arguably outshine dedicated handhelds anyway. The 120Hz anti-glare AMOLED panel on the S25 Ultra is gorgeous and not much smaller than the Steam Deck OLED screen, meaning the Steam Big Picture UI scales cleanly with zero tiny, illegible text.

I wish I could say every single title in my library runs flawlessly, but that wouldn’t be completely honest. Since every game handles translation differently, performance varies wildly depending on the age and complexity of the game in question.

Older 3D classics and 2D indies are rock solid. I set my resolution to 1080p and jumped into Portal 2 for what is probably my sixth playthrough. It ran locked at 60fps, which is still mind-boggling to witness natively on a phone. Meanwhile, Burnout Paradise Remastered hit a fairly stable 60fps at 1080p with some dips into the high 40s, and a personal favorite, Dave the Diver, was solid but sometimes struggled to hit 45fps when lots of things were going on on-screen.

Dipping down to 720p is absolutely necessary for heavier titles, though let’s be honest, 720p (or 800p) is native for a Steam Deck anyway, so you’re not losing out if you want portability.

I was desperate to get Batman: Arkham Knight running nicely since I have been replaying it lately, and it landed right in the “not perfect, but just about playable” category. Even after tweaking the settings at 720p, it hovered around 30-40fps before it tanked hard when the skybox opened up and more assets were loaded in.

Cyberpunk 2077 failed to launch for me, which I expected, but I have no doubt in the future it will be running with some added work.

Beyond raw framerates, there are a few practical caveats you will run into immediately, starting with internal storage limits. Unless you bought a 512GB or 1TB phone, you cannot realistically keep three or four 50GB+ PC games installed alongside your daily apps and camera roll. I need space for photos and videos of my dog, so giving that up isn’t something I’m willing to do on my “main” phone. A backup or dedicated gaming handheld is fine, though, but with storage prices ballooning, it might be a costly side arm.

Thermal throttling is another unavoidable hurdle on a wafer-thin device with no active cooling. When a Snapdragon chip does this much heavy lifting, the chassis gets scorching hot fast, and performance eventually drops as the system throttles to protect itself.

Because of that excessive heat, a dedicated controller is practically mandatory just to keep from scalding your hands. All that thermal output also translates to rapid battery drain, meaning a demanding 3D game can easily wipe out a full charge in under an hour. Gaming phones with built-in cooling fans do exist, though I certainly wouldn’t recommend buying one just to squeeze out an extra twenty minutes of playtime here.

Even with the heat and storage hurdles, we are in the very early days of this project. Think of it this way: right now is the absolute worst that DroidDeck will ever be.

I don’t doubt we would have seen something like this without recent AI development progress, but talented devs have accelerated the work by tenfold in recent months. To go from a standing start to being able to run PC games on your phone almost natively is a heck of an achievement no matter which project you prefer.

As drivers mature and translation layers become more efficient, the ceiling for native PC gaming on Android is massive. At a time when dedicated gaming hardware and handheld PCs are ballooning in price, you no longer need to spend a fortune to play your Steam backlog on the go if you already own a powerful smartphone.

DroidDeck is completely free to download and tinker with, so you have nothing to lose by giving it a shot. Just make sure you have a spare controller handy before you boot it up.

#10525. AI can act exactly like a human, but we still don't think it's conscious

Even when artificial intelligence behaves exactly like a human, people are reluctant to consider it conscious, according to a new study from Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich (LMU).

As artificial intelligence becomes more advanced, reports of AI systems misleading users, working together, or appearing to pursue independent goals are becoming increasingly common. These behaviors often lead people to describe AI as though it can think, feel, or make decisions for itself. But does behaving like a human actually convince people that a machine has consciousness?

New research from LMU suggests that people make a clear distinction between displaying intelligent behavior and possessing a conscious mind, even when humans and AI act in precisely the same way.

How Scientists Tested Perceptions of AI Consciousness

Published in the journal Cognition, the study was led by Dr. Louis Longin of LMU's Chair of Philosophy of Mind, working alongside Dr. Bahador Bahrami, Professor Ophelia Deroy, and other researchers.

"Whereas previous studies have typically asked general questions about whether AI actually has mental states, our study is the first to directly compare how people attribute the same mental states to AI and humans behaving in exactly the same way, under identical circumstances," says Longin.

"We may be using mental terms to refer to AI, but this may only be because we lack better words," says Ophelia Deroy, philosopher and a senior author of the study. "After all, AI is trained to act and speak like a human, so these descriptions seem natural."

To investigate how people judge consciousness, the researchers conducted experiments involving nearly 1,100 participants. One group read brief descriptions of artificial agents that showed different levels of responsiveness to their environments. For example, some agents reacted to sounds or recognized the emotional states of others.

Another group encountered the same situations, except the characters were described as humans rather than AI systems. Participants then evaluated how conscious or aware each character seemed to be of its surroundings.

A Surprising Gap Between Human and AI Consciousness

The experiments revealed that responsiveness influenced how participants judged both humans and artificial intelligence. Characters that reacted more actively to their surroundings were generally considered more conscious and more aware.

However, a striking difference emerged when participants evaluated consciousness specifically. Even the most responsive AI was rated as less conscious than the least responsive human. In other words, identical behavior did not lead people to assign the same level of consciousness to humans and machines.

The results changed considerably when participants were asked about awareness instead. For this characteristic, judgments of humans and AI followed largely similar patterns, suggesting that people interpret awareness and consciousness in fundamentally different ways.

"There is a growing worry in public discussion that people will see AI behaving in a human-like way and start treating these systems as if they had human-like mental states," says Longin, lead author of the study. "We found something much more nuanced: People are quite willing to say that an AI notices things and is aware of its surroundings. But they clearly draw a line when it comes to the term 'consciousness'. People consistently attribute less consciousness to AI than humans -- even when their behavior is identical."

Although people sometimes describe AI as conscious when discussing its actions, the findings indicate that they still distinguish between artificial systems and human minds.

The researchers believe the difference between consciousness and awareness offers an important insight into how people understand artificial intelligence.

Awareness is a relatively neutral term that can describe an agent's ability to detect and respond to information in its environment. Consciousness, however, carries deeper implications about subjective experience and what it means to have an inner mental life.

Because awareness focuses more directly on observable behavior, people appear comfortable applying it to both humans and AI. Consciousness carries a different meaning, leading people to judge the two much less similarly.

The findings also highlight why the language used to discuss artificial intelligence matters, particularly as AI systems become increasingly capable of producing convincing human behavior.

"Our study teaches us lessons for how we communicate about AI," says Bahador Bahrami, who is also a senior author of the study and an expert in social neuroscience. "Companies and journalists often reach for hyped descriptions of AI models, and credit them with intentions, plans, or even moral conscience and hesitation. What our study shows is that everyday judgments are much more discriminating: People do not simply put AI and humans on the same mental scale. But the boundary also depends on which terms we use. For more neutral descriptors such as 'awareness', the distinction can largely disappear. That makes our choice of language important."

#10522. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#10519. People are taking an unapproved weight-loss drug. AI found something surprising

An experimental weight-loss drug that has not yet received regulatory approval is already attracting users willing to obtain unapproved versions online. Now, scientists are using artificial intelligence to investigate their experiences, uncovering unexpected symptom reports that differ from those documented in clinical trials.

The drug, retatrutide, belongs to a new generation of GLP-1 medications designed to help regulate appetite and body weight. Although it remains under investigation and is not approved for use, people are purchasing unapproved formulations that are not intended for human consumption and discussing their experiences on internet forums.

Researchers at the University of Pennsylvania analyzed nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments from approximately 40,000 users to investigate these accounts. Their study, published in JAMA Network Open, provides the first systematic examination of potential side effects reported by people claiming to use unapproved retatrutide.

One finding stood out. Among users whose reported symptoms could be classified, increased appetite was the most common complaint. That was particularly unexpected because retatrutide is designed to suppress hunger, and its Phase 2 clinical trials primarily identified digestive problems as the most frequent side effects.

AI Uncovers Unexpected Weight-Loss Drug Reports

"Our goal is to identify hypotheses in online conversations that we could test. AI helps us find recurring patterns in people's self-reports, including experiences that may be missed by conventional drug-safety monitoring," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS), Director of Penn's Computational Social Listening Lab and senior author of the study.

However, the researchers emphasize that the findings should not be interpreted as proof of previously unknown side effects. Reddit users represent a very different population from clinical trial participants, and their accounts cannot establish whether the drug caused the symptoms they described.

There is also considerable uncertainty about what these individuals are actually taking. Products sold outside regulated medical channels may differ from the genuine experimental medication in their ingredients, purity, and dosage.

"We can't say that retatrutide is causing these symptoms, or even that Reddit users are actually taking genuine retatrutide, since the drug is not legally available, and gray market formulations vary," says Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author of the study.

Rather than confirming new risks, the research offers scientists an opportunity to identify questions that warrant more rigorous investigation.

"This is the first glimpse into the experiences of a population that is otherwise outside conventional drug safety monitoring because they are taking unapproved versions of a drug that has yet to finish clinical trials," says Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS.

How Researchers Analyzed 150,000 Reddit Posts

The project builds on previous research by the University of Pennsylvania team. In that earlier investigation, scientists used large language models (LLMs), the type of artificial intelligence that powers many modern chatbots, to examine more than 400,000 Reddit posts discussing semaglutide and tirzepatide.

That analysis revealed reports of possible side effects, including menstrual irregularities, that may not have been fully documented in clinical trials or official drug safety records.

"We wanted to apply the same methodology to gray-market retatrutide," says Sehgal.

Retatrutide is particularly interesting because it works differently from existing GLP-1 medications. Semaglutide acts on one hormone receptor involved in blood sugar control and appetite regulation, while tirzepatide targets two. Retatrutide activates three receptors, potentially producing a different combination of effects throughout the body.

To investigate what people were experiencing, the researchers used AI to examine nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments. They identified approximately 14,000 users who claimed to have personally taken an unapproved version of retatrutide.

The AI systems then extracted descriptions of symptoms that users associated with the medication and translated those descriptions into standardized medical terminology. This allowed researchers to compare reports even when different people used different words to describe similar experiences.

After excluding expected effects such as weight loss and reduced appetite, the team identified 7,823 users who reported at least one symptom that could be medically classified.

"A researcher might be able to examine a few thousand Reddit posts by hand, but looking at nearly 150,000 would be an enormous undertaking," says Sehgal. "AI makes it possible to analyze these conversations at scale, identifying patterns in what people are sharing that would otherwise be difficult to recognize."

The most striking difference involved hunger.

In retatrutide's Phase 2 clinical trials (the drug is currently in Phase 3 trials, the final step before FDA approval), participants most commonly experienced gastrointestinal complaints, including nausea. These effects are familiar concerns with medications that influence appetite and digestion.

But among Reddit users who reported taking unapproved retatrutide, the pattern looked different. Increased appetite emerged as the most frequently described classifiable symptom, despite the substantial weight-loss effects demonstrated in clinical testing.

The researchers also compared the new findings with their earlier analysis of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

Compared with those earlier Reddit reports, discussions involving unapproved retatrutide contained proportionally more mentions of appetite-related changes, sleep disturbances, increased heart rate, and muscle-related symptoms. Reports of nausea and other digestive complaints were proportionally less common.

These differences could reflect how retatrutide interacts with the body. However, they could also result from differences between the groups of people discussing the medications, their other drug use, or the contents of the unapproved products.

"Retatrutide works differently from existing GLP-1 drugs, so it's possible that its effects on the body are different, too," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study.

"But the people discussing it on Reddit also may be different," she notes. "Users willing to obtain an unapproved medication on the gray market likely differ from other clinical populations. Some may be taking multiple experimental peptides, and we can't verify exactly what they're taking. We need more research to understand what, if anything, these differences in self-reported symptoms mean."

Can AI Detect Drug Safety Concerns Earlier?

Beyond the findings about retatrutide, the study demonstrates how AI could potentially improve the early detection of drug safety concerns.

Traditional monitoring systems depend heavily on clinical trials, reports submitted to regulators, and information collected by healthcare professionals. People obtaining experimental medications through unapproved channels may not be represented in those systems.

Social media offers another source of information, allowing researchers to identify recurring experiences that might otherwise escape attention. However, these reports must be independently investigated before they can be considered reliable evidence of a drug-related problem.

The Penn team has already seen an example of how this approach can help guide further research.

In their previous investigation of semaglutide and tirzepatide, researchers identified online reports of menstrual irregularities. Those findings subsequently encouraged another scientific team to examine similar symptoms using the FDA's database of reported adverse drug events.

The follow-up study, published in Obstetrics & Gynecology, also detected disproportionate reports of certain menstrual changes. Although those findings did not establish causation, they provided additional reasons to investigate the potential connection.

The researchers hope their retatrutide findings will lead to similar independent studies, particularly as the drug advances through clinical development.

"That's what we hope will happen with this study," says Sehgal. "Other researchers can take what we find on Reddit and look for the same patterns in other datasets, using entirely different methods. Whether those signals hold up or not, the goal is to build a better understanding of what people are actually experiencing."

The research was conducted through the University of Pennsylvania's School of Engineering and Applied Science and Perelman School of Medicine. Benjamin Rader of Boston Children's Hospital also contributed as a co-author. Funding was provided in part by the University of Pennsylvania's Penn Medical Communication Research Institute. Tronieri disclosed receiving an investigator-initiated grant from Novo Nordisk on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, along with consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. No conflicts of interest were reported by the other authors.

Stingray systems are deployed in salmon pens along the Norwegian coast, protecting over 112 million fish.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Submarine AI laser robots have been a surefire success for Norwegian salmon farmers, according to the most recent annual report from the aptly named Stingray Marine Solutions. The Oslo, Norway-based firm now has over 3,500 units deployed around salmon pens along the Norwegian coast, protecting over 112 million fish. Stingray deployments are up by about 1,000 over the last year. This operation has grown to become one of the largest commercial AI deployments in Scandinavia.

These high-tech AI-enhanced submarines protect 20-25% of farmed salmon in the country, according to the latest figures. Using this high-tech AI laser solution to ‘optically delouse’ the salmon means fish don’t have to be passed through harsh chemical baths to prevent infestations of these tiny parasitic crustaceans. The result is improved salmon growth and welfare, and consistently cleaner fish exhibit reduced mortality.

The video above shows a behind-the-scenes peek at the 250-employee-strong Stingray operation. There’s some colorful laser action with two Stingrays seen zapping away in a salmon pen included in the clip. They use AI to recognize and target the sea lice.

Sea lice are a big problem for fish farmers, with the control of these parasites being a central challenge for the industry. Traditionally, measures such as chemical treatments were used to help control the buildup of sea lice. However, while chemicals can treat a large number of fish simultaneously, they can be bad for the environment, can stress fish, lice can develop resistance, and the introduction of any kind of poison or toxin into food production isn’t usually good for humans either.

In contrast, the Stingray AI laser system identifies and targets only sea lice, works continuously 24/7 as the fish go around normally in their pens, and avoids any chemical introduction or resistance issues. The reduction in sea lice around the farming area is better for other wild fish populations nearby, too.

Solutions like Stingray do have a few drawbacks. Primarily, fish farming businesses might look sheepishly at the up-front costs of deployment. Moreover, it might be best used as a preventative measure, keeping localized lice populations low. The Stingray’s range is limited now by its deployment in the salmon pens, so lice can gather in darker, murkier corners and outside their patrol area.

#10516. MIT scientists build a tiny robot that swims using living muscle cells

A swimming robot does not necessarily need a bulky motor or powerful mechanical parts to move through water. MIT researchers have demonstrated that a single, carefully engineered layer of living muscle cells can generate enough force to propel a tiny robot forward.

The engineers developed an extremely thin swimming robot that uses living muscle tissue to move its fins. Described in a new study published in Advanced Functional Materials, the device has a flexible gel framework roughly the length and width of a stick of gum. The gel forms two fins, each coated with a layer of muscle cells far thinner than a human hair. These cells have been genetically modified to contract when exposed to light.

By directing light onto either fin, the researchers can trigger muscle contractions that make the fin flap and push the robot through water. Adjusting which fin receives light and how frequently it is illuminated allows the team to control both its speed and direction.

In experiments, the remarkably thin robot successfully swam and turned through a simple underwater maze. At maximum speed, it covered approximately four times its body length per minute. Although that is much slower than an Olympic swimmer, who can travel up to 65 body lengths per minute, it is comparable to the swimming pace of a cow shark moving leisurely through the ocean.

"It takes a lot of force to move through water versus air," says study author Ritu Raman, associate professor of mechanical engineering at MIT. "The robot's quite strong, given its size. "

A New Generation of Robots Powered by Living Muscle

The device represents the first demonstration of a very thin, two-dimensional robot that can move from place to place using living muscle tissue.

Most existing biohybrid robots rely on relatively large, three-dimensional structures made from laboratory-grown skeletal muscle. Producing these structures typically requires millions of cells. Raman explains that thinner designs could reduce manufacturing costs while potentially improving movement efficiency.

"Currently, biohybrid robots from our group and others' are built from bulky, 3D chunks of lab-grown skeletal muscle that require millions of cells to fabricate," says Raman, who notes that thinner, less bulky designs such as the team's new bot could be cheaper to build and could move more efficiently. "We believe that biohybrid robots powered by living muscle could one day perform delicate jobs like exploring environments too fragile or unpredictable for conventional hardware, because living tissue is soft, responsive to its surroundings, and can heal itself."

The MIT research team also included first author Maheera Bawa, along with Arielle Berman, Laura Schwendeman, Ferdows Afghah, and Seanbiron Johnson.

From Artificial Muscle to a Swimming Robot

The new swimming robot builds on an earlier achievement by Raman's laboratory. Last year, the researchers created an artificial muscle disk inspired by the human iris, demonstrating how a thin layer of living cells could produce precisely controlled movements.

For that project, the engineers patterned a gel disk with grooves extending outward from its center and forming concentric circles. They then placed living muscle cells on the surface, where the cells grew into a thin layer following the patterned channels.

When illuminated, the muscle tissue contracted in different directions, causing the disk to stretch and compress. Its movements resembled the way the iris of the human eye changes shape as the pupil expands or narrows.

The experiment established that researchers could grow extremely thin muscle layers in intricate arrangements and use light to control their movements along multiple directions.

"People hadn't seen this muscle architecture engineered from scratch before," Raman says. "And the cells were moving in multiple directions. But they only moved about 100 microns. From a robotics perspective, their movements were tiny."

For the latest project, the researchers wanted to dramatically increase the amount of movement and force generated by the muscle cells. Their goal was to produce contractions powerful enough to propel a robot through water.

They determined that achieving this would require a better foundation for the living muscle tissue.

The earlier artificial iris had used fibrin, an exceptionally soft gel, as its underlying structure. However, the team found that fibrin could quickly shrink under the forces exerted by contracting muscle cells, limiting the effectiveness of the resulting movement.

To overcome this problem, the engineers investigated three characteristics of the gel foundation: its chemical composition, its stiffness, and the dimensions and geometry of the grooves used to organize muscle growth.

"For engineering any type of tissue, it's known that these are knobs you can tune," Raman says. "And we wanted to optimize all these parameters to support live muscle cells."

Engineering a Stronger Muscle-Powered Robot

Finding the right supporting material required testing gels with different formulations, stiffness levels, and surface patterns.

The researchers compared several groove shapes, including narrow channels with square profiles and longer channels with curved bottoms. They discovered that muscle cells aligned more effectively inside square-shaped grooves.

This alignment was important because muscle cells that grow in the same direction are more likely to merge into fibers. Those fibers can then form stronger muscle tissue that contracts in a coordinated way. The square grooves therefore provided a more effective structure for producing force.

The team also replaced fibrin with gelatin methacrylate (GelMA), a material commonly used to grow and engineer biological tissue.

By adjusting the formulation of GelMA, the researchers produced supporting gels with varying degrees of stiffness. They then examined how muscle cells developed on each surface and measured their ability to generate force.

Stiffer gels produced the best results. On these firmer surfaces, muscle cells grew in better alignment and generated stronger contractions.

The engineers next investigated how thick the gel should be. They determined that a film measuring approximately half a millimeter offered an effective balance. It was sturdy enough to support a single layer of muscle cells while remaining sufficiently lightweight to prevent the contracting cells from detaching from the surface.

The team further improved muscle performance through a form of exercise. Repeated pulses of light stimulated the cells to contract, strengthening the muscle tissue before it was incorporated into the swimming robot.

With the gel structure optimized and the muscle cells strengthened, the researchers assembled their swimming device.

The finished robot consisted of a thin gel framework divided into two fins. Both sides of the gel were patterned with square-bottomed grooves and covered with living muscle cells. As the cells developed, they fused into organized muscle fibers capable of contracting together.

Each fin could be activated independently using light, giving the researchers a simple way to control the robot's movements.

"You can think of the robot as having two independent muscles," Raman says. "If we shine a light on just one, only that muscle moves. If shining on both, they both flap."

To test the device, the researchers placed it inside a large petri dish filled with water. They then moved a light source by hand, illuminating the fins to guide the robot through a maze positioned inside the dish.

As the muscles contracted, the fins flapped and propelled the robot through the water. By changing the light stimulation, the engineers successfully controlled its movement and helped it navigate the obstacles.

Although the robot demonstrated the ability to swim and steer, its current shape remains relatively simple. The primary objective of the experiment was to establish that an exceptionally thin layer of engineered muscle tissue could generate enough force for swimming.

The researchers now plan to refine the robot's body to improve its speed. Beyond demonstrating a new approach to biohybrid robotics, the technology could eventually be useful for monitoring aquatic environments or operating in locations where conventional rigid machines might cause damage.

"Our next goal is to optimize the body design to enable faster swimming," Raman says. "But even at slow swim speeds, one could imagine a muscle-powered swimmer being used for purposes like environmental monitoring in aquatic environments."

The Office of Naval Research provided partial funding for this research.
