#10510. Cambridge scientists create a $10 chip that could put a medical lab on your wrist

A new optical sensor costing just $10 could bring laboratory-level precision to wearable health monitors, potentially allowing people to track blood glucose without needles. The technology could also transform food testing, industrial quality control, and environmental monitoring.

Researchers at the University of Cambridge and GlitterinTech (a start-up founded by the same research group) have developed a new type of optical spectrometer that combines exceptional accuracy with a remarkably small size and low cost. Measuring just centimeters across, the device could be incorporated into portable electronics and wearable health technology. Its potential applications range from monitoring important health markers in real time to identifying materials and detecting changes in chemical composition.

Optical spectrometers work by analyzing light to reveal information about the substances it interacts with. They are essential tools in medicine, manufacturing, chemical testing, and environmental research. However, making these instruments smaller has traditionally meant compromising their performance. Compact spectrometers often have limited measurement ranges, reduced resolution, or lower accuracy, making them better suited to identifying substances than performing precise measurements.

The researchers believe their new design could overcome these limitations. Known as a convolutional spectrometer, it uses a mathematical principle called the convolution theorem to analyze light in a fundamentally different way. According to the team, this approach delivers performance that surpasses existing types of spectrometers in several key areas, including designs based on light dispersion, Fourier transforms, and computational reconstruction.

A New Way to Analyze Light With Extraordinary Precision

Traditional spectrometers generally separate incoming light into its component wavelengths or use complex calculations to reconstruct its spectrum. The new device takes another approach by performing a mathematical operation called convolution directly on the incoming light.

To accomplish this, the researchers arranged optical components with repeating spectral responses in a sequence. These components can include unbalanced Mach-Zehnder interferometers or micro-ring resonators. By adjusting them in proportion to one another, the system can shift its response to different wavelengths in a controlled, linear fashion. Fast Fourier transforms are then used to accurately recover the light spectrum.

"The key insight was to go back to the mathematics and ask whether there was a fundamentally cleaner way to retrieve spectra," said Dr. Chunhui Yao, a lead author of the study. "By using the convolution theorem directly in the optical domain, we avoid many of the limitations that have held miniaturized spectrometers back. This gives us high precision, strong noise tolerance, and very low computational overhead, all in a compact and low-cost system."

The researchers built the spectrometer using a silicon nitride photonic platform and integrated it with onboard electronics. It operates across a broad range of near-infrared wavelengths (1200-1700 nm) and can collect and process measurements in less than a second.

Another important advantage is its ability to expand its measurement capabilities without major hardware changes. Because the system uses repeating optical patterns, its spectral bandwidth can theoretically be extended almost without limit. Adding more optical components in sequence can also increase its resolution exponentially.

A $10 Sensor That Could Transform Food Testing and Healthcare

To evaluate the device outside purely theoretical measurements, the researchers tested it on a variety of materials and substances. The results demonstrated its potential for applications that normally depend on larger, more expensive laboratory instruments.

When used to identify plastics, pharmaceuticals, coffee, flour, and tea, the spectrometer achieved a 100% classification success rate in the reported tests. It also measured concentrations in water-based and organic solutions with accuracies of approximately 0.01%, exceeding the performance of commercial benchtop spectrometers in those experiments.

The healthcare demonstrations were particularly promising. Under conditions designed to reflect real physiological environments, the device accurately measured several human biomarkers, including skin moisture, blood alcohol, blood lactate, and blood glucose.

The researchers also demonstrated noninvasive glucose tracking over an extended period in one participant. Although further testing would be needed before the technology could become a practical medical product, the finding suggests a possible route toward monitoring blood sugar without repeatedly piercing the skin.

"These biomedical demonstrations are particularly exciting," said Prof. Qixiang Cheng, who led the project. "What makes this work stand out is not just performance in the lab, but technical readiness. Dr. Chunhui Yao's contribution was crucial in translating a mathematical concept into a fully packaged, robust system that operates reliably across temperature extremes and real-world conditions. That combination is what opens the door to practical deployment."

The sensor also demonstrated impressive resistance to temperature changes. It continued operating reliably between -20°C and 80°C, an unusually wide temperature range for a miniature spectrometer. This durability could be especially valuable for wearable devices, factory equipment, and sensors operating outdoors.

Why a Simpler Design Could Be a Major Breakthrough

The new spectrometer's advantages extend beyond its accuracy, size, and price. Its internal design also reduces the amount of computing power needed to analyze incoming light.

Many existing miniature spectrometers depend on complicated calibration procedures and demanding algorithms to reconstruct spectral information. These methods can become unreliable when measurements contain noise, and they often require substantial processing resources.

The Cambridge team's convolution-based approach avoids much of this complexity. Because the measurements follow a linear mathematical relationship, the system can recover spectra rapidly and consistently while using relatively little computational power.

"This is a beautiful example of how elegant engineering can unlock real impact," said Prof. Richard Penty, who contributed to the photonic system design and integration. "The architecture is simple, scalable, and manufacturable, yet it delivers precision that rivals or exceeds much larger instruments. Dr. Chunhui Yao played a central role in bringing together the photonic design, system integration, and experimental validation that made this possible."

Its relatively simple construction could also make the technology easier to manufacture on a large scale, potentially bringing sophisticated optical measurements to devices where conventional spectrometers would be too bulky or expensive.

Bringing Laboratory-Grade Measurements to Everyday Devices

The researchers envision a future in which accurate optical sensing becomes a routine feature of everyday technology rather than something largely confined to specialized laboratories.

In industrial settings, inexpensive spectrometers could be installed throughout manufacturing facilities to continuously check material composition and product quality. Similar sensors could monitor food freshness, identify substances, and analyze environmental conditions on a much larger scale than is currently practical.

Healthcare could see equally significant changes. Compact sensors might eventually enable wearables to measure hydration levels, detect signs of alcohol intoxication, track biomarkers related to physical fitness, or continuously monitor glucose levels for people managing diabetes.

"Our vision is to make spectrometry as ubiquitous as temperature or motion sensing," added Prof. Qixiang Cheng. "This work shows that high-quality spectral information doesn't have to be confined to the laboratory -- it can be embedded directly into the technologies people use every day."

As photonic technology becomes smaller and more advanced, the convolutional spectrometer provides a promising example of how mathematical insights and practical engineering can make sophisticated scientific instruments simpler, cheaper, and more accessible. By combining high precision with a device that costs around $10, the researchers have opened a potential path toward a new generation of smart sensors capable of measuring the hidden chemistry of our bodies and surroundings.

Materials provided by University of Cambridge. Original written by Michael Shuff. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10508. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

This modder thought opening a window was too much work, so he did something cool about it

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

u/Motor_Attention1865 on Reddit didn't like how his PC added extra heat to his room in the winter, so he went the extra mile to dump the heat outside in a fashion similar to mini-split air conditioners. The external cooling system is controlled by an Arduino Mega that the PC communicates with when it needs extra cooling. The system opens a three-way valve to route coolant outside and prevents condensation by keeping the coolant temperature above the dew point.

Our intrepid modder's room has heated floors, and the PC was dumping too much heat under the table where his PC sits, even in 25 °F (-4 °C) weather. He grew tired of manually opening a window to regulate the temperature in his space, so he put in much more effort to create a split cooling system that puts another radiator outside in the below-freezing temperatures, cooling down the PC significantly.

Motor_Attention1865's build itself is impressive enough, with a delidded Core Ultra 7 270K Plus cooled by a Thermal Grizzly 1851 Mycro Pro direct-die water block with Thermal Grizzly Conductonaut Extreme liquid compound. Not only is his RTX 4070 Ti Super also watercooled with a Bykski water block, but the 2x24GB DDR5-8400 G.Skill RAM is also watercooled using Bykski heat spreaders with an IceManCooler two-channel water block. The cooling system within the PC has two radiators: a 120mm in the back and a 360mm in the front, all fed by an EK Kinetic D5 pump-reservoir.

The external cooling system, however, is even more impressive. The 240mm outdoor radiator with two Noctua iPPC-3000 fans isn’t outrageous, but the automated system that makes it useful is what’s interesting. Next to the PC is a box with a Bykski D5 pump and reservoir that pushes water outside, but it doesn’t use the external radiator until the PC needs the extra cooling.

Upon startup, the PC runs a Python script that communicates with an Arduino Mega connected to three temperature probes checking the coolant temperature and three more temperature and humidity sensors that monitor the PC case and the ambient air. The sensors give the Arduino Mega information on where to switch the motorized three-way valve depending on the temperature and humidity.

The problem with extreme cold in any PC is condensation. If coolant gets below the dew point of the environment, condensation forms, potentially shorting out PC components. To prevent this disaster, the Arduino determines the dew point based on the humidity and temperature of the room and in the PC. Motor_Attention1865 set the Arduino to close off the outside radiator at +10 °C above the dew point, or +5 °C during benchmarking, allowing for low temperatures without risking droplets forming in the PC.

Because of the freezing or below-freezing temperatures at which this external loop is meant to operate, Motor_Attention1865 also added pure ethylene glycol to the Aquacomputer Double Protect Ultra Clear coolant in the loop, lowering its freezing point from -18 °C to -28 °C.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Thanks to the supplemental cooling system and delidding, the system achieves temperatures below 50 °C under heavy use. According to Motor_Attention1865, the RTX 4070 Ti sits at 37 °C and the CPU hovers around 45-50 °C while gaming. Temperatures were higher in benchmarks, however — in Cinebench R23, the CPU sat at 52 °C, while in 3DMark Steel Nomad, the GPU was at 48 °C.

Above all, however, Motor_Attention1865 no longer needs to bother himself with opening a window if their room gets too hot with the PC under load. It's the small things.

#10505. Scientists overcome a hidden obstacle to more powerful quantum computers

Silicon spin qubits could play a major role in the future of quantum computing because they can be produced using the same semiconductor manufacturing methods that make today's computer chips. However, researchers have faced a significant challenge. When multiple silicon qubits are controlled at the same time, their operations tend to become less accurate. Building practical, large quantum computers will require scientists to control many qubits simultaneously while keeping errors to a minimum.

Researchers at the RIKEN Center for Emergent Matter Science and their collaborators have now made important progress toward solving this problem. In a study published in Nature Physics, the team demonstrated highly accurate control of several silicon spin qubits operating simultaneously. Their experiments used an array of five qubits connected to a shared microwave control line. By identifying a major cause of errors during simultaneous operation, the scientists developed a technique to reduce those errors and maintain precision.

Leon Camenzind, a RIKEN research scientist and co-first author who supervised the experiments, explains the significance of the results: "This shows that high-fidelity control can be maintained even when several qubits are operated at the same time and represents an important step toward scaling up silicon-based quantum processors."

Scientists Identify a Hidden Source of Quantum Computing Errors

Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum computers use quantum bits, or qubits, that can exist in combinations of both states. Silicon spin qubits store this information using the spin of individual electrons confined within silicon. Performing reliable quantum calculations requires extremely precise control over these electron spins.

Microwave signals provide that control, but operating multiple qubits simultaneously can create unwanted effects that interfere with their accuracy. The researchers traced much of this problem to a phenomenon called the AC Stark shift.

This effect occurs when microwave control signals alter a qubit's effective energy levels, producing changes in the phase of its quantum state. Those phase shifts can disrupt the intended operation of individual qubits, particularly when several are being controlled at once.

To counteract the problem, the team created a calibration method that compensates for these unwanted shifts. Rather than separately calibrating every possible combination of qubits, the technique relies on calibrations performed between pairs of qubits. This makes the approach more practical for systems containing larger numbers of quantum bits.

"Rather than requiring an entirely separate calibration procedure for every possible combination of qubits, our method uses calibrations between pairs of qubits to compensate for the unwanted phase shifts," explains Yi-Hsien Wu, the first author who carried out the experiments as part of his PhD research.

Quantum Qubits Achieve Accuracy Above 99.99%

The researchers combined their calibration technique with an improved device design and carefully adjusted microwave control pulses. Their results showed that exceptionally high accuracy could be maintained even when multiple qubits were operating simultaneously.

When the five qubits were tested individually, each achieved single-qubit gate fidelity well above 99.99%, with the highest approaching 99.999%. Fidelity describes how closely a quantum operation matches its intended result.

The team then tested how well the system performed when several qubits were active together. With as many as three qubits operating simultaneously, fidelity remained above 99.99%. Even when all five qubits were controlled at once, accuracy stayed at approximately 99.9%, approaching the levels needed for practical fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Although the calibration method greatly reduced errors, it did not eliminate them entirely. The researchers found that the remaining loss of accuracy was linked to drive-induced decoherence, an effect caused by the greater microwave power required to operate multiple qubits simultaneously.

Unlike the unwanted phase shifts corrected by the new technique, decoherence involves the loss of the delicate quantum coherence needed for reliable operations.

According to Camenzind, "This is a limitation, but further improvements to device design could help mitigate this source of error."

A New Path Toward Larger Quantum Processors

The findings show that controlling several silicon spin qubits at the same time does not necessarily require a major sacrifice in accuracy. By correcting a key source of errors, the researchers have demonstrated a promising approach for operating more qubits through shared microwave control lines.

This is especially important for the development of large quantum processors. Giving every qubit its own independent control system would make increasingly complex devices difficult to manage. Shared control lines could offer a more practical way to expand these systems while maintaining the precision needed for quantum calculations.

"By addressing a key source of control errors through pairwise calibration," says Camenzind. "Our study supports the use of shared control lines as a practical approach to scaling up quantum processors. Combined with silicon's compatibility with established semiconductor manufacturing, we believe that this advance could help pave the way toward larger quantum processors capable of performing increasingly complex computations."

The broader objective is to develop quantum computers that can reliably perform calculations despite the errors and disturbances that affect quantum systems. Such machines, known as fault-tolerant quantum computers, will require precise, dependable control over large numbers of qubits.

"Our long-term goal is to build a fault-tolerant quantum computer based on silicon spin qubits, and reliable parallel control is an essential step toward that goal," adds Seigo Tarucha, project manager of a silicon quantum computing project under JST's Moonshot Goal 6.

Materials provided by RIKEN. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10499. Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

Materials provided by Norwegian University of Science and Technology. Original written by Steinar Brandslet. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10498. Top 4 MCP Servers for Marketers: Features, Use Cases, and Limitations

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You no longer need to build a dashboard every time you want to investigate a drop in traffic or compare campaign results. With servers built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), you can ask an AI assistant a question and let it retrieve the relevant data directly from your marketing tools.

But connecting an assistant to Google Analytics is not the same as connecting it to HubSpot or Ahrefs. Each server exposes different data, capabilities and levels of access. Some are built for quick analysis inside one platform, while others support reporting across several channels. This guide compares four servers, what they can do, how they are set up and where their limits matter. Details such as plans and tool lists change often, so confirm them against each vendor's documentation before you commit.

The summary below reflects each vendor's documentation at the time of writing.

Google publishes an official, open-source Google Analytics MCP server for GA4. It connects AI assistants to the Admin and Data APIs, so it can list accounts and properties, run standard, realtime, funnel and conversion reports, and inspect custom dimensions and metrics. That makes it useful for investigating traffic changes, landing page performance and engagement trends.

Example prompt: "Which landing pages lost the most engaged sessions this month compared with last month?" The assistant can pull the relevant reports and help you spot changes, and you can then check whether the decline comes from specific channels, countries or devices.

Setup: The server runs locally. Google's documentation lists Python 3.10 or later, pipx or uvx, a Google Cloud project with the Analytics Admin and Data APIs enabled, application default credentials with the read-only Analytics scope, and at least Viewer access on the property you want to query.

Access: Google describes the server as available for read requests only, so it cannot change your Analytics configuration.

Limitations: It covers GA4 only, so it cannot combine website metrics with CRM records or advertising costs on its own. Running it locally also means you maintain the configuration yourself.

Best for: Web analysts and technical marketers who regularly investigate GA4 performance.

Marketing performance usually spans several platforms. Google Ads generates clicks, GA4 tracks site activity and a CRM records leads. Seeing the whole picture means bringing those metrics together.

Coupler is a no-code data integration platform that offers MCP access to prepared business data. It lists more than 400 supported data sources, including Google Ads, Facebook Ads, GA4, HubSpot, LinkedIn Ads and Shopify, and lets teams combine and transform data before an assistant queries it.

Example prompt: "What was our cost per lead across Google Ads, Meta Ads and LinkedIn last quarter?" For paid search specifically, Coulper describes a Google Ads MCP server that lets you ask about campaigns and compare them with other connected channels.

Setup: Coupler is hosted, so there is nothing to install locally. You connect your platforms, configure data flows, choose refresh schedules and connect a compatible AI assistant. According to the vendor, queries run against the prepared datasets, which is useful for recurring reports because teams can reuse refreshed data instead of preparing exports by hand.

Best for: Agencies and teams that need consistent reporting across advertising, website analytics, ecommerce and CRM platforms.

HubSpot's remote MCP server became generally available on April 13, 2026. It connects compatible AI clients to CRM data such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities, and can also read marketing objects such as campaigns, landing pages and blog posts. That helps marketers investigate lead quality, pipeline performance and lifecycle trends without reviewing records one by one.

Example prompt: "Which deals created last quarter originated from webinar leads, and how many closed?" The assistant can retrieve the records and examine conversion patterns, provided the attribution data exists.

Setup: HubSpot hosts the server. In your account you create an MCP auth app under Development, then connect an MCP client that supports OAuth 2.1 with PKCE to the HubSpot endpoint. See HubSpot's remote MCP server documentation for current steps.

Access: Unlike a purely analytical server, HubSpot supports selected create and update actions on records such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities. Other objects, including marketing content, are read-only.

Limitations: Available operations differ by object type, and incomplete lead sources or inconsistent lifecycle stages will weaken any analysis.

Best for: B2B marketers and revenue operations teams that manage customers and pipeline in HubSpot.

Ahrefs offers a hosted MCP server that brings its SEO data into compatible assistants. It supports conversational research into keywords, backlinks, competing pages and organic visibility, so you can follow up with questions instead of exporting report after report.

Example prompt: "Which websites link to both of our main competitors but don't link to us?" The assistant can retrieve backlink data and help you identify outreach targets, which you can then narrow by Domain Rating or organic traffic.

Setup: According to the Ahrefs MCP documentation, the remote server is available on paid plans starting from Lite, and you authorize it through your AI client. Ahrefs also publishes a getting started guide.

Limitations: Calls made through MCP use your monthly API units in the same way as direct API usage, and rows per request depend on your plan. Ahrefs documents a default cap of 200,000 units per month for keys created through OAuth, which workspace admins can change. The data is also SEO-focused rather than first-party conversion analytics.

Best for: SEO specialists, content marketers and link builders who already use Ahrefs.

Yes, but start with the connections your team actually needs. Each additional server adds tools the assistant has to choose between, which increases complexity. Combining Ahrefs and HubSpot, for example, supports separate SEO and CRM investigations, but connecting several servers does not automatically create a reliable cross-platform reporting system.

For recurring reports, prepare consistent datasets and validate the first results against your existing dashboards. That catches errors caused by mismatched metrics or attribution rules.

Are MCP servers safe for marketing data? Security depends on the provider, the authentication method and the permissions you grant. Use trusted servers, review access scopes, and enable write operations only when necessary.

Do I need coding skills to use MCP servers? Not necessarily. Hosted services usually need less configuration, while local servers such as Google's need extra setup.

Which AI clients support MCP? Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor and other tools support MCP connections, though functionality and setup differ.

Do MCP servers cost extra? It depends on the provider. Some require paid plans, and others consume API credits or have usage limits.

Pick the server that matches the question you ask most often: Google Analytics MCP for GA4 behavior, Coupler for cross-channel reporting, HubSpot for CRM and lifecycle analysis, and Ahrefs for SEO research. Start with read-only access, check usage limits and permissions, and validate early answers against the reports you already trust.

#10497. Payday Aces High review: This year’s must-play multiplayer VR game

Join up with three other heisters and get revenge on the billionaire who sent you to jail in the first place in Payday Aces High, now available on Meta Quest, SteamVR, and Steam Frame.

Payday Aces High is the return to the series’ roots I had been hoping for. Payday 3 wasn’t received particularly well, and while the team behind it has turned things around in the past few years, it’s pretty clear that Payday 2 is still everyone’s favorite entry. But that stops for me now as we enter a new chapter in the series, with four new characters, a new “villain,” and made-for-VR mechanics that shine.

I’ve been playing Payday Aces High throughout the alpha and beta stages and got access to the final review build a week ago. Since then, my son and I have robbed banks, stolen from museums, hoisted a fragile quantum computer out of an icy compound, and generally made life a living hell for Warren Jupiter, the man who made billions framing the characters you play as in Payday Aces High.

If you’re familiar with Payday games, this one will mostly feel like a return to the original game with new mechanics in tow. Some of the fan-favorite mechanics from Payday 2 (and even 3) aren’t here — you can’t take hostages, for example — but other mechanics, like gun upgrades and perks, will keep you coming back over and over again.

While you can choose from the four main Aces characters — the Ace of Hearts, Clubs, Diamonds, and Spades — the character itself is just a visual change rather than a stats-based one. You customize your own stats throughout the adventure as you unlock points and earn money. You’ll also collect the infamous masks that each character dons, including DLC sets (and missions) scheduled for the next few months.

Payday Aces High begins with six unlockable missions, while a seventh free one (named Coup de Grace) is landing on December 14. Developer Fast Travel Games is a VR veteran and is hosting community challenges throughout the next few months, unlocking special loot when completed. The game has full crossplay support, so it should be very easy to find and invite friends, no matter which platform they prefer.

I played the standalone Quest 3 version, which looks quite nice visually and sports great performance. VR-specific mechanics are excellent, including on-body inventory, fun skill check-style minigames to keep equipment working, physical interaction with locks and other environmental details, and lots of shooting with physics-based gun mechanics. Hacking a computer while shooting in a completely different direction is something that only makes sense in VR, and I love leaning into those types of mechanics.

The biggest miss of Payday Aces High is not providing enough bespoke VR mechanics. You can’t grab characters, can’t break glass with a melee attack, and the stealth leaves a bit to be desired. I would love to be able to toss an object across the room to distract guards for a moment, for instance, as it would fit the medium very well.

But outside of a few immersion-breaking decisions like this, the action is incredibly fun, challenging, and, maybe most important of all, rewarding. My son and I have completed several missions many times over in hopes of perfecting the formula, and the increasing complexity and difficulty of each has proven to be an enjoyable challenge. Payday Aces High is my multiplayer VR game of the year, hands down, not just because I already loved the series, but because I find co-op multiplayer to be the most fun thing to do in a VR headset.

#10492. A tiny supercurrent could unlock quantum information without destroying it

One of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing is surprisingly simple to describe: how do you read information without changing or destroying it? Unlike the bits used in ordinary computers, quantum bits, or qubits, are extraordinarily sensitive to measurement. Trying to determine their state can cause that state to collapse, potentially erasing the very information scientists want to retrieve.

Researchers have now proposed a way to overcome much of this difficulty by using an unusual electrical signal produced by the qubit itself. Their approach focuses on a special type known as an "Andreev qubit," which can generate an oscillating electric supercurrent through its own quantum behavior. By monitoring this current, scientists may be able to determine the qubit's state without directly measuring it or significantly disturbing the information it contains.

The research, supported by FAPESP, was published in Physical Review B Letters. It was led by José Carlos Egues, a full professor at the São Carlos Institute of Physics at the University of São Paulo (IFSC-USP) in Brazil, and Yugui Yao of the School of Physics at the Beijing Institute of Technology in China. If experimentally confirmed, their method could simplify quantum computing by removing the need for extra qubits used in certain measurement techniques.

Why Measuring Quantum Information Is So Difficult

Conventional computers store information in bits that have one of two values, 0 or 1. These values can be checked repeatedly without changing the information. Quantum computers work differently because their qubits can exist in superpositions, meaning they can combine the states 0 and 1 in ways that are not possible with conventional bits.

This unusual ability is central to the potential power of quantum computing. However, it also creates a major vulnerability. Quantum states can be disrupted by interactions with their surroundings, including the instruments scientists use to examine them.

"In the conventional formulation of quantum mechanics, a projective measurement causes the system to assume one of the states associated with the measured quantity. Thus, if a qubit has been prepared in a certain superposition of 0 and 1, its direct measurement tends to destroy that superposition and, consequently, the information it contained," says Egues.

To minimize this problem, physicists developed techniques known as "quantum nondemolition measurements," or QND. Many of these approaches use an additional quantum system called an ancilla. Rather than measuring the original qubit directly, researchers transfer information to the ancilla and measure that auxiliary system instead.

The term "ancilla" comes from a Latin word meaning "slave" or "servant." In modern scientific usage, it describes something that performs a supporting function. Here, the ancilla acts as an intermediary, allowing researchers to obtain information while preserving the original qubit as much as possible.

Although effective, these procedures come with practical disadvantages. Scientists must prepare the auxiliary qubits, connect them to the qubits being studied, transfer information between them, and perform repeated measurements. These additional steps require time and equipment, and the complexity becomes increasingly significant as the number of qubits grows.

"Our strategy is different. Instead of transferring the information to an auxiliary qubit, we seek to obtain it from a signal produced by the qubit under study itself," says the researcher.

A Special Quantum Bit That Produces Its Own Signal

The proposed technique relies on the unusual properties of an Andreev qubit. Its name honors Soviet physicist Alexander Andreev (1939-2023), who identified a distinctive quantum process in 1964 that occurs where normal materials meet superconductors.

Superconductors are materials that can conduct electricity without electrical resistance under appropriate conditions. At the boundaries involving these materials, special quantum states known as "Andreev states" can form. In the system studied by the researchers, these states are confined to an extremely small region between two superconducting materials.

These quantum states can represent the basic values 0 and 1, along with superpositions of those values, making them suitable for storing quantum information.

"In the system under consideration, this region is a quantum dot - that is, a nanoscale structure in which electrons are spatially confined - formed in an indium arsenide [InAs] nanowire connected to superconducting materials on both sides. It's in this environment that the states characterizing the Andreev qubit are formed," Egues explains.

The key to the new approach is an electrical phenomenon called a "supercurrent." Unlike the current flowing through ordinary electrical conductors, a supercurrent moves without resistance.

The researchers found that when the Andreev qubit exists in a superposition, the supercurrent develops a component that oscillates over time. These fluctuations arise from quantum interference between the different states making up the superposition.

Crucially, the oscillations are not random. Their behavior contains information about the qubit's underlying quantum state, potentially allowing scientists to determine what that state is without directly measuring the qubit.

"It's precisely that property that we use to read the qubit. It's that supercurrent that lies at the heart of our proposal," the researcher points out.

How a Tiny Electrical Current Reveals Quantum Information

To understand how the technique works, it helps to consider how physicists describe a qubit's state mathematically.

One common representation is the "Bloch sphere," a geometric model in which every possible pure state of a single qubit corresponds to a point on the surface of a sphere. Two angles determine the location of that point.

The first angle describes how much each of the two basic states contributes to the superposition. The second describes their relative phase, which specifies how the quantum components are aligned with one another. Together, these two measurements provide the information needed to identify the qubit's state.

According to the new research, both pieces of information can be extracted from the supercurrent. Its steady component and the size of its oscillations reveal the relative contributions of the two basic states. Meanwhile, the timing of the oscillations provides information about their phase relationship.

"Our study showed that this information is encoded in the supercurrent itself. The supercurrent has a constant component and a fluctuating component. The value of the constant component and the amplitude of the oscillation reveal the proportion of each state (0 and 1) in the superposition, and the moment when that oscillation begins reveals the phase relationship between them. Thus, by measuring how the current varies over time, it's possible to reconstruct the initial state of the qubit," Egues explains.

This means the electrical current effectively carries a signature of the quantum information stored in the system. By observing how that signature changes over time, researchers could reconstruct the qubit's original state.

A Supercurrent That Oscillates Without Voltage

Another surprising feature of the proposed system is that the electrical current can oscillate without an externally applied voltage.

Ordinarily, producing an oscillating supercurrent in this type of superconducting system requires a voltage. This familiar phenomenon is called the "AC Josephson effect."

The researchers predict something different in their Andreev qubit. Here, the current's oscillations arise naturally from the qubit's own quantum evolution. No outside voltage is required to generate them.

"Our system generates a supercurrent that varies over time like an alternating current but without voltage. It's the very evolution of the qubit's state that produces that oscillation," says Egues.

This behavior is important because it transforms quantum activity occurring on an extraordinarily small scale into a detectable electrical signal. The current provides a connection between the microscopic world of quantum states and the larger physical systems scientists can measure.

That connection is what makes the proposed reading technique possible.

Detecting Quantum States With an Extremely Sensitive Sensor

The researchers envision placing the Andreev qubit inside a closed superconducting circuit, allowing its supercurrent to circulate continuously.

Next to the circuit would sit a SQUID (superconducting quantum interference device), a highly sensitive instrument capable of detecting extremely weak magnetic fields.

Whenever an electric current flows, it produces a magnetic field. Because the supercurrent in this system oscillates, the magnetic field it generates also changes over time.

The nearby SQUID could detect these tiny magnetic variations and use them to determine the behavior of the circulating current. Since the current contains information about the qubit's quantum state, the researchers could then reconstruct that state without measuring the qubit directly.

Egues compares the arrangement to two compasses that interact through a magnetic field.

"We can think of the qubit as one compass and the SQUID as another compass. The current in the circuit produces a magnetic field, and the SQUID 'senses' that field. Since the coupling between the two is very weak, the effect of the measuring instrument on the qubit tends to be very small."

The weak interaction between the sensor and the qubit is especially important. It offers a way to gather information while minimizing the disturbance that normally accompanies quantum measurements.

Reading Quantum Information With Almost No Disturbance

In quantum physics, observing a system is not necessarily a harmless process. A measuring instrument interacts with whatever it is attempting to measure, and that interaction can change the system's behavior.

This fundamental difficulty puzzled the earliest researchers studying quantum mechanics. It remains an unavoidable feature of quantum measurements today.

The proposed technique does not eliminate this effect entirely. The SQUID still interacts with the circuit, creating a small amount of feedback that can influence the qubit. However, the team's simulations suggest that this disturbance can be reduced to a level so small that it becomes effectively negligible.

"Our method doesn't eliminate the fundamental problem of quantum measurement. But it can approach the limit of a non-destructive readout and, at the same time, avoid the use of auxiliary qubits - the ancillas - employed in other non-destructive quantum measurement protocols," Egues notes.

The approach could also remove another complication found in some existing techniques. Because researchers would not need to restore the qubit to its original state after every measurement, they could make repeated observations as the state changes.

This would allow scientists to follow the qubit's quantum evolution over time, rather than obtaining information only through separate measurement and reset cycles.

Simulations Achieve Up to 99.85% Accuracy

To investigate whether the method could work outside ideal theoretical conditions, the researchers performed computer simulations designed to account for imperfections that would arise in real experiments.

These calculations included measurement noise, as well as the small amount of feedback introduced by the detection coil. The goal was to determine whether the qubit's state could still be reconstructed accurately despite these disturbances.

The results were encouraging. Depending on the number of measurements used, the simulations produced accuracies of 99.52%, 99.81%, and 99.85%.

According to the researchers, these values fall within the standard thresholds used in quantum error correction schemes, which are designed to manage errors in quantum information processing.

The findings suggest that the method could provide highly accurate quantum state reconstruction without the additional complexity of auxiliary qubits.

However, the technique has not yet been demonstrated experimentally. The results come entirely from theoretical analysis and computer simulations, so researchers still need to establish whether a physical device can achieve comparable performance.

Could This Make Quantum Computers Simpler?

Despite the lack of experimental confirmation, the researchers emphasize that their proposal does not depend on technologies that have yet to be invented.

The calculations use realistic properties drawn from existing quantum systems, and SQUID sensors are already established tools for detecting exceptionally weak magnetic fields and electric currents.

What remains to be built is the specific arrangement of components required to implement the proposed measurement technique.

"All the numbers we used are realistic. In principle, this can be achieved. In fact, we used parameters from existing systems in our calculations. What doesn't yet exist is that geometry, that setup that we proposed," he argues.

This makes the approach different from theoretical concepts that cannot be realized until scientists develop entirely new materials or manufacturing techniques. In principle, the necessary components already exist, although assembling them into the proposed configuration and demonstrating the predicted behavior remain important challenges.

The research offers two potentially significant advances.

From the standpoint of fundamental physics, it predicts that a qubit can produce an oscillating supercurrent resembling the AC Josephson effect, but without requiring an external voltage. Instead, the oscillations emerge from the qubit's own coherent quantum evolution.

From a technological perspective, the study shows how those oscillations could serve as a measurable signature of the qubit's internal state. By analyzing the electrical current, researchers could recover quantum information while causing very little disturbance to the system.

If future experiments confirm these predictions, the method could make reading Andreev qubits substantially simpler. Eliminating auxiliary qubits and repeated resets could reduce the equipment and procedures needed to retrieve quantum information.

More fundamentally, the work points toward an intriguing possibility: information hidden in the microscopic quantum world may be accessible through an electrical current produced by the quantum system itself, offering a promising new way to observe delicate quantum states without destroying them.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine sparked a new era of drone warfare. Russian invaders first used drones with fiber-optic tethers to combat electronic warfare on the front lines, but both sides now use these jam-proof drones. Against that backdrop, the AI data center buildout is making it harder for Ukraine to buy the fiber-optic spools that it needs to continue scaling its drone manufacturing capabilities. A research and development representative for BlueBird Tech, a Ukrainian defense firm specializing in drones, told Ars Technica in an interview that prices of fiber optic cables have risen around six to 10 times in the past couple of years as AI data center demand soaks up more and more of the supply of this resource.

According to that interview, It’s estimated that Ukraine and Russia are producing around 50,000 fiber optic drones monthly, with each drone capable of ranges of up to 62 miles. But Ukraine has only been able to acquire 72 miles of fiber optic cable from Chinese suppliers during August 2025, whereas Russia imported a whopping 328,000 miles during that same month. It was estimated that Russia acquired 37 million miles of fiber during the entirety of 2025, accounting for over 10% of the global production.

What makes matters worse for Ukrainian defenders is that there is an estimated 111-million-mile shortfall in fiber optic cable supply across the world, adding to the various shortages that the data center industry has faced in recent years. Aside from the material shortfall, Ukraine faces trade barriers in acquiring more fiber. The BlueBird representative claimed that some Chinese companies have outright refused to sell Ukraine fiber optic cables.

China is the primary supplier of this material, so it’s easier for Russia to obtain them through its massive land border with the country. While Ukraine could purchase fiber optic cables from suppliers located in other countries, that's not much of an alternative. The BlueBird rep notes: "there can be intermediaries in other countries, and prices can differ depending on the country and the purchasing route. But in the end, China remains the main source of the fiber optic material we are talking about.”

There has also been talk of producing fiber optic cables domestically in Ukraine, but such manufacturing requires specially trained personnel who must practice on the already limited supply of fiber optic cables. Even if manufacturers automate production, they still require experienced personnel who can oversee the line and ensure the quality of the final product.

The data center build out has been causing all kinds of shortages. We’ve seen supply shortfalls in GPUs, memory chips, and even PCBs, but there’s also shortages in other seemingly unrelated industries like power infrastructure, optical transceivers, and even in critical skilled labor like electricians. This overwhelming demand has started affecting other industries not related to computing, like cars, home construction, and now, fiber optic attack drones.

#10486. How Developers Can Improve Their Learning and Productivity With AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how developers learn, write code, and manage their daily work. Tasks that once required hours of searching, testing, and manual effort can now be completed with help from AI. Developers can use it to understand complex programming concepts, find coding errors, learn new technologies, and complete repetitive tasks.

However, AI is not only useful for writing code faster. It can also help developers improve their technical knowledge, solve problems, and build better learning habits. When used correctly, it can become a useful assistant throughout the software development process.

The key is to understand where AI can help and where human skills are still needed. Here are several practical ways developers can use AI to improve their learning and productivity.

Learning a new programming language or understanding a difficult concept can take time. Developers often need to read long explanations, watch tutorials, and test different examples before they understand how something works.

AI can make this process easier by explaining technical concepts in simple language. Instead of searching through several websites, developers can ask direct questions and receive explanations based on their current knowledge.

For example, a developer learning JavaScript can ask AI to explain promises, callbacks, or asynchronous functions. It can break these topics into smaller parts and provide simple code examples to make them easier to understand.

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain the same concept in different ways. If one explanation is difficult to understand, a different example or comparison may make the idea clearer.

AI explanations are not always accurate, especially when dealing with complex programming concepts or unfamiliar technologies. Developers should verify important information before using it in their projects. Hallucination detection can help identify fabricated facts, incorrect technical explanations, and misleading information in AI-generated responses, making it easier to check the accuracy of what they learn.

This helps developers spend less time struggling with unfamiliar topics and more time building their technical knowledge.

Software development changes regularly. New frameworks, libraries, programming languages, and development methods continue to appear. Developers need to keep learning if they want to stay up to date.

AI can support this process by helping developers create learning plans based on their goals and current skills.

For example, a developer who wants to learn Python can ask AI to create a beginner-friendly learning plan. The plan can include basic syntax, variables, loops, functions, object-oriented programming, and small projects.

AI can also recommend practical exercises for each topic. Instead of only reading about a programming concept, developers can write code and test their understanding.

Here are some ways to use AI when learning new technologies:

However, developers should not depend entirely on AI-generated explanations. They should also read official documentation and test examples themselves. This helps them understand how technologies work in real development environments.

AI can help developers improve their coding skills by providing examples, explaining different solutions, and identifying areas that need improvement.

For example, a developer may write a function that works but contains unnecessary steps. They can ask AI to review the code and suggest ways to make it easier to read, maintain, or understand.

AI can also explain why one solution may be better than another. This allows developers to learn different approaches instead of always following the first solution they find.

Some practical ways to use AI for coding improvement include:

Developers can also ask AI to create small coding challenges based on their skill level. For example, someone learning Python can request exercises about lists, dictionaries, loops, and functions.

The important thing is to understand the code instead of copying it without review. Developers who examine the logic behind each solution can improve their programming knowledge over time.

Developers learn new information from many sources, including online courses, technical blogs, documentation, tutorials, and coding projects. Without a proper system, it can become difficult to find useful information when they need it again.

AI can help developers organize this knowledge and make learning resources easier to review.

For example, developers can use AI to summarize long technical explanations, create revision questions, and organize important programming concepts into smaller topics. They can also turn their learning notes into structured study guides that make it easier to revisit important information.

A well-organized knowledge system can be useful when learning a new framework or preparing for technical interviews. Instead of searching through old browser tabs or reading the same documentation repeatedly, developers can return to their saved explanations and notes.

Here are some useful ways to organize technical knowledge:

Technical documentation can contain detailed explanations that are difficult to remember. Developers can use AI to summarize important sections and turn them into short notes.

For example, when learning a new API, they can save information about its main functions, parameters, common errors, and practical examples.

Reading information does not always mean remembering it. Developers can ask AI to create questions from their notes so they can test their understanding.

For example, after learning about database indexing, they can create questions about how indexes work, when to use them, and how they affect query performance.

Developers should also save important information about their projects. This may include coding decisions, API details, database structures, and solutions to common problems.

Keeping these details organized makes it easier to return to a project after a break or help another developer understand the work.

Developers spend a large part of their working time on tasks that do not always require complex problem-solving. These may include writing basic code structures, creating test cases, preparing documentation, and handling repeated coding patterns.

AI can help reduce this manual effort by generating suggestions and automating parts of these tasks.

For example, when starting a new project, developers often need to create similar code structures, such as basic functions, configuration files, and reusable components. An AI coding assistant can generate an initial version that developers can review and adjust.

Automating these activities gives developers more time to focus on important parts of their projects, such as system design, complex features, and problem-solving.

However, generated code should always be checked before it is added to a project. Developers need to make sure it follows project requirements, coding standards, and security rules.

Finding and fixing bugs is an important part of software development. Even experienced developers can spend hours trying to understand why a program is not working as expected.

AI can help make debugging easier by explaining error messages, identifying possible causes, and suggesting solutions.

For example, when a developer encounters a Python error, they can share the relevant code and error message with an AI assistant. The assistant can explain what the error means and suggest possible ways to fix it.

AI can also help developers review code before submitting it. It may identify common issues such as:

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain why a particular bug occurs instead of only requesting a corrected version of the code.

This helps developers understand the problem and avoid making the same mistake in future projects.

Still, AI suggestions are not always correct. Developers should test proposed fixes, review the surrounding code, and confirm that the solution does not introduce new problems.

Managing several development tasks at once can be difficult. Developers may need to work on new features, fix bugs, review code, attend meetings, and meet project deadlines.

AI can help organize these responsibilities by breaking large tasks into smaller and more manageable steps.

For example, if a developer needs to build a user authentication system, they can ask AI to divide the work into separate tasks. These may include creating registration forms, setting up database tables, implementing login logic, adding password protection, and testing the system.

This makes it easier to understand the work involved and decide what needs attention first.

AI can also help explain technical requirements before development begins. This may reduce confusion and help developers identify missing details early.

However, AI-generated plans should be treated as starting points. Developers still need to consider their actual deadlines, technical limitations, and project requirements before following them.

Although AI offers many benefits, depending on it for every programming task can affect a developer's ability to solve problems independently.

If developers always ask AI to write code, fix errors, and explain every concept, they may miss opportunities to develop their own technical skills.

A 2026 Anthropic study found that participants using AI scored 17 percentage points lower on a programming knowledge quiz than participants who worked without AI in the experiment. This highlights why understanding the work still matters when using coding assistants.

Developers can avoid excessive dependence on AI by following a few simple practices:

When facing a coding problem, spend some time understanding it and trying possible solutions. If you get stuck, use AI to explore the issue rather than asking it to complete everything.

Never add AI-generated code to a project without understanding what it does. Review the logic, check the functions, and test the output.

AI can sometimes provide outdated information, incorrect code, or explanations that do not fit a particular project. Check important details against official documentation and reliable technical resources.

Regular coding practice without assistance helps developers maintain their problem-solving skills. It also shows whether they truly understand a programming concept.

The best approach is to use AI as a helpful assistant while keeping independent thinking and technical judgment at the center of development work.

Using AI occasionally can be helpful, but developers can get more value from it by making it part of a regular learning and working routine.

A simple daily routine can help developers balance their project responsibilities with continuous learning.

For example, developers can follow this approach:

Start with a learning goal: Spend some time understanding a new programming concept, framework, or technology. Use AI to explain difficult topics and provide practical examples.

Plan the day's development tasks: Ask AI to help organize important tasks and break complex work into smaller steps.

Use AI during development: Get help with unfamiliar code, debugging, test cases, and repetitive tasks. Review all suggestions before using them.

Save useful information: Keep important explanations, coding examples, and solutions in an organized knowledge base for future reference.

Review what you learned: At the end of the day, revisit important concepts and test your understanding through small questions or coding exercises.

Developers do not need to spend hours following this routine. Even a short period of focused learning each day can help them build knowledge while managing their regular development work.

AI is becoming a useful part of modern software development. It can help developers understand difficult concepts, learn new technologies, improve code quality, debug problems, and complete repetitive tasks more efficiently.

However, better productivity does not simply mean writing more code in less time. Developers also need to understand what they are building, improve their problem-solving skills, and keep learning as technology changes.

The most effective approach is to use AI for support rather than letting it make every decision. By combining AI assistance with regular practice, independent thinking, and organized learning, developers can improve both their technical skills and everyday productivity.

#10482. I bought the knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands, and it’s crazy how good they are [Gallery]

I love swapping out the bands on my Pixel Watch 5 and Fitbit Air, but Google’s official options are not cheap. It doesn’t take long to find a plethora of knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands around the web and, as it turns out, they’re actually really good.

Over the past few generations, I’ve collected a ton of Pixel Watch bands and, at first, that was almost entirely first-party bands. For the first couple of years of Pixel Watch, third-party bands were either non-existent or of terrible quality, but they’ve gotten a lot better.

Last year, I showcased some knock-off Active Sport bands that, for $8, very effectively filled the gap for me while Google’s lineup was lacking some colors I wanted.

Since then, I’ve tried others. The most recent batch is a 6-pack of Pixel Watch bands that are copies of Google’s standard “Active” band. They’re made from silicone, not the fluorelastomer from past generations, nor the “Hydrogenated Nitrile Butadiene Rubber (HNBR)” used on Pixel Watch 5. But, in action, it gets the job done.

The bands sit somewhere between Google’s small and large sizes and slot into the Pixel Watch 5’s mechanism perfectly. If you handed this to me and said it was an official band from Google, I’d probably believe you.

To get this level of quality at such a low price is wild, all while getting colors that, again, Google doesn’t make. I found this while just wanting a purple band, but the price tag pushed me to just buy a whole pack, and the striking deep red included sneakily became one of my favorites.

Pixel Watch isn’t alone here – third-party knock-off bands for Fitbit Air are very good, as my colleage Damien Wilde has pointed out repeatedly.

I originally bought the Stephen Curry edition Fitbit Air and, long story short, I don’t have that official band anymore. But the $9 (currently even cheaper!) knock-off I bought on Amazon is quite literally identical in quality, feel, and even the details. The only thing it’s missing is the Stephen Curry badge (which, frankly, I don’t miss anyway), but it still has that little piece of added material where the wordmark would have been.

The same goes for the matching band I picked up for my Pixel Watch 5.

Google doesn’t sell this band on its own, which is annoying, but the knock-off I bought is basically identical to what I felt during our hands-on with the official one back in August, though this one doesn’t have the space for the Stephen Curry badge the real one does. The lugs connecting to the watch are also different and not nearly as stylish, but I’m absolutely not complaining here.

Is it possible that Google’s supply chain is producing these near-identical versions? Given the quality at play, I really wouldn’t be surprised! That said, a lot of these are simple copies. It’s not all that hard to spin up generic versions of products like these.

Most third party cheap Fitbit Air bands I've tried range from "decent" to "very good".

Most third party cheap and expensive Pixel Watch bands I've tried range from "very, very bad" to "almost decent".

The major issue with PW 3rd party bands isn't the band itself. It's the connecting lugs.

Not how they look, but how they're manufactured. High tolerances, rattling, too lose or too tight connections.

And that applies also to certified "made for Google" bands (i.e. the Spigen ones).

I've given up. I'm going to buy the official Pixel Watch leather band and take the official lugs out of it, so I can use with any 22mm strap.

I’ll still happily pick up some of Google’s official bands because it’s hard to beat some of those color options but, for the most part, it’s getting hard to go wrong with the knock-off Pixel Watch bands, and even more so with the Fitbit Air. The simpler tracker gets even better third-party options seeing as Google made the mounting mechanism free for everyone.

Whatever the case, third-party / knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands are in a really good place right now, and that’s refreshing after a few years of using this lineup every day.

Starbase, Texas, which hosts SpaceX’s Starship rocket program, has just posted a notice of a public hearing for a gas-fired power plant and supercomputing facility within the corporate limits of the city. The project has an initial cost of $24 billion and will apparently be owned by SpaceX. Bloomberg reports that the project could face resistance from incoming Texas governor Gina Hinojosa, who campaigned against approving new data centers until the state's legislature has enacted stronger protections against potential harms from those projects.

Until now, Elon Musk's SpaceXSI has been building out its Colossus I and Colossus II data centers near the Mississippi-Tennessee border, but those projects have faced backlash, attracting a lawsuit from area residents that aims to curb the pollutants produced by the large number of gas turbines required to power those facilities.

Now, it seems that SpaceXSI wants to build another site in Texas that would lie in a city that mostly sits on land owned by SpaceX and whose population mostly comprises its own employees. The project might face reduced local opposition compared to the company's Memphis facilities, as Starbase is essentially a company town for SpaceX. But Hinojosa's arrival in the governor's office could trump that seemingly permissible local regulatory climate.

The project would seem to be a part of SpaceX’s push to build 10 gigawatts of compute in 2027 alone, and the $24 billion budget for this new facility could make it one of the biggest and most expensive data center projects focused on a single site. The company's announcement also notes that the $24 billion outlay it foresees was only for the “initial phases of the projects” and that it could “include future expansions.” That suggests that SpaceXSI could invest in more capacity in Starbase, Texas, going forward, assuming the favorable regulatory climate the company likely hoped for in the area holds.

Beyond its new terrestrial ambitions in Starbase, SpaceX also remains hard at work on launching data centers into space. Musk has already laid out the plans for a first-generation AI1 compute satellite with a 150kW peak compute payload, and submitted a proposal to build a 1-million-satellite Orbital Data Center System to the FCC. That work could accelerate after data center measures lost big in the poll in the recent U.S. midterm elections. In a post-election post on X, Musk ventured that “data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now.”
