#10476. How Motion Design Improves SaaS Onboarding and Feature Adoption

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most SaaS products don't lose users because the software is bad. They lose users in the first few minutes, before anyone has seen what the product can actually do. Someone signs up, lands on an empty dashboard, feels a flicker of confusion, and quietly leaves. The industry has a name for the gap between signing up and getting value: activation. Closing it is one of the hardest problems in product design.

Motion design is one of the most underused tools for closing that gap. Used well, animation isn't decoration sprinkled on at the end. It's a layer of communication that tells users where to look, confirms that their actions worked, and makes a complex interface feel learnable instead of overwhelming. This article looks at why motion influences behaviour during onboarding, the specific patterns that move the needle on activation and feature adoption, and how to build them responsibly as a developer — with an eye on performance and accessibility throughout.

Motion works in onboarding because it speaks to how human perception actually operates, not because it looks modern. A few principles do most of the heavy lifting.

It directs attention. The eye is drawn to movement before it reads a single word. A gently pulsing button or an element that slides into view tells a new user where to go next without a paragraph of instructions. On a crowded first-run screen, that guidance is the difference between a confident first action and a confused exit.

It preserves context. When a panel expands, a card flips, or a new view slides in from the right, a short transition shows users where things came from and where they went. This spatial continuity keeps people oriented. Interfaces that snap instantly between states force the user to re-scan the screen each time and silently rebuild their mental model.

It confirms actions. Every click, toggle, and form submission is a small question: did that work? A quick visual response — a checkmark drawing itself, a row settling into place — answers it immediately. Without that feedback, users repeat actions, doubt the system, or assume something broke.

It shapes perceived performance. A thoughtful loading animation or skeleton screen makes a wait feel shorter and the product feel responsive, even when the underlying request takes exactly as long. Perception of speed is part of the experience, and motion is how you manage it.

These are the patterns that reliably help new users reach their first win.

Onboarding doesn't end after the first session. Most SaaS products keep shipping features, and each one faces its own adoption problem: users settle into a few familiar workflows and never discover the rest. Motion is how you reintroduce the product to people who already think they know it.

Contextual nudges that appear in motion — a tooltip that eases in near a newly relevant button, a gentle highlight on a feature the moment a user hits the limit it solves — are far more effective than a generic email. They arrive at the point of need and use movement to pull attention without hijacking the screen.

"What's new" reveals can announce an update with a small, well-timed animation rather than a modal that gets dismissed on reflex. A badge that animates in, or a panel that slides open on the first visit to a page, respects the user's flow while still being noticed.

Animated demonstrations carry the most weight for genuinely complex features. Showing a feature in motion — rather than describing it in text — lets users grasp a multi-step workflow in seconds. For intricate capabilities, this is where a short, purpose-made explainer earns its place, a point we return to in the next section.

The right tool depends on how complex the animation is and where it lives. Roughly in order of weight:

CSS transitions and keyframe animations should be your default. They cover the large majority of onboarding motion — hovers, state changes, highlights, progress bars — and the browser can run them efficiently. Reach for JavaScript only when CSS genuinely can't express what you need.

The Web Animations API gives you CSS-level performance with programmatic control, which matters when animations depend on data or user input, or need to be sequenced and interrupted. SitePoint's guide to the Web Animations API is a solid introduction, and the broader web animation archive covers libraries like GSAP for orchestrating complex timelines.

Lottie is the bridge between designer-made animation and the web. Designers export vector animations as JSON, and a lightweight player renders them natively at small file sizes — ideal for polished illustrative moments like success states and animated empty states, without hand-coding every frame.

Pre-rendered video is the right call when a feature is genuinely involved and a looping CSS animation or a tooltip simply can't carry the explanation. A short, well-produced walkthrough can compress a multi-step workflow into something a user absorbs in under a minute, which is why many teams lean on a dedicated SaaS explainer video for their most complex features and onboarding moments. The trade-off is weight and production effort, so reserve video for the handful of places where it genuinely outperforms in-interface motion, lazy-load it, and never autoplay it with sound.

Motion that stutters does more damage than no motion at all, and motion that ignores accessibility can actively harm users. Two disciplines keep animation on the right side of that line.

For performance, animate the properties the browser can handle cheaply: transform and opacity. These can run on the compositor thread without triggering layout or paint, which is what lets animation hold a smooth 60fps. Animating properties like width, height, top, or left forces the browser to recalculate layout on every frame and is the usual cause of janky motion. Keep animations short — most interface transitions should land between 150 and 300 milliseconds — because motion that overstays its welcome makes a product feel slow.

For accessibility, honour the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Some users experience dizziness, nausea, or disorientation from movement, and the operating system lets them ask for less of it. Respecting that setting is straightforward:

Beyond that, never let an animation block interaction. If a user has to wait for a flourish to finish before they can click, the motion is working against them. Animation should support the task and then get out of the way.

Motion design is a product decision, so treat it like one and measure it. The metrics that matter map directly to the problems motion is meant to solve.

Treat animations as hypotheses and A/B test them. Ship the animated onboarding step to half of your new users and measure activation against the control. Motion should earn its place with data, not assumptions — and occasionally the data will tell you an animation is slowing people down, which is just as useful to know.

The best onboarding motion is the kind users never consciously notice. It simply makes the product feel clear, responsive, and easy to learn — guiding attention, confirming actions, and turning a complex interface into something approachable. That's the real payoff: not animation for its own sake, but fewer confused users, faster time to value, and features that actually get discovered.

Start small. Pick the single point in your onboarding where users most often stall, add one purposeful animation that removes the confusion, and measure what happens. Motion, used deliberately, is one of the most direct levers you have on activation and adoption — and like any good tool, it works best when it draws no attention to itself.

#10468. AMD shares how it can retain 95% of server performance with just half the DRAM, sidestepping memory shortages with MEXT

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

MEXT offloads cold memory pages to flash storage and serves them up with a predictive AI model.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In June, AMD overtook the predictive memory engine known as MEXT, which claims to offer upwards of 99% of the performance of a system with full DRAM capacity while running with as little as 25% of the physical DIMMs. The technology offloads cold memory pages to flash storage and uses an AI model to serve pages before they’re needed, and AMD says it plans to extend the tech across a wide swath of its IT infrastructure next year.

The pitch behind MEXT seems too good to be true. According to AMD and MEXT founder Gary Smerdon, the technology can cut the physical DRAM required in a server by 20% to 75% depending on the application and performance target. That missing memory still registers as installed DRAM to the OS. Instead of DRAM, however, MEXT uses relatively inexpensive flash storage to offload cold pages, flying them back in as they’re needed with an AI model. In some of AMD’s internal workloads, such as Synopsys PrimeTime, the company says it’s able to achieve 95% of the performance of a full server while running at 50% DRAM capacity with MEXT.

AMD has deployed MEXT internally on 145 servers, which it says will expand to 2,000 servers next year. In addition, the company has announced a partnership with HPE to bring MEXT to customers. Currently, we’re in phase 1 of the rollout, with some EPYC Venice servers carrying a “MEXT Ready” badge and a dedicated NVMe drive or bay for the offload. Phase 2 brings MEXT licenses out to these servers, as well as the RAM View application. AMD hasn’t said when Phase 2 will begin.

The AI-driven DRAM pricing surge impacts more than just gaming PCs, with enterprises also feeling the brunt of price increases, even if they aren’t running AI workloads. If MEXT is able to achieve what AMD claims, it could be transformative given the current market. Because of that, it’s worth understanding what exactly MEXT is, as well as what AMD claims it can do.

Although MEXT is exciting in concept, it’s obvious that AMD is taking a conservative approach to the rollout. Extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence, after all. The applications of MEXT are currently narrow, though AMD says it’s expanding the workloads it’s using MEXT on with promising early results.

The basic setup of MEXT is simple in concept: cold pages go to flash, AI brings those pages back as needed, and it’s as if the memory was never missing in the first place. The main problem should be obvious. Flash is orders of magnitude slower than DRAM, so streaming pages back into memory from flash as they’re needed isn’t feasible. MEXT’s trick is using an AI model to predict the pages you’ll need. And, as long as the model is able to stay ahead by at least the transfer latency from the SSD, it’s as if the physical DRAM was installed the whole time.

This isn’t a crazy concept considering how a transformer-based LLM, which MEXT uses, operates. If you’re unfamiliar with the inner workings, this open-source visualization breaks down the concept of a transformer AI model cleanly. The foundational concept is next token prediction, which underpins the design of modern LLMs. Given a set of tokens, the LLM determines which token (word, portion of a word, etc.) is most likely to come next. It makes a prediction. In the case of MEXT, it’s predicting what page is needed next, not what word comes next.

Memory offloading to flash isn’t a new concept, but MEXT founder Gary Smerdon says that AI is the critical difference in this technology. MEXT itself is deployed as a Linux kernel module that works with any Linux distro. It uses an “ensemble of AI models,” with the primary transformer-based LLM driving the technology. And critically, it doesn’t come pre-trained.

Instead, AMD says that MEXT trains in under a second when first deployed, operates on one EPYC core without a GPU, and continually trains as the workload progresses. This approach makes sense given the varied workloads that MEXT may apply to. The idea is that it would learn the access patterns of the application in order to drive that prediction engine.

MEXT isn’t perfect, but it doesn’t need to be perfect in every situation. For instance, the order of stored pages isn’t important. With text, an LLM needs to predict the correct text in the correct order, but with MEXT, the order doesn’t matter. As long as the page is in DRAM when it’s needed, that’s fine.

In order to understand the application of MEXT to different workloads, AMD has developed RAM View to go alongside the tech. It’s a memory utilization tool that shows which pages are hot and which have gone cold. As you can see from the example above, a system with 1.5 TB of memory shows near-full utilization across the full workload, but around half of the pages stored in memory haven’t been touched in five minutes or more at any given time.

AMD provided this generalized example and didn’t clarify what workload it was testing. That’s very important, as the utilization breakdown will look vastly different depending on the application(s) running on the server. Initially, AMD is applying MEXT to critical EDA applications it uses internally, such as Synopsys PrimeTime, but the company says it has focused on optimizing agentic AI, database, and media and entertainment workloads as well.

This is important not only because memory access patterns change between applications, but also because performance in those applications reacts differently when a system doesn’t have enough memory. The “memory wall,” as AMD calls it, should be a familiar concept regardless of whether you’ve ever touched a server. With MEXT, AMD says it’s trying to extend this memory wall, which may look different for different applications.

For instance, the company is confident that many workloads can reduce capacity by around 20% to 25%. As the reduction increases, however, the applications become more specialized, and the performance delta becomes greater.

AMD has shared some internal MEXT results, which look impressive, though as usual, it’s always smart to handle first-party benchmarks like this with skepticism. Starting with PrimeTime, where AMD has focused much of its attention on MEXT up to this point, it says servers running at 50% capacity retain 95% of the performance of a full server, and going down to as little as 25% capacity still maintains 80% of the application’s performance.

PrimeTime demands a lot of memory for large designs. There are some applications where performance is nearly identical, according to AMD. In a 3-node cluster running in Azure with 5th-gen EPYC CPUs, AMD says MEXT offers 99% of the performance of the full cluster while running at 50% DRAM capacity with the Spark SQL workload from the TPC-DS benchmark. SQL databases try to cache as much data as possible in memory to prevent I/O bottlenecks, creating a golden opportunity for tech like MEXT that wants to free cold pages.

Perhaps the most applicable workload at the current moment, however, is agentic AI, and AMD says MEXT has similar applications there. Across a five-agent concurrent geomean, AMD claims 99% of the performance of a full server with 50% memory capacity, and 85% of the performance with 25% capacity. With a “memory-hungry” agent, AMD claims 90% and 88% performance for 50% and 25% capacity, respectively.

The slide you can see above has footnote tags. However, AMD has not shared the footnotes with us yet, so we don’t have a view into how these tests were run. Naturally, we assume AMD has, at minimum, represented the performance of MEXT in the best light possible. We can’t do a full analysis without knowing how the tests were run, however.

What is interesting is AMD’s cautious approach to rolling out MEXT. It’s an EPYC-exclusive feature right now, though AMD left the door open for support on future products (consumer devices, such as Strix Halo) far down the road. It will be sold as a license, bundled with the RAM View software, but we don’t have a timeline on when that will happen. And, despite a partnership with HPE to push out “MEXT Ready” servers, we still don’t know when MEXT itself will be ready for deployment beyond AMD’s walls.

Given that conservative approach, there are likely pitfalls to MEXT that we’re not seeing yet, and it’s hard to imagine it will be broadly applicable to every server, at least at the 50% and 25% DRAM capacity levels that AMD showed off with its benchmarks.

However, even being able to trim 25% of a server’s DRAM capacity represents a massive cost savings in the current supply-constrained environment. If some workloads can push that to 50% capacity, that’s even better. Of course, we’ll need to see how MEXT holds up once it’s available broadly, but if AMD’s claims hold water, it could become a defining technology in data centers that can’t seem to get enough memory.

#10464. Why Your AI Agent Can't Read the Web (and How to Build a Fetch Layer That Can)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

This is the least glamorous part of building with LLMs and it's where a lot of agent projects quietly fall over. The model is rarely the problem. The fetch is. In this article we'll look at why live web retrieval has become so unreliable for AI tools, what the numbers say about the shift, and how to build a small, well-behaved fetch layer in Python that fails loudly instead of feeding your model garbage.

A few years ago, a plain requests.get() worked on most of the public web. That's no longer true, and the reason is simple: there are now more bots than people.

According to the 2025 Imperva Bad Bot Report, automated traffic made up 51% of all web traffic in 2024. That's the first time in a decade it has passed human activity. Bad bots alone (scrapers, credential stuffers, scalpers) went from 32% to 37% of traffic in a single year.

Source: Imperva Bad Bot Report 2024 and 2025

From a site owner's point of view, your polite research agent and a credential-stuffing botnet look much the same at the network edge. Both are non-browser clients making fast requests from data-center IP ranges. So the defaults have tightened. In July 2025 Cloudflare began blocking known AI crawlers by default for new customers, and bot management products now look at TLS fingerprints, header order, IP reputation and JavaScript execution, not just the User-Agent string.

It helps to understand the publisher's side of the deal, because it explains why your agent is being treated with suspicion.

Cloudflare splits AI crawling into three purposes: training (collecting data to train models), search (building an index for AI search answers) and user action (fetching a page because a person just asked a question). Training dominates.

Training crawls take content and send nothing back. Cloudflare measures this with a crawl-to-referral ratio: how many pages a platform crawls for every visitor it sends to the site. In its crawl-to-click gap analysis, the ratios for some AI platforms ran into the tens of thousands, compared with single digits for traditional search.

If you ran a publication and saw those numbers, you'd block too. The catch is that blocking is usually done by user agent or IP range, so it hits user-action fetches as well. Those are the ones that happen when a person asks your agent to read a specific article, and Cloudflare's 2025 Year in Review found they grew more than 15x during the year. Your agent is caught in a fight it didn't start.

The mix of crawlers also shifts quickly, which makes static allow and block lists fragile on both sides:

And restrictions keep piling up. The Consent in Crisis study audited 14,000 web domains and found that a large and fast-growing share of the sources behind the C4 training corpus now restrict AI crawlers, either through robots.txt or their terms of service.

Source: Longpre et al., Consent in Crisis (NeurIPS 2024)

So that's the environment your agent fetches in. Here's how to build for it.

Before writing any retry logic, put names on your failures. "The request failed" is useless to both you and the model. In practice almost every failed fetch falls into one of four buckets:

Bucket 2 is the dangerous one, because a block page often comes back with a 200 OK. If you don't check, the model will happily summarise "Checking your browser before accessing..." as if it were the article.

Here's a compact fetch layer in Python using httpx and the standard library's robots.txt parser. It's around 100 lines and covers the four buckets above.

Use a descriptive user agent with a contact URL. It costs you nothing and site operators are far more likely to allow a client they can identify and reach. Then check robots.txt (standardised as RFC 9309) before every new host.

The lru_cache means you fetch robots.txt once per origin rather than once per page. In a long-running service you'll want a cache with a TTL instead, since robots.txt files do change.

This is the core of the layer. Instead of returning raw HTML, return a result that says which bucket you landed in.

The marker list is deliberately simple. It won't catch every interstitial, but it catches the common ones, and a false "blocked" is much cheaper than a confident summary of a CAPTCHA page. Log every blocked result with its first few hundred characters so you can grow the list from real data.

Retries should depend on the bucket. Throttled and broken responses get exponential backoff with jitter. Blocked and not-allowed responses get no retries at all.

Note the cap on wait. A site sending Retry-After: 3600 is telling you to go away for an hour. An interactive agent should report that to the user rather than block a request thread for that long.

The cheapest fetch is the one you don't make. Agents often re-read the same documentation page several times in one session. Store the ETag and Last-Modified headers and send them back as If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. A 304 Not Modified response costs the server almost nothing and lowers the odds of being rate limited.

Wire these into fetch() by passing headers=conditional_headers(url) to client.get(). On a 304, return the cached text. On a 200, call remember().

Finally, don't hide failures from the LLM. Give the tool output a structured shape so the model can reason about it:

(extract_main_text is whatever readability or boilerplate-removal step you already use. Libraries like trafilatura work well here.)

With this in place, the model's answer changes from a made-up summary to "I couldn't read that page because the site blocks automated access. Here's what I found from two other sources." That's a better product, and it builds trust with users.

Once you've done everything above, some legitimate user-action fetches will still fail. That's usually because the site blocks data-center IP ranges outright, not because of anything your agent did. Teams handle this in a few ways, roughly in order of preference:

The principle is the same in every case: the network path is a reliability decision, not a way around a site's wishes. Your robots.txt check and your honest user agent should stay in place whichever route the request takes.

Once your fetch layer returns structured results, observability is easy. Emit one event per fetch with the host, status bucket and latency, and watch these:

If you're prototyping agents with AI coding assistants, ask them to generate these metrics hooks from the start. They're easy to bolt on now and painful to add later. For a sense of how well current tools handle this kind of infrastructure code, Vibe Coder Journal publishes hands-on reviews and benchmarks of AI coding tools.

The web your agent reads in 2026 isn't the open, fetch-anything web many of us learned on. More than half the traffic is automated, publishers have good reasons to be wary of AI crawlers, and the defaults now lean toward blocking. You can't change that. You can make your agent a good citizen, though: one that identifies itself, respects the rules, backs off when asked, and tells the truth when it can't read something.

Most of the reliability gains come from the unglamorous parts: classifying failures, caching and passing honest errors to the model. Get those right and your agent will feel much more dependable, even on the days when half the web won't let it in.

It depends on the jurisdiction, the site's terms of service and what you do with the content. Respecting robots.txt and site terms is the baseline. For commercial products, get proper legal advice.

Many site owners expect it to, and RFC 9309 makes no exception for user-triggered fetches. The safest default is to obey it and tell the user when a page is disallowed.

Many bot-protection systems serve a challenge page with a 200 status. Check the body for challenge markers before passing it to the model, as in the looks_blocked() function above.

Only for pages that render their main content with JavaScript. Try a plain HTTP fetch first. Headless browsers are slower, cost more to run and are easier to fingerprint.

#10457. Who is Alice Huang? What to know about the businesswoman involved in the Nvidia scandal

She was building a career in Chinese finance before turning to tech with a leadership role at a data center company in Singapore. He is the high-profile CEO of Nvidia, the California-based company that makes computer chips relied upon by advanced artificial intelligence systems.

An affair between Nvidia’s Jensen Huang and Alice Huang, who reportedly was suspected of working as a spy for Beijing, has rocked the tech and political worlds.

The Chinese businesswoman also known as Huang Le had a close relationship with Jensen Huang, according to reports over the past month by the New York Times. The newspaper also reported that top policymakers were informed in a memo that intelligence indicated she was a spy for China’s Ministry of State Security, recruited to influence Nvidia to make its most advanced chips more available in China.

In one widely shared photograph of the pair, Jensen Huang and people surrounding him are holding drinks in Taipei, while Alice Huang sits behind him, apparently watching him in conversation. The picture was posted in 2024 by software company NetThunder to its LinkedIn page.

It remains unclear how long the affair lasted, and when exactly the relationship might have ended. The Associated Press was unable to reach Alice Huang for comment.

Who Alice Huang is

Publicly available information about Alice Huang is scant.

She was a Chinese finance executive before becoming involved in artificial intelligence, data centers and computer chips.

In 2023, she became a director at Singapore-based Megaspeed, which describes itself as an “NVIDIA cloud partner.” Last year, Megaspeed faced scrutiny from the U.S. over its alleged skirting of U.S. export restrictions and routing of a large amount of advanced Nvidia chips for Chinese users.

Megaspeed was incorporated in 2015 in Singapore with the name 7Road International, after being spun off as the overseas arm of a Chinese video game developer, according to corporate registration records.

Filings show that in March 2023, it renamed itself Megaspeed International, with all of the shares transferred to Huang Le Limited, a company registered in the British Virgin Islands.

That same month, Alice Huang became Megaspeed’s controlling shareholder, according to research by the C4ADS, a Washington-based nonprofit focused on global security.

But by November 2023, she was no longer listed as a director at Megaspeed, and all her shares were transferred to a Singaporean businessman, Tan Yong Pong, who also is known as James Tan.

While public records suggest Alice Huang left Megaspeed, short seller Culper Research issued a report in May this year that cited a Megaspeed employee saying Huang was “actually still our boss” and was still involved in the company’s daily operations.

Before Megaspeed, Alice Huang previously held senior positions at the Chinese firm Shanghai Hexi Investment Co. from around 2015 to 2017, according to social media posts and records from Simuwang, a Chinese private equity database.

Her roles at Hexi included deputy general manager, executive director and member of the investment committee, according to Hexi’s social media posts, among possible other roles.

Hexi’s founder Kang Wei, who appeared on some records alongside Alice Huang, was fined in 2018 by China’s securities regulator 400,000 yuan ($60,000) over financial misconduct behaviors while Hexi was fined 17.7 million yuan ($2.7 million).

Before that, Alice Huang worked at HSBC’s Hong Kong private banking arm and at CITIC Securities, a Chinese investment bank.

HSBC and CITIC did not immediately respond to a request for comment on her employment history.

Beijing has refuted claims that Alice Huang was a Chinese spy. Its Foreign Ministry said this week that the “Chinese spy allegation is unsubstantiated. It is nothing but smears against China.”

Why Megaspeed is at the center of the controversy

With the emergence of the latest scandal, Singapore-based AI computing company Megaspeed, where Alice Huang was a senior executive, is once again under the spotlight.

Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer, was originally incorporated in 2015 before changing its name to Megaspeed International in 2023. Nvidia lists Megaspeed as a partner on its website, while Megaspeed says on its website it’s “a core business partner of NVIDIA’s computing power cloud business in the Asia-Pacific region.”

It was the overseas gaming arm of Shenzhen-based developer 7Road Holdings Ltd.

Late last year, Megaspeed denied accusations following reports that it was routing Nvidia chips to China, saying in a statement on its website that “there has been no evidence of any violations concerning the illicit transfer of chips or any illegal activities.”

Singapore’s government said in late October, after fresh reports that Alice Huang was accused of being a Chinese intelligence source, that authorities were investigating Megaspeed and that Singapore “does not condone businesses” circumventing or violating other countries’ export controls. However, it said, there were no investigations conducted in respect to Alice Huang.

Megaspeed did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

—Associated Press

#10456. Ray-Ban Meta Audio review: Great specs, shame about the price

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses attempt to curb the controversy by removing the cameras, and while they’re still an excellent product, the price makes them harder to recommend.

This has been a bit of a rollercoaster year for Meta wearables. Meta’s VR division shrank considerably earlier this year, then later announced a massively redesigned follow-up that’ll launch next year. And while Meta has debuted dozens of new smart glasses styles throughout the year that have sold quite well, the company can’t seem to get away from controversy. That’s where Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses come in.

Now, that doesn’t mean Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses aren’t worthwhile. They feature a much wider prescription range (-12 to +6) than the majority of Meta smart glasses models, which often cap out at -6 to +6. The battery life is also significantly better thanks to the lack of a camera and other improvements in the hardware. And, of course, if you want a pair of AI glasses but don’t want the camera, these are the only options from Meta.

If I were to choose between the two available models, I’d pick the Clubmaster style every time. Not only do they look better overall and feature the thinnest frames of any Meta smart glasses, but they also have substantially better nosepads that are adjustable and can be easily replaced.

The Burbank model looks just fine, but the nosepads aren’t the same, and the glasses feel more rigid overall (which I find less comfortable). Both models come in lots of colors with many different lens options, including clear, tinted, polarized, Transitions, and prescription.

Both models feature a touchpad on the outside of the right temple arm, as well as a small, circular button up top to activate Meta AI. You can alternatively use the “Hey, Meta” keywords to activate Meta AI. The button and touchpad can be disabled if you like. Only the touchpad has an alternative function, and that alternative function only controls music playback via Spotify or Amazon Music. Other Meta glasses offer far more options.

Both styles are the lightest Meta glasses available. Most Meta smart glasses with cameras weigh in around the 54g mark. Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses weigh just 44g. They’re not as light as Even Realities G2, RayNeo iO, or MemoMind One, but 44g is still considered pretty light for smart glasses.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses reuse the case from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2, which looks more like a traditional Ray-Ban case rather than the foldable one that ships with Meta Ray-Ban Display or the 2026 Meta Camera Glasses. I don’t have an issue with Ray-Ban Meta Audio’s case at all, but I really enjoy the foldable one and wish it were included.

Audio playback on these is great, and while the speakers are identical to all other Meta glasses products, using them as spatial surround sound “headphones” while watching a movie with surround sound is a fantastic experience. They also have spatial audio recording and truly excellent noise cancellation thanks to the 6-mic setup.

But the lack of the newer processor from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 3 means the glasses don’t support the new hearing aid function. To me, that’s a massive missed opportunity since these are audio-only glasses. When I asked Meta about it, they only said it wasn’t available on Ray-Ban Meta Audio for now, so this problem could be fixed in an upcoming update.

And that’s actually where Meta’s glasses might shine brightest: software updates. The company has been exemplary with update cadence on all of its products, and its smart glasses constantly get new, innovative features that surprise and delight. While I’m a little underwhelmed with the number of features available on these glasses today, this product will likely look quite different a year from now, a fact that’s backed up by years of Meta significantly improving product feature sets over time.

If you buy these glasses today, know that you’re mainly investing in them for the future. They’re fine enough for today’s usage, especially if you’re interested in a hands-free way to use Meta AI products like Muse or just want an open-ear way to listen to music and take phone calls, but Meta’s penchant for continually adding new features is, undoubtedly, going to be the highlight for everyone who ever buys them.

#10455. How to Use CSS Attribute Selectors to Build More Flexible User Interfaces

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developing a website, developers often rely on CSS classes to style buttons, forms, navigation elements, and other interface components. This approach works well, but some situations require styles to depend on an element's HTML attributes rather than additional class names.

CSS attribute selectors provide a practical solution. They allow developers to target elements based on whether an attribute exists, what value it contains, or whether its value matches a particular pattern. Used carefully, they can simplify styling and make reusable components easier to maintain.

An attribute selector targets an HTML element using square brackets. For example, a developer can select every input field that has a required attribute:

This rule applies to inputs with the required attribute, regardless of their input type.

Developers can also target a specific attribute value:

This is useful when different input types need different visual treatments without introducing additional classes into the HTML.

Forms are a practical place to apply attribute selectors because HTML already provides attributes that describe how individual fields behave.

Consider a registration form containing email, password, and telephone fields. Each input can be styled according to its purpose:

The CSS can target these fields independently:

This approach keeps the markup descriptive while allowing the stylesheet to respond to the attributes already present in the document.

One important distinction is that required indicates a validation requirement, while :invalid represents an input's current validity state. These should not be treated as interchangeable.

CSS provides several matching operators that help developers target more specific attribute values.

These operators can be useful when working with links, downloadable files, language attributes, and reusable components.

For example, a website might want to distinguish PDF downloads from ordinary navigation links:

This adds a small visual indicator to links whose URLs end in .pdf.

Attribute selectors can also help identify links with different destinations or behaviors.

For example, external links that use HTTPS can receive a subtle visual indicator:

However, matching an HTTPS URL does not necessarily prove that a link points to an external website. Internal links can also use HTTPS, so developers should consider the actual navigation requirements before applying special styling.

For more examples of selector combinations and matching rules, SitePoint's guide to CSS Attribute Selectors provides a useful reference.

Although attribute selectors are flexible, they should not replace every class-based styling decision.

A selector such as [class*="button"] can match unintended elements if their class names happen to contain the same text. Similarly, broad attribute rules may affect components that were not originally intended to receive those styles.

For predictable results, use exact attribute matching whenever possible and prefer descriptive classes for major component styling.

Developers should also remember that CSS selectors only control presentation. They do not replace JavaScript validation, server-side security checks, or proper HTML semantics.

The MDN documentation on attribute selectors provides further details about selector syntax, matching behavior, and supported operators.

Before adding attribute selectors to a large stylesheet, inspect the affected elements using browser developer tools.

Check whether the selector matches the intended elements.

Test fields with different attribute values.

Verify disabled, required, and invalid form states.

Check navigation links and downloadable files.

Test the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

Review whether existing component styles are being unintentionally overridden.

This process helps identify unexpected matches before they affect the production interface.

CSS attribute selectors give developers another way to create flexible and maintainable interfaces without adding unnecessary classes to every HTML element.

They are particularly useful for form styling, link identification, and components that already contain meaningful HTML attributes. By choosing precise selectors, understanding matching operators, and testing changes before deployment, developers can use this feature to write cleaner CSS while keeping their markup descriptive and organized.

#10453. Nvidia stock gets its first boost of the Jensen Huang scandal era. What’s driving shares higher today?

Shares of Nvidia Corp. (Nasdaq: NVDA) rose over 4% in premarket on Wednesday, and it has CoreWeave to thank. 

The semiconductor giant has spent days watching its stock tumble following news that CEO Jensen Huang had an extramarital affair with a alleged Chinese spy. Shares were down 15.61% in the last five days and 26.34% for the month. 

Now, Nvidia’s stock appears to be benefiting from good news over at CoreWeave. On Tuesday, the cloud-computing company shared third-quarter earnings that beat Wall Street’s revenue estimates and included an improved outlook.

CoreWeave’s revenue rose to $3.66 billion, higher than the expected $3.55 billion, according to consensus estimates cited by CNBC. Its revenue was up 168% year-over-year (YOY). 

The company also raised its 2026 full year outlook for revenue from $13 billion to $13.3 billion. 

CoreWeave’s shares (Nasdaq: CRWV) rose over 15% during premarket Wednesday in response.  

Why does CoreWeave’s success help Nvidia? 

Overall, when one major AI company succeeds it helps ease investors’ concerns about the AI bubble bursting, buoying fellow AI stocks alongside it. 

In this case, Nvidia has even more to gain from CoreWeave’s success. In January, Nvidia invested $2 billion in CoreWeave, making it the company’s second largest shareholder.

So, when CoreWeave does well, it directly boosts Nvidia’s standing. 

This jump also marks a turnaround for CoreWeave, which has seen its stock struggle amid fears of AI overspending.

So-called neocloud companies like CoreWeave, a cloud provider specialized for AI, have been hit especially hard recently. Ahead of its earnings report, CoreWeave’s shares were down 40.63% in the last month. 

What will happen at Nvidia? 

How things at Nvidia will shake out is anyone’s guess right now. Betting markets predict that Huang won’t leave the CEO role—at least any time soon. The company’s board of directors is currently investigating his affair and any impact on the company. 

At the same time, Baird just downgraded Nvidia’s stock from “Outperform” to “Neutral.” Morgan Stanley also dropped Nvidia as one of its top picks, CNBC reports.

#10451. Your next great hire might have a military résumé

How many military veterans has your company hired in the past five years? And how many of those hires came from actively recruiting veterans rather than simply waiting for them to apply?

I’ve spent more than 30 years helping companies find talent, and I know how easy it is to rule out good candidates when their backgrounds don’t exactly match what a hiring manager expects.

Military veterans are a good example. They’ve spent years developing practical skills, but getting employers to recognize how those skills translate to civilian jobs can be an uphill battle.

In fact, while 69% of U.S. employers report difficulty finding qualified talent, many veterans are struggling to find work that makes full use of their experience. According to Indeed and The Harris Poll, 57% of veterans surveyed felt underemployed or believed their jobs underrepresented their military responsibilities.

Some of the people you’re looking for could already have years of relevant experience, even if their résumés don’t look quite like what you’re used to seeing.

4 ways to tap into overlooked veteran talent

I’ve worked with plenty of employers who want to hire veterans but aren’t sure how to evaluate their military experience. A few changes to how you approach recruiting and hiring make a real difference.

1. Understand the experience behind military job titles

Our team at Orion Talent helped place a Navy pilot in a service area manager position at a medical imaging company. On paper, he probably wasn’t the candidate you’d expect to oversee technicians who install and repair MRI and CT equipment in hospitals; he’d never worked in healthcare or managed a service business.

But look a little closer and there were clear parallels between the work he did as a naval aviator and the role of service area manager. He had direct experience operating complex, high-value equipment where maintenance discipline could literally mean life or death. He also worked closely with maintenance crews and led people under pressure.

Our job was to help the candidate and hiring manager see how his experience fit the role, and once they did, it was clear he was the strongest applicant.

When you’re interviewing veterans, spend time getting into the details of what their military work actually involved. For instance, I always want to ask about the equipment they worked with, the people they supervised, and the decisions they were responsible for making. You might be surprised by how much of that experience applies to the position you’re trying to fill.

2. Prioritize skills and learning agility over traditional credentials

I’ve encountered employers with pretty entrenched ideas about what a qualified candidate should look like, especially for technical positions. They want someone who can come in and do the job, which makes sense.

But when those requirements become too prescriptive, you risk screening out candidates with relevant experience. This happens a lot with highly technical roles, which are notoriously difficult to fill.

Someone who served in the Navy’s nuclear program or worked as an Electronics Technician, Electrician’s Mate, or Electrical Power Production specialist with the Air Force could have experience that’s directly relevant to a variety of roles in data centers, power generation, advanced manufacturing, and other critical infrastructure operations.

Most of the time, applicable experience doesn’t come through as a 1:1 match with the job description, so you have to take the time to probe for it. But if you give veterans (and all candidates) a chance to show what they know and how quickly they pick up new skills, you’ll likely be surprised by the number of qualified candidates you have to choose from.

3. Prepare your organization to support veteran talent

Expanding your talent pipeline to include more candidates with nontraditional experience is a different thing from actually hiring them.

Recruiters may understand why someone’s military experience makes them a strong candidate, but the hiring manager who makes the final call often can’t quite envision them in the role and goes with a “safer” choice.

This isn’t just an anecdotal observation. Research from Harvard Business School and the Burning Glass Institute found that skills-based hiring reforms led to increased opportunities for workers without bachelor’s degrees in fewer than one of every 700 hires.

As someone who’s seen the value veterans bring to private-sector jobs again and again, that frustrates me. My challenge to every organization this Veterans Day is to commit to coaching its hiring teams on how to evaluate veteran job candidates. If you don’t know where to start, bring in a consultant with specialized military recruiting expertise to help.

4. Build relationships before positions need to be filled

A lot of recruiting happens because somebody leaves or a new position opens up, and suddenly there’s a mad scramble to find the right person. But service members are often planning their next career move well before leaving the military, which means they’re not available to start in the next two to four weeks.

However, taking an always-on approach to recruiting means you can tap into military talent pools while simultaneously bolstering your talent pipeline.

If you’re always talking to potential candidates, even without a perfect open position, you can avoid the scramble when it’s time to bring someone on board. That’s especially true if you know you have plans to open a new location or expand a facility in the months or year ahead.

If you’re in a specialized or technical field like energy or data centers, talking to transitioning service members early (I recommend three to six months before their separation) is beneficial for both of you.

In these sectors, attracting candidates with the right skills who are willing to relocate is tough. But transitioning service members are in the process of deciding where to live. Starting the conversation early means you both have time to determine if your organization is a good match for their skills and career goals.

Give military talent the recognition it deserves

Approximately 200,000 service members transition out of the military each year. That’s talent we can’t afford to leave on the table.

If you’re not already recruiting transitioning service members, why not? If the answer is that you’ve just never thought about it before, please do.

Our former service members are highly skilled leaders, and I hope every HR executive, recruiter, and hiring manager will take the time to reflect on how their hiring practices could be more inclusive of veterans and all nontraditional candidates.

You might just find that someone whose background looks a little different on paper is exactly the person your team has been looking for.

#10448. Years after the stabbing death of Cash App founder Bob Lee, a judge delivers a prison sentence

The tech consultant convicted in the stabbing death of Cash App founder Bob Lee was sentenced in San Francisco on Tuesday to 16 years to life to prison.

A jury in December 2024 found Nima Momeni guilty of second-degree murder, rejecting the defendant’s claim that he had acted in self-defense. He has remained in custody since his arrest nine days after the April 4, 2023, killing.

Before imposing the sentence, San Francisco Superior Court Judge Alexandra Gordon rejected Momeni’s request for a new trial. His attorneys have said they plan to appeal.

Members of Lee’s family addressed the court on Tuesday, including his 18-year-old daughter, Scout Lee, who spoke directly to Momeni.

“You did not just end his life, you ended an entire future built around a man,” she told Momeni, according to media reports.

Momeni, wearing an orange jail jumpsuit, maintained that he acted in self-defense. He told Lee’s family that he understood their loss.

“Unfortunately, we have completely different understandings of what transpired,” Momeni said.

Lee, a renowned tech mogul, was found staggering on a deserted downtown street, dripping a trail of blood and calling for help. Lee, 43, later died at a hospital.

Prosecutors said Momeni planned the attack on Lee, driving him to an isolated spot under the Bay Bridge and stabbing him three times, including once to the heart, with a knife he took from his sister’s kitchen. They said Momeni was angry with Lee for introducing his younger sister to a drug dealer she says gave her GHB and other drugs and then sexually assaulted her.

But Momeni testified on the stand that Lee was the one who attacked him with a knife, angry after the tech consultant chided him about spending more time with his family instead of searching for a strip club that night. Momeni, who studies martial arts, said he didn’t realize he had fatally wounded Lee or that Lee was even hurt.

Lee had created mobile payment service Cash App and was the chief product officer of the cryptocurrency MobileCoin when he died. He had recently moved to Miami from the San Francisco Bay Area, where his ex-wife Krista Lee lives with their two children, and had returned to California for a visit.

Jurors in the end rejected a charge of murder in the first degree, which required prosecutors prove that Momeni acted deliberately, willfully and with premeditation. Murder in the second degree does not require premeditation.

—Associated Press

#10444. People can’t stop trolling AI job ‘interviewers’

AI is conducting job interviews more and more widely. And while it may be anxiety-inducing for some—as they attempt to figure out what a robot deems hirable—others are rage-baiting the technology for the lolz in skits online.

In one recent viral video shared to X, content creator Nick Reid hilariously trolled his AI interviewer, “Karen,” who was seemingly screening him for a job at Burger King—one he clearly didn’t want. 

Mind you, Karen isn’t a real BK “employee”: ”We do not use, nor are we aware of any Franchisee across the U.S. or Canada using a tool like this,” Burger King told Fast Company in a comment. But the comedy skit still highlights how awkward interacting with AI bots can be.

And for viewers fed up with the AI revolution, it may be more than a little cathartic to watch.

In the video, which has racked up nearly 8 million views, Reid appears shirtless and with a shoe on his head and introduces himself as Crangeus McBasketball. “Why on Earth do you have a shoe on your head?” Karen replies, while he laughs in her face.

Mr. McBasketball continues to mock the AI by telling it he’s extremely busy and responding to its questions in more and more ridiculous ways. When the AI asks if he would be able to reliably get to shifts at the restaurant, he responds by saying, “Yeah. I have feet.” Karen’s not having it: “That is an incredibly odd way to answer a simple question.” 

But this isn’t the only corner of the internet in which “jobseekers” are taking the mickey out of AI avatars. In another video shared to LinkedIn, a man trolls his AI interviewer by speaking utter gibberish. Again, the AI doesn’t mince words. 

“This is an absolute waste of my time,” it tells him. “I don’t know if you are having a breakdown or if you simply lack the basic capacity to speak English,” it says.

YouTuber Graham Raabe recently took a swing, too. In a video that now has over 6.3 million views on YouTube and additional ones on X, the creator staged a fake interview with the purpose of pitting two AIs against one another. The creator brings his own bot to the interview, whom he introduces as his “representative,” then excuses himself from the room. Hilarity ensues when the two AIs are left to talk amongst themselves circuitously without an agenda and zero human awareness. 

The AI rage-baiting trend is undoubtedly funny. But the comedy videos also sort of demonstrate how sophisticated the AI interviewers actually are. 

While there have been some cases of AIs glitching out or getting confused, even when interviewers are purposefully trolling bots, the AIs seem to be responding in a more and more humanlike fashion—like showing frustration with having their time wasted, or recognizing that the candidate is not actually making an effort. 

Yet, at the same time, they’re also still far from human; Raabe’s AI “interviewer” and “representative” cross-talked in circles frequently and spent over a minute repeatedly saying goodbye to each other even after Raabe left the room.

Jokes aside, while some find real job interviews with AI to be totally cringe, others actually see its value: According to a 2025 study from the University of Chicago, interviews conducted by AI may actually lead to an increase in job offers. The survey found that AI interviews led to a 12% increase in offers, as well as a 17% increase in 30-day retention. 

Of course, many are frustrated with non-human interviews taking off, thus the trend’s emergence in the first place. Candidates often want to connect with an actual human to get a feel for a company, and they also don’t like the fact that AI is replacing so many real people for certain tasks. This comedic content taps into that.

Sure, the real thing may have real benefits. But for those who can’t take the AIs seriously, or are overall fed up with the surge in AI taking over tasks, trolling the bots nobody asked for seems like a spot-on human response.

#10443. Linux kernel reviewer steps down from memory management duties due to influx of AI slop patches

"Nowadays it's pretty much half of what I see, if not more."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Linux Kernel is probably one of the best ecosystems to observe regarding the impact of AI in programming. The critical importance of its code and its maintainers' quality standards mean that patches, including but not only AI slop, go through rigorous review and are often discarded. Still, the amount of AI-produced code is often far higher than what the human reviewers can deal with. Lorenzo Stoakes, a lead Linux reviewer for memory management, has elected to step down from some of his former duties for exactly that reason.

Stoakes announced his decision today in a thread on the Linux kernel mailing list (LKML). He'll be leaving the core, reclaim, Rust, THP (Huge Pages), and miscellaneous sections of the kernel memory management section, while staying on for his remaining duties, including KVM. The partial departure appears amicable, and the developer doesn't appear to be angry or bitter. He says that his decision was due to the mounting pressure on his inbox, that he'll wrap up the patch series he's currently working on, and he'll occasionally pitch in regardless.

However, the underlying cause is also clear: "Of course the AI wave has been horrible, and is getting significantly worse. At first it was once in a while that I'd see slop. Then it was at least once a week. Then every few days. Nowadays it's pretty much half of what I see, if not more."

This is a sentiment echoed by many programmers in the open-source community, and the latest in a running series of AI-provoked project departures, submission rules adjustments, and strategic shifts. AI-assisted programming has arguably been both a boon and a curse for open-source software. The project's open nature means that anyone can find a bug and report it or fix it, or add functionality — all of which are good things.

However, that also means that literally anyone can contribute, and in a world ridden with low-effort AI slop and untold amounts of AI agents, that's... suboptimal. Open-source projects are arguably the hardest-hit because they very rarely have the adequate manpower or funds to keep the code up to date, much less deal with the output of a million robotic monkeys on a million digital typewriters.

Linus Torvalds, creator and headmaster of Linux, has taken a pro-AI stance, supported not only by the speed of additional contributions, but also the fact that bots often don't make mistakes that even seasoned developers do. This topic has been the subject of a lot of LKML discussion, and the current stance is that AI-assisted code commits don't need to be marked as such.

The problem, then, has arguably become one of filtering: it's easy enough to be accepting of AI contributions when your day isn't spent waddling through mud to find the one gold nugget, which is what Stoakes was seemingly weary of.

Back in July, he'd already brought up concerns about the "recent wave of slop in [memory management]," adding that "the amount of time wasted on AI slop is already too much and we're only at the start of this." Even as recently as a month ago, he said that "what kills the project is having 1,000 slop submissions that 2 or 3 people have to do something with."

#10440. NYC Ferries are becoming floating classrooms for thousands of students

On weekday mornings, thousands of New Yorkers traverse the city by ferry. Thirty eight boats in the city’s fleet criss-cross the East River, travel up the Hudson, or loop around the bottom of Brooklyn to the Rockaways in order to bring people to work or school. In a city surrounded by water, the ferry is just another way to commute.

But after that morning rush hour, starting in April 2027, some NYC Ferries will be taking on a new task. They’ll transform into floating classrooms, giving the city’s public students an on-the-water, interactive lesson in marine science, the history of the city’s waterways, and what maritime careers could await them.

Those lessons will come from educators with the Billion Oyster Project, a nonprofit that works to restore oyster reefs across New York Harbor and help city residents connect to the water all around them. 

“We as New Yorkers have been disconnected by 100 years of danger and pollution and dirtiness from the fact that we are a water town,” says Brian Reagor, director of development at the Billion Oyster Project, which announced the partnership alongside leaders from the NYC Economic Development Council and ferry operator Hornblower Group on Nov. 9 . “[The harbor] is our biggest open space, and yet so few New Yorkers ever come anywhere near it.”

Aquatic lessons

The 18-year-old Billion Oyster Project has long focused on education alongside its larger goal of restoring one billion oysters back to New York Harbor. It crafts curriculums for city educators, brings workshops into classrooms, and has a partnership with the New York Harbor School on Governors Island, a maritime vocational high school, with parts of the nonprofit’s oyster restoration efforts. 

Through these efforts, Billion Oyster Project has taught more than 66,000 students about the Harbor and its inhabitants. But it wants to reach more, and specifically wants to bring more of those kids out onto the water.

Initially, Reagor says, the nonprofit considered buying its own boat, but it quickly realized that would be too expensive. A teacher at the Harbor School brought up partnering with the NYC Ferry. The city’s ferry boats are busy for the morning rush hour, from 7 to 10 a.m. But then ridership drops off.

Last year, the nonprofit approached NYC Ferry, the Hornblower Group (which operates the city’s ferry fleet), and the New York City Economic Development Corporation about using one of those under-utilized boats to take students out on the water. 

Getting their sea legs

The floating classrooms will operate on school days from Labor Day to Memorial Day, taking up to two classroom trips per day during off peak hours. That can reach 300 students and teachers daily. The nonprofit’s goal is to bring more than 10,000 public school students onto the harbor annually.

The exact curriculum of the floating classrooms will vary depending on the ferry route. The boats will pick up students and teachers from any of the 25 ferry stops along the harbor and take them on a 90 minute field trip. 

The primary goal of the floating classrooms is to simply introduce students to the harbor. “We’re going to talk about the animals that live in the harbor,” Reagor says. “We’re going to talk about the ecosystem and the food web. We’re going to talk about the history of the harbor, how the harbor was the centering point of the city for so many years.”

Students might be surprised to learn, for example, that there are seahorses all throughout the East River. They could meet one on the adventure if they’re lucky, Reagor says. They’ll also learn about the return of dolphins, whales, ospreys, and bald eagles to the harbor. All those animals are the top of the food chain, and they’re back because of efforts to revive oyster reefs.

Bringing oysters back

In the 1800s, New York clearcut thousands of acres of oyster reef and overharvested the mollusk to the point where it was functionally extinct by the turn of the 20th century. Then, industrialization turned the waterfront into a hotbed of pollution. The 1972 Clean Water Act helped turn things around, and now the water is as clean as it was in the early 1800s. “It’s actually one of America’s and the climate movement’s great success stories,” Reagor says. 

That’s allowed oysters to thrive again. Billion Oyster Project has put about 200 million oysters and 19 acres of reef back in the water so far. Oysters filter the water, stabilize the shoreline against climate impacts, increase biodiversity, and create habitats for species like fish, crab, shrimp, and more. 

Restoring New York Harbor also restores the image of New York as a water city, one teeming with maritime and environmental careers. New York County alone, which covers Manhattan, is home to 17% of all the marine jobs is the Mid-Atlantic region, according to the NOAA Office for Coastal Management.

Students can get on a marine career path through the Harbor School, a public high school that also offers a free associates degree. More than 50 of the school’s Vessel Operations graduates now work at NYC Ferry as captains or deckhands. But students can get primers on careers in aquaculture, marine biology, ocean engineering, diving professions, coastal sustainability, and more.

The second aim of the floating classroom initiative is to open students’ eyes to those kinds of opportunities. “We’re teaching STEM skills while also showing them a potential vision for their own futures, if they are so inspired,” Reagor says. 

But even if students don’t walk away from a floating classroom voyage with the desire to pursue a marine career, that’s okay. “We have at least left you with a better understanding of the environment right outside your door,” Reagor says. “You’ve been on the harbor. It’s not this scary, mythical thing. The idea is making nature, the environment, and climate real to people.”

#10438. How to Get a YouTube API Key and Use the Data API (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You need a YouTube API key the moment your app has to read anything from YouTube: a video's length, its view count, a channel's latest uploads. Getting one takes about five minutes. Using it well takes a little longer, because YouTube changed how its quota works in June 2026, and plenty of guides still describe the old rules.

In this guide we'll get a key, lock it down and test it. Then we'll use it from Node.js for three real jobs: adding up the length of a playlist, listing a channel's videos without spending a search, and estimating what a video earned. Along the way we'll cover what the YouTube API costs (nothing), how the 2026 quota works, and the errors you're most likely to hit.

A YouTube API key identifies your project to the YouTube Data API v3. With just a key, you can read public data:

A key isn't enough for anything that touches someone's account. Uploading videos, editing playlists or reading private data needs OAuth 2.0, where the user signs in and grants your app access.

You'll need a Google account. The YouTube Data API is free, so there's nothing to pay for.

The restrictions matter more than they look. A key is just a string in a URL, and anyone who copies an unrestricted one can spend your daily quota for you. With an API restriction, the key is useless for other Google APIs. With an application restriction, it only works from your server or your site. Changes to restrictions can take up to five minutes to apply, so don't panic if a request fails straight after you save.

Before writing any code, check the key with a single request. This one asks for the first video ever uploaded to YouTube, and uses the fields parameter to trim the response down to four values:

That request cost 1 unit of your daily quota, which brings us to the question everyone asks.

Yes. There's no charge per request and no paid plan. Instead you get a daily quota, and since June 1, 2026 it's split into separate buckets:

Google's quota calculator lists the cost of every method. Quotas reset at midnight Pacific Time. Every request costs at least 1 unit, including requests that fail, so a retry loop around a broken call can use up a day's quota on its own.

If you've read older guides, search is the big change. A search used to cost 100 units out of the same 10,000, so 100 searches emptied your whole day. Uploads were worse still, at about 1,600 units each until December 2025. Now both have their own buckets, and the 10,000 units are left for everything else.

To give you a feel for what 10,000 units buys, here's what each example in this guide costs:

You can't pay for more. To go above the defaults, fill in the YouTube API Services Audit and Quota Extension Form, which is linked from Google's page on quota and compliance audits. YouTube first audits your project to check it follows the YouTube API Services Terms of Service, then someone from the API team gets back to you. Google doesn't give a timeline, so apply well before you need the extra quota.

Most projects never need to. The rest of this guide is about getting a lot done with very few units.

The examples need Node.js 20.6 or later, for the built-in fetch and the --env-file flag. There are no packages to install. Make a folder with a .env file in it:

Every example imports one small helper. It builds the URL, adds your key and turns API errors into readable messages:

Run any example with node --env-file=.env followed by the file name.

YouTube doesn't tell you how long a playlist is. To work it out, you need two methods:

Durations come back in ISO 8601 format. PT15M33S is 15 minutes 33 seconds, PT1H2M3S adds an hour, and a stream longer than a day looks like P1DT2H3M4S. The toSeconds() function below handles all three.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Full Courses in One Video playlist:

That's 184 hours of courses for 4 units of quota. Private and deleted videos still show up in a playlist's items, but videos.list doesn't return them, which is why the script prints "52 of 52 videos available". On a playlist with removed videos, the two numbers will differ.

This is the core of the free YouTube playlist length calculator I built on createthat.ai. The live version adds a target time box that works out the speed you need to finish by a deadline, and it accepts channel, video and Shorts links as well as playlists.

The obvious way to find a channel's videos is to search for them. Don't: search is now capped at 100 calls a day, and it isn't built to list everything a channel has posted. Every channel has an uploads playlist instead, and you can reach it in two cheap calls:

Two units, and none of your 100 daily searches. To get every upload rather than the newest five, page through the playlist with the same pageToken loop as Example 1.

videos.list with part=statistics returns a video's view, like and comment counts. Dislike counts have been private since December 2021 and favoriteCount is always 0, so don't build anything on those. The script also copes with a missing like or comment count, which you'll see on some videos.

This time the fields parameter trims the response to the values we use. That makes the response smaller, but it doesn't change the cost: the call is 1 unit either way.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Learn Python full course, one of the most-watched videos in that playlist:

Treat the last line as a rough range, not an answer. The Data API doesn't share anyone's revenue, so any estimate is views multiplied by an RPM: what a creator keeps per 1,000 views. RPM moves a lot with the topic and where the viewers live, and the estimate assumes the video shows ads, which not every channel does.

And sometimes you don't need an API at all. When I built a font tester, I needed a list of Google Fonts. The Google Fonts Developer API would have meant a key and a call on every visit, for a list that barely changes. Instead I generated a file of the 500 most popular fonts once, from Google's public font metadata, and the page loads it like any other file. No key, no quota and nothing to fail.

When a call fails, the response's error.message and error.details[0].reason tell you why. These are the ones you'll see most:

For the invalid key error, the full response looks like this:

Yes. Creating a key and calling the YouTube Data API costs nothing. You're limited by the daily quota instead, and there's no paid tier: more quota comes through an audit.

In the Google Cloud console, select your project and go to APIs & Services > Credentials. Your keys are listed under API keys, with a Show key option next to each one.

It works, but anyone can read the key from your page. If you do it, restrict the key to your website and to the YouTube Data API v3. A small server route that calls the API for you is safer, and it gives you somewhere to cache results.

By default, 100 searches, 100 uploads and 10,000 units for everything else. Most reads cost 1 unit and videos.list takes 50 IDs per call, so that's enough for the details of up to 500,000 videos a day.

Getting a YouTube API key is the easy part: one project, one API to enable, one key and two restrictions. The 2026 quota is generous if you work with it:

All three examples together cost 7 units, out of 10,000. From here, try commentThreads.list to pull a video's comments 100 at a time, or extend Example 2 to page through a channel's entire upload history and add it all up with the code from Example 1.
