#10437. This new blood test claims to detect over 50 cancers before onset of symptoms

Cancer isn’t the leading cause of death in the United States; heart disease ranks first in that regard. But it’s cancer that strikes fear into the hearts of many Americans.

Cancer can take an excruciating toll on its millions of victims and their family members. Cancer treatments are often highly toxic, and the disease is increasingly striking people at younger ages, which is why it accounts for more years of potential life lost than heart disease.

So when a blood test comes along that claims to be able to screen for over 50 cancers at once in a single blood draw before symptoms appear—as is the case for the Galleri test, by the biotech company GRAIL—it might seem like society has a Holy Grail of modern medicine at their fingertips.

But a closer look at the evidence suggests that the Galleri test is not yet the universal screening breakthrough patients and doctors hope for.

As a physician who studies early cancer detection, and as chair of the American Cancer Society’s screening guideline development group, I have been following this test’s development with interest.

While the Galleri test certainly shows great promise, clinical trial data indicates that it struggles with early-stage cancer detection and has not yet been shown to lower the risk of death from cancer or prevent advanced cancer. Across all cancer stages, the test missed over two-thirds of cancer cases.

On Sept. 23, 2026, an advisory panel to the U.S. Food and Drug Administration voted to recommend approval of the Galleri test. While a recommendation from the FDA’s advisory panel is not the same as full approval from the agency, it marks a critical first step in the process of potentially making the test widely available to the American public. The FDA has not yet announced when it will make a decision on the test.

But even as the advisory panel approved the test, panelists recommended that the word “early” be removed from “early cancer detection test” in product labeling. Panel members were not convinced the test can find cancers at their earliest and most curable stages, and they expressed uncertainty about the test’s benefit to patients at this time.

Given this uncertainty, careful scrutiny of the data behind the test can help doctors and patients make informed decisions about whether to use it.

What is the Galleri test? How does it work?

Galleri is part of an emerging class of multicancer early detection tests. These tests do not diagnose cancer: If a test is positive, further evaluation is necessary to determine whether cancer is present, and, if so, what type of cancer. It is offered as a screening test, similar to mammography for breast cancer screening or PSA testing for prostate cancer screening, but with the potential advantage of screening for many cancers at once, including many for which no screening test currently exists.

The Galleri test looks for DNA fragments in blood that are associated with cancer. When something goes wrong with your DNA, whether because you inherited a mutation or something in your environment triggered that mutation, the processes that regulate cell division can go awry. Uncontrolled cell division can trigger the growth of cancer cells.

Galleri and other multicancer early detection tests in development look for differences that distinguish the DNA produced by tumor cells from normal cells. Galleri identifies unique tags on cancer DNA called methylation markers and uses machine learning algorithms to generate a positive or negative test result.

What are the strengths of the Galleri test?

The Galleri test looks for DNA signals from over 50 cancers, including the 12 that account for almost two-thirds of cancer deaths in the U.S, such as lung and pancreas cancer. Importantly, it provides information on which organ the possible tumor likely originated from. Currently recommended cancer screenings are largely limited to four types of cancer.

Many of the cancers the Galleri test looks for are not currently included in routine screening. Moreover, the test has a very low false positive rate: Only about 0.5% of people who get the test are incorrectly identified as having cancer, or one person out of every 200 tests. A false positive rate is the percentage of people who are told they have cancer but actually don’t. This requires that patients get more testing, which can be costly, sometimes risky and often worrisome.

In comparison, many currently recommended screening tests, such as the Cologuard stool test for colorectal cancer, have false positive rates in the 10% range.

This very low false positive rate also translates into another strength – a very high positive predictive value, or PPV, which is the probability that a positive test result actually means cancer is present. The PPV of the Galleri test was 52% across three annual screens, meaning over half the positive results actually led to a cancer diagnosis. This is good because it gives people confidence that the test is more than 50% correct.

For context, the PPVs of most recommended screening tests—such as stool tests for colorectal cancer, mammography for breast cancer, or the HPV test for cervical cancer—are typically below 10%, meaning most positive results don’t lead to a cancer diagnosis.

The Galleri test also can predict with high accuracy where a cancer originated in the body. In one study of 32,000 U.S. adults age 50 and older who received a Galleri blood test, cancer was diagnosed in about 0.5% of them—173 patients. Of these patients, the test correctly predicted the cancer type in 91%. This specificity could help clinicians select more targeted evaluations rather than looking for cancer anywhere in the body.

What are the limitations of the Galleri test?

Despite these impressive results, the Galleri test has several major shortcomings.

For one, the test is not as good at detecting cancer in its earliest stages as currently recommended screening tests, which is the main point of a cancer screening test. The test’s ability to detect cancer at all stages was less than 31% over three annual rounds of screening, meaning the test missed 69% of cases. Of the cancers detected in the study, comparing outcomes from getting the Galleri test three years in a row to routine cancer screening, 60% of the cancers detected in the people who got the Galleri test were early stage, which was almost identical to those who didn’t get it.

In addition, the Galleri test did not perform any better than standard screening tests at detecting cancer early. The detection rate for early-stage cancers was only 21% over three years of screening, meaning almost 80% were missed. Cancer is most curable in its early stages, so this shortcoming is a major one. Such a low detection rate can lead to false reassurance from an incorrectly negative test result.

Perhaps the most important shortcoming of the Galleri test is that it hasn’t yet proved that it can save lives or even reduce the risk of being diagnosed with late-stage cancer. The main goal of the trial was to show that the test could reduce the incidence of late-stage cancers over three years of screening—and it failed to do so. In other words, one of the hopes was that the test could reduce the risk of being diagnosed with advanced cancer by finding and treating cancer early—but as of now, there is no evidence to support this.

There was a hint that the incidence of Stage 4 cancer was trending lower by the third year of screening. But more time is needed to determine whether this is a true finding and, if so, whether it leads to a lower risk of dying from cancer.

Requests for comment from GRAIL were not returned.

What did the FDA’s panel recommend?

The FDA advisory panel took three votes. While one of of these votes followed the evidence, two of them were surprisingly counterintuitive.

First, the panel voted 10-0 to recommend that the Galleri test is safe. While undergoing a blood test doesn’t typically involve any substantive risk, a positive result may prompt further testing, such as biopsies, that are riskier than a blood test and can incur additional costs.

These harms can be particularly hard to swallow if the test turns out to be a false alarm, as it will in about half of cases. One study evaluating Galleri’s safety found that 0.6% of those who had the test ended up undergoing an invasive diagnostic procedure, but none experienced serious complications. Hence, the safety vote appears to have been warranted.

Second, the panel voted 6-4 to recommend that the test is effective, though a number of the panelists cast their yes vote on the condition that the company remove the term “early” from the label “multi-cancer early detection” test.

This outcome, however, does not have the evidence to back it up. Available studies indicate the Galleri test does not reduce the incidence of late-stage cancer and performs poorly in detecting early-stage cancer. Additionally, there is no evidence that it lowers the risk of dying from cancer.

Finally, the panel voted 7-2 with one abstention to recommend that the benefits of the Galleri test outweigh the risk. But if there is no proven benefit to the test, how can the benefits outweigh the risks, even if the risk is low?

While the advisory panel acknowledged the test’s serious limitations, it still went ahead and recommended approval. One of the panel members stated that the ability to find more early-stage cancers in a broader population is a benefit, even though that hasn’t been proved yet. “So perhaps my ‘yes’ is a hopeful (vote) rather than an accurate one,” said the panel member.

The problem is that “hope” does not reduce death and disability from cancer.

Should you get the Galleri test or not?

If the FDA agrees with its advisory panel’s recommendation and approves the Galleri test, it will likely be more widely marketed to the American public. Regardless, there remain many caveats, and I believe patients will want to be cautious.

First, FDA approval does not guarantee insurance coverage. The Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services has a separate coverage determination process, and it is not required to grant coverage to FDA-approved tests. The out-of-pocket cost of the Galleri test is currently US$949.

It remains unclear whether the test can reduce your risk of dying from cancer or being diagnosed with advanced cancer. However, if you’re willing to pay the annual cost of the test, are willing to undergo further testing despite a high rate of false alarms, and are not overly reassured by a falsely negative test result, you can ask your doctor for more information about the test.

What doctors and researchers are certain about is the importance of staying up to date with currently recommended cancer screening tests for colon, breast, cervical and lung cancers, even if you decide to get the Galleri test. These tests are proven to detect early-stage cancer and lower your risk of dying from cancer. That’s the bottom line.

Andrew Wolf is a professor emeritus of medicine at the University of Virginia.

#10436. Google Messages homepage redesign: Pinned chat avatars, bottom search, corner Gemini

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages for Android is testing a big homepage redesign that has three core components.

Update 11/7: Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Hate the idea of just the pinned "bobble head" look. I like being able to view message tidbits. Plus, it's directly copying Apple (enough said). I have to use an iPhone at my job. I hate the pinned bubble conversation look and more importantly it's function.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive, while boosting one-handed usability.

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image. This matches the design leveraged by Workspace apps on desktop and mobile.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to all testers. Some users have all aspects of the redesign, but it appears that Google is rolling out the various components in a piecemeal fashion.

#10434. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s involvement with an alleged Chinese spy is the stuff of Hollywood thrillers. These 10, for example

The billionaire CEO of one of the world’s most successful companies is drawn into an affair with an alluring, jet-setting businesswoman. He becomes closer and shares more—only to discover that she’s secretly a Chinese spy. It sounds like the plot of a Hollywood blockbuster. But a version of that plotline is allegedly unfolding for real inside Nvidia, the world’s second most valuable company. 

CEO Jensen Huang has admitted to an affair, reportedly with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed who is accused of working with the Chinese government. Nvidia’s board says it’s still investigating the allegations, and that it has no reason to believe trade secrets were compromised. 

Hollywood, meanwhile, has been exploring the “honey trap” plot for nearly a century. Here are 10 notable examples.

1. The Americans (2013-2018)

FX’s critically acclaimed spy drama offers a multi-layered portrayal of the long-term honey trap. Keri Russell and Matthew Rhys star as Elizabeth and Philip Jennings, Soviet spies infiltrating America during the Cold War. Most honey trap plots portray a female spy as the seducer. The Americans flips the script—with devastating results.

2. Red Sparrow (2018)

Jennifer Lawrence plays Dominika Egorova, a Russian ballerina recruited into a secret intelligence program. Pursuing a CIA operative and carefully controlled by her Russian handlers, Egorova discovers that the manipulation of a honey trap agent can go both ways.

3. Lust, Caution (2007)

The honey trap is a staple of Western spy films. But the same basic plotline cuts across cultures. In this film from Ang Lee, Tang Wei portrays a young woman who is recruited by a resistance organization during the Japanese occupation of China. Her mission is to arrange for her target’s assassination, but feelings begin to get in the way. 

4. Notorious (1946)

Alfred Hitchcock’s 1946 film is proof that the honey trap has fascinated screenwriters and directors for generations. With a legendary cast, including Ingrid Bergman and Cary Grant, Notorious explores what happens when a honey trap becomes a love triangle.

5. The Little Drummer Girl (2018)

Although Hollywood has provided especially fertile ground to depict honey traps on the screen, the idea of a honey trap is a staple of the spy novel, too. Case in point: John le Carré’s espionage thriller, which was adapted for the screen as The Little Drummer Girl. The miniseries depicts a young recruit of the Israeli intelligence forces tasked with infiltrating a Palestinian militant organization, blurring the lines between fake and real identities.

6. Black Book (2006)

This Paul Verhoeven film is set in the Nazi-occupied Netherlands during World War II. Tasked with infiltrating German intelligence circles, Jewish operative Rachel Stein (Carice van Houten) begins to question the real loyalties of those around her.

7. The Handmaiden (2016)

Most honey traps involve spies and the clandestine world of espionage. But some explore the same general idea in less secretive places. South Korean filmmaker Park Chan-Wook’s 2016 film depicts a young pickpocket who is recruited to help a con artist seduce a wealthy heiress. It quickly becomes unclear, though, who is manipulating whom.

8. M. Butterfly (1993)

Much of what happens in the top secret world of espionage remains exactly that: top secret. But occasionally real-life spy dramas are declassified. When that happens, they often make great material for a Hollywood story. That’s the case with this film about a diplomat who is seduced by a Chinese opera singer. Inspired by real events, the film shows how even a high-level figure can be drawn into a honey trap. 

9. From Russia With Love (1963)

James Bond may be able to skydive into a falling airplane or windsurf a tsunami, but even he can fall prey to the honey trap. The classic Bond film From Russia With Love centers around Soviet clerk Tatiana Romanova, who appears willing to defect—and draws Bond into a romantic entanglement. But as is so often the case in Bond’s world, things get “shaken” up, as the truth about Romanova comes out.

10. Ex Machina (2014)

Not every honey trap operative needs to be human. In Alex Garland’s classic sci-fi film, a young programmer, Caleb, is tasked with evaluating an advanced artificial intelligence system named Ava. As man and machine become closer, the film questions whether their attraction is real—or whether Ava is secretly manipulating Caleb to achieve her own aim. Given Nvidia’s role as the shovel seller of the AI gold rush, the idea of a honey-trapping AI feels especially apt.

#10433. Android tablets are back, but are they for you?

Android tablets have always been a bit of a touchy subject, but it’s abundantly clear that the form factor is making a comeback. But, as someone who’s a fan of the smaller sizes, are you actually using the gargantuan Android tablets that every company seems obsessed with? In other words – are you the customer for the Android tablets these brands are selling?

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

For a while there, Android tablets had hit what felt like a dead end. Bigger smartphones and cooling of the tablet market as a whole, along with the general sentiment that Apple was doing a way better job with the iPad anyway, left the category in a place that felt stagnant – and that’s the nice way of putting it.

But, over the past few years, Android tablets have had a renaissance.

New form factors, big releases from a variety of brands, and flagship-tier devices left and right. Despite Android’s push into foldables and still-huge displays on nearly every smartphone, tablets are very much back. Prior to RAMageddon’s worst impacts, tablet sales were growing for nearly everyone, and Android brands were seeing a larger slice of the pie.

Anyway, this is on my mind right now because I’ve had a lot of Android tablets arrive in my office in the past few weeks, capping off a lot of tablet announcements in the past few months. I just finished up with Redmagic Astra 2, a tablet I think fits the description of “Android’s iPad Mini OLED,” and I’m in the process of moving into tablets from Lenovo and Amazon. Our Jordan Floyd has also been testing out the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra behind the scenes. And if there’s one trend amongst recent tablet releases, it’s that big tablets seem to be the primary focus among a lot of brands.

Take Amazon’s new Google Play-equipped Alexa Tablet lineup, for example.

The Alexa Tablet 12 is the crown jewel of the lineup, packing the most powerful specs and a premium anti-glare display. It’s the only one that gets a “Pro” moniker, while the 11-inch and 8-inch models are noticeable steps down. Unboxing the Alexa Tablet 8 this week, for example, I was immediately reminded this is not a premium device – the screen makes that clear without any hesitation whatsoever. It was a case of whiplash after setting up the Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 with its 4K display.

It’s a weird one for me. I’ve never been a big fan of these super-sized Android tablets (or iPads, for that matter). I can get behind a 10-inch or 11-inch model, but I find myself more at home on the smaller slates. They’re fantastic for travel as an alternative to draining my phone’s battery, while I find myself just reaching for a laptop instead if I want a bigger screen. A big tablet, regardless of specs, can rarely replace a proper laptop for me, so lugging both around just sounds like more trouble than it’s worth.

But, clearly, people are buying these bigger slates. Samsung appears to be seeing enough success with its mammoth Galaxy Tab S Ultra lineup to keep the 14.6-inch tablet around each year while the smaller sizes are now on an every-other-year cycle. Lenovo’s lineup is virtually all underpowered when the screen size goes down, aside from the Legion Tab that’s now $900.

Are you buying these bigger tablets? What are you using them for?

I imagine that most folks opting for a bigger tablet are using these in the place of a laptop. Thinner and lighter, longer battery life, etc. And in cases like the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro and its $550~ price tag, I get the appeal. Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 takes that down a notch for me at $799 – you’re getting into laptop territory there – but it still is a compelling-enough alternative if it fits your needs. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra at $1,400 plus a $200 keyboard, yeah I don’t get that one at all.

Let’s discuss! I want to know if you’re using an Android tablet, what you’re using it for, and what size you actually prefer.

Ladies and gents, we’ve finally got a new flagship in the US. Motorola Signature 27 is launching on November 19 and looks excellent, though its flashy spec sheet won’t come cheap at $1,599.

Finally, something good came out of Googlebook

Googlebook is a disaster, but there’s finally one good thing coming out of it in Henry Cavill’s new “Mustache Protocol” game which, supposedly, was built on Googlebook. I’ll bite my tongue there, but the accompanying video brought a needed bit of joy into my week.

I use an 8" Samsung. Not the best in specs, but fine for browsing and reading books. I had a Nexus 10 years ago and gave it to my sister-in-law as I rarely needed something that big. My wife also uses an 8" Samsung, mostly for reading.

#10431. Cyanogen co-founder unveils Kora, a Linux OS that splits AI models across the CPU, GPU, and NPU

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Intel and AMD NPU support is planned, but founder says neither will be used for language models.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A team led by Cyanogen co-founder Kirt McMaster has unveiled Kora, a new Linux desktop OS with integrated AI models and agents that can split workloads across CPU, GPU, and NPU resources, as well as other machines on the same local network. We reached out to the firm for more info.

Kora, the first product from Kora Systems Inc., previously Playtron, carries the tagline “The Operating System for Intelligence” and has its own Cortex “hybrid inference engine” technology, which is not open source and is optimized for local models. The OS, built “from the kernel to the shell,” runs local models by default and cloud models when needed, and is expected to arrive on PCs in 2027.

The desktop follows the AI-focused motif with “neural workspaces” and a “neural file system,” which refer to sandboxed workspaces and files indexed by semantic meaning. Kora has three operating modes: “Work," "Create," and "Play." That last base is covered by GameFrame, which descends from Playtron’s GameOS. Humain OS, which Kora is developing with Saudi AI company Humain, is the enterprise variant of this OS.

Currently, the only NPU named in the release material is the Hexagon NPU found in the Snapdragon X2 Elite, which Qualcomm pegs at 80 TOPS. On the X2 Elite, Cortex can schedule AI workloads across the CPU, GPU, and NPU, as well as other systems on the local network, which could be a novel capability if it works beyond the Qualcomm hardware.

McMaster told Tom’s Hardware that the Snapdragon X2 Elite NPU runs Google's Gemma 4 26B model at 25 tokens per second for decode. Intel and AMD NPU support is in the works, but according to the developer, neither NPU will be used for LLMs, as GPUs are “just better for LLM workloads on x64.”

Each NPU vendor has its own software stack, or runtime, and prefers that models are compiled in its own format: Qualcomm with QAIRT/QNN, Intel with OpenVINO, and AMD with Ryzen AI software. The press kit doesn't identify support for any Intel or AMD chip, NPU runtime, model format, quantization, or speed figures for those platforms.

Our earlier Windows testing showed the same kind of divide: Snapdragon reached its NPU through the QNN provider under ONNX Runtime, while Intel’s work ran through IPEX, its extension for PyTorch, on the GPU because it was faster than the NPU.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

When asked about cross-vendor support, McMaster reiterated that “only Hexagon on Snapdragon X2 Elite is supported today.” Intel NPU support is planned, with “conversations underway,” though McMaster later clarified that neither Intel’s nor AMD’s NPU will be used for LLMs. On AMD’s Ryzen AI chips, Kora will likely use the NPU for embedding and speech-recognition models through ONNX.As for what drives the Qualcomm Hexagon NPU, McMaster said Kora uses the “llama.cpp Hexagon backend” and “the upstream FastRPC driver.” That suggests the operating system reaches the NPU through the open-source llama.cpp project, not Qualcomm’s own QAIRT/QNN runtime, by way of the FastRPC driver in the mainline kernel.

Language models; KORA cites Gemma 4 26B at 25 tokens per second

Likely embedding and speech-recognition models; not language models

When asked about model formats, McMaster said, “we predominantly support GGUF,” llama.cpp’s model file format, and that Lemma, the inference engine inside Cortex, “can split model layers across processors as needed.” Lemma is built on llama.cpp, whisper.cpp, stable-diffusion.cpp, and ONNX. McMaster says that which runtime is used depends on the model's requirements, and more runtimes will be added down the line.

For today, llama.cpp has a Hexagon backend for Snapdragon and an OpenVINO backend for Intel CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, although the latter is still marked “In Progress,” and no AMD NPU backend, which would explain why AMD goes through ONNX. AMD NPUs can run LLMs on Linux through the separate Lemonade 10.0 stack with FastFlowLM, as reported by Phoronix in March, though that isn’t the route Kora has chosen.

Beyond NPU stacks, those hoping to run Kora will need to be mindful of their system's available memory. The Gemma 4 26B model Kora uses, which Google lists as Gemma 4 26B A4B, needs about 14.4GB of memory to load its weights at 4-bit (Q4_0) quantization, by Google’s estimate, before any context window, while third-party listings put the download for the Q4_K_M file Kora uses at about 16–17GB. The model only activates about 4 billion of its 26 billion parameters per token but still needs to be fully loaded into memory.

McMaster said that with the model, the X2 Elite reaches “25 TPS decode throughput and 550 TPS prefill throughput,” or tokens per second, running “entirely on the Hexagon NPU with 32K context.” A dense model, rather than a mixture-of-experts, would be far slower. Not given are the RAM configuration or the specific X2 Elite chip.

On memory, McMaster said that “we recommend 64GB because it gives you access to better models,” but that is not a firm requirement. The company projects that “about 70 percent of the model calls it manages will run locally in 2027.”

Kora says its enterprise-focused Humain OS is due first and will be “commercially available in 2027,” on OEM PCs using the Snapdragon X2 Elite. Kora will follow "later in 2027.”

McMaster says that “Kora will be posted on our site and downloadable for anyone to install.” Included will be a list of recommended configurations, “but it should be installable on most modern PCs.” Exact pricing and the overall business model will be shared closer to the date of availability.Edit: 11/11/2026 9:06am PT: Added further follow-up details provided by the maker.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

#10430. Prada’s new exhibition in Milan is projected to make over $100 million in annual revenue

Iconic A-line skirts twirl overhead, silver-covered handbags gleam in a secret underground passage and a spacesuit prototype brings more than a century of fashion history into the future at Prada’s new permanent exhibition in Milan, called “Pradasphere.”

The highly visual journey through the Italian luxury brand’s evolution is connecting Prada aficionados with the fashion house’s heritage in ways that go beyond nostalgia, and already is promising a commercial payoff.

“The exhibition is a multiplier,” said Lorenzo Bertelli, Prada Group’s chief group marketing officer.

Prada projects that the Pradasphere exhibition anchoring the brand’s expansion in the Galleria Vittorio Emanuele shopping arcade will add 100 million euros ($111 million) in annual revenues, and Bertelli said that target could be surpassed.
Pradasphere is fully booked into the new year and Bertelli said visitors have shown a tendency to spend more after immersing themselves in the brand’s history, sometimes going back to make an additional purchase after walking through the eight Pradasphere spaces.

“We see that the customers who go through the museum have a completely different perspective of the brand and also they browse the store with completely different minds,” said Bertelli, who has been identified as the future leader of the Prada Group. The 38-year-old, whose roles include executive chairman of Versace, is the son of creative director Miuccia Prada and Prada Group chairman Patrizio Bertelli.

Longtime Prada lovers will know that Mario and Martino Prada founded Fratelli Prada in 1913 in the historic Galleria. But they may never have seen in person the luxury bags and accessories, including a pleated evening bag with a devilish head-shaped clasp, its tongue sticking out, a detail that appeared on the Spring-Summer 2024 runway.

And it may be news that the brothers designed the brand’s signature inverted triangle logo.

Miuccia Prada’s third-generation entrance into the family business is announced with trademark nylon bags that she began designing in the 1970s, embodying the Prada high-low, industrial-luxury paradox, and a chocolate brown opera coat from her first runway collection presented in 1988.

The exhibition contains many runway hits, including the 2012 Flame heels and the Formica tile prints that gave birth to Prada’s “ugly chic” reputation, while also highlighting Miuccia Prada’s and the senior Bertelli’s reach into art, architecture, film and sport.

“Curiosity and having fun is what drives my parents,” the younger Bertelli said, adding that experimentation doesn’t always bring immediate financial success, “but for sure it drives the enrichment of our story, and our culture and our knowledge.”

He cited as an example investments in sports and engineering made with the formation of the Luna Rossa America’s Cup sailing team in the 1990s, which helped develop the kind of materials expertise that led Prada to collaborate with Axiom Space on a space suit, a prototype of which is displayed at the end of the exhibition.

Prada helped design the suit’s outer layer, which is engineered to withstand extreme thermal conditions, as well as the high-performance inner layer, with a focus on thermal regulation and comfort. It is intended for astronauts walking on the moon on NASA’s Artemis IV mission, targeted for 2028.

Just 20 visitors are admitted to Pradasphere by appointment each hour to explore the compact eight rooms of the exhibition, which Bertelli promises will be in constant evolution.

Prada’s presence in the Galleria now spans eight floors, encompassing the existing Marchesi 1824 pastry shop and Osservatorio Fondazione Prada contemporary art space, alongside the new exhibition, private salon and apartment.

Most visitors end their journey in a secret underground passage connecting the menswear and womenswear boutiques, the latter occupying the original Fratelli Prada premises.

The passage contains “lesser-known” chapters from the brand’s history, including a chandelier dress created for Baz Luhrmann’s “The Great Gatsby.”

“It was a great experience,” said Luca Missio, an architect from the northeastern Italian city of Udine. “I recognized the stores I’ve visited around the world, in Tokyo and New York, in the models and displays. That was lovely.”

—Colleen Barry, AP Fashion Writer

#10428. Measles is back. This tool calculates the exact cost to our economy

Measles is making a comeback, and the outbreak isn’t just costing Americans their health. The surge is also costing the U.S. economy millions.

In 2025, the United States experienced the largest number of measles cases since its elimination in 2000. Falling vaccination rates fueled the surge, which has roared back with 5,182 cases reported in 2026 alone as of early November, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

For some context, a total of 2,289 cases were confirmed in 2025—less than half of what we are seeing so far this year. Hardest hit is the state of Pennsylvania, where six people have died. In addition, one person has died in New York and another in Wisconsin. The CDC’s most recent map shows measles cases in almost all U.S. states.

The costs could continue to rise as the epidemic gets worse, according to a new tool from Johns Hopkins University, which calculated the cost for the outbreak in 2025 at more than $37 million. Overall, the tool estimates the measles outbreak has cost nearly $83 million, including $56 million for the public health response, $25 million in lost productivity, and more than $1.8 million in medical expenses.

That analysis looked at measles outbreaks across 18 states, and found it costs about $250,000 initially to respond to each outbreak, regardless of its size, plus roughly $16,300 per additional case. 

“Whether it’s one case or 20 cases, those initial mobilization costs are incurred,” measles study coauthor Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center (IVAC) at JHU in Baltimore, said in a release. “As additional cases are identified, additional resources are needed, but it is really that initial response effort driving the high price tag of even small outbreaks.”

On top of that, the U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status, which is being reviewed this week by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO).

What to know about measles

Measles is a very contagious disease caused by a virus, per a fact sheet from the CDC.

It can be dangerous, especially for babies and young children, and spreads through the air when a sick person coughs or sneezes. The virus can stay in the air for two hours after a person with measles leaves the space.

It is so contagious that about 9 out of 10 people who come near a person with measles and are not protected by vaccination will also become infected.

#10424. This viral map ranks Taco Bell locations by cheapest price—and one stands out above the rest

Not all Taco Bells are created equal.

At the 87 Taco Bell locations throughout New York City, menu items can sometimes double in price from franchisee to franchisee. That’s why an influencer named Yuval set out to compare the prices of every single Taco Bell location in the city, charting the cost of individual menu items as well as the average price point for a given store.

Yuval presented his results in a viral TikTok that shows New Yorkers how to live más for menos.

The Bell of the ball

To find the cheapest Taco Bell in NYC, Yuval analyzed the costs of best-selling menu items, then ranked the locations based on how their prices compare with other Taco Bells on average.

According to Yuval’s map, one location stands out above the rest for cheap eats: the Jackson Heights Taco Bell. That one restaurant is home to the cheapest Crunchwrap Supreme ($5.19), Beefy 5-Layer Burrito ($4.09), Chalupa Supreme ($4.49), Cheesy Gordita Crunch ($4.99), Chicken Quesadilla ($5.69), and Nachos BellGrande ($5.89) in the city.

But Jackson Heights is outranked on a few best-selling menu items. There’s the Build Your Own Luxe Cravings Box, which goes for $8 in Jackson Heights, compared with the five Taco Bells on Staten Island that offer it for $7.29.

There’s also the Cheesy Bean and Rice Burrito, which costs just $1.49 at Taco Bells in midtown Manhattan, Hell’s Kitchen, Flushing, and Corona. Flushing and Corona are also home to the cheapest Crunchy and Soft Tacos in the city at $2.19 each.

For the cheapest possible Spicy Potato Soft Taco, you’ll have to head to Chelsea, where it costs just $1.29.

When it comes to dessert, several Manhattan locations offer Cinnamon Twists for only a dollar. That includes three Taco Bells on the Upper East Side, two in the East Village, one in Greenwich Village, and one on the Upper West Side—locations that, strangely enough, rank among the most expensive for other menu items, including a $14.99 price point for the Luxe Cravings Box, more than double the cheapest rate elsewhere in the city.

If you don’t live in Queens and don’t want to make the trek to Jackson Heights, these are the cheapest locations on average in every other borough, according to the map:

  • Manhattan: Inwood and Hell’s Kitchen 
  • Brooklyn: Bedford-Stuyvesant and Bushwick
  • The Bronx: Fordham, University Heights, Mount Hope, and Concourse
  • Staten Island: New Dorp and New Springville

Not in NYC? Never fear: Taco Bell enthusiasts outside of the city can get a sense of relative prices by checking out taconomical.com, a heat map comparing Taco Bell prices across America—though it hasn’t been updated since 2023.

Why the price differences?

Amid thanking Yuval for his boots-on-the-ground journalism, many folks in his comments section were dumbfounded to learn that prices could vary so widely within a single fast-food chain. “America is not real 😭,” reads one comment. “[What do you mean] they allow them to change prices, let alone in the same city.”

“The prices at a franchise should be the same at least across an entire country for the same item. This is ridiculous,” reads another.

For its part, Taco Bell keeps its commentary to a minimum. While Taco Bell didn’t respond to Fast Company’s request for comment, it does address the question “Why is pricing different at some Taco Bell locations?” on the brand’s FAQ page, albeit briefly. The answer simply reads: “Price and participation vary,” then encourages customers to reach out to their local restaurants for specifics.

#10421. ‘I’ve been a Democrat my whole life’: Alex Karp suddenly wants you to know that Palantir is progressive

It was only a matter of time until tech CEOs rethought their alliances with the Trump administration and pivoted back to the left now that the blue wave has swept the midterm elections.

But while many had their eyes on larger tech figures closer to the president, like Meta’s Mark Zuckerberg or on-again-off-again presidential ally Elon Musk, Palantir CEO Alex Karp beat them to the punch.

In a sit-down interview with Politico, Karp soft-launched his avowed support for the Democrats and voiced concern over the current administration’s policies.

“I’ve been a Democrat my whole life. I grew up in a far-left environment. I know more about being a progressive than almost anybody on the planet, and I absolutely believe Palantir—all of our products are essentially progressive,” Karp told Politico.

While in the past, Karp has focused on supporting the military industrial complex through technology, he turned his focus onto economic injustice, suggesting Democrats have an advantage over Republicans when it comes to developing a solution.

“I’m really not pitching Palantir,” Karp said. “I’m actually pitching, ‘What are you going do about economic justice in this country?’ For many Republicans, there’s an orthodoxy that capitalism solves all problems. Maybe that was once true. I never thought it was true. It’s definitely not true now.

Past controversies, lucrative Trump-friendly contracts

The statement has undoubtedly raised some eyebrows on social media, particularly as the technology company—which Karp once referred to as “anti-woke“—has gained heat in the past years for its lucrative contracts with the Trump administration.

Perhaps Palantir’s most notable—and controversial—contract is the $30 million deal for ImmigrationOS, the software it developed to help U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) track and deport undocumented immigrants. Karp addressed the controversy during the interview, commenting on Trump’s approach to immigration enforcement.

“I was really happy to hear the president be honest that—you know, his words, I believe, were something like, ‘Yeah, we kinda went too far,’” Karp continued. “And I’m glad he’s saying in public now what I’ve been saying in private.”

Karp’s donation history is actually a mixed bag. This year, he donated $100,000 to Americans for Security PAC, a conservative political action committee, according to OpenSecrets, a nonpartisan nonprofit that tracks the flow of money in American politics. But his individual candidate donations show that he has financially supported Republican Rep. Jay Obernolte of California, as well as Democratic Sen. Mark Warner of Virginia and the Democratic Party of Virginia.

“My giving is equal Democrat, Republican,” Karp said in the interview. “I can tell you, without going into things I’m not gonna disclose, I’m very balanced, and everyone in the political arena knows it. And I’ll tell you what—I’m also not the village idiot.”

‘Pure survival grift’

Still, social media users aren’t quite buying it.

Some have taken the interview’s revelations as fodder for memes, using AI to transform Karp into a woke figure with pink-colored hair, piercings, and pronouns in sight.

But for many others, the contradictory messaging reads as opportunistic.

“Karp spent years selling surveillance and control tools to the regime, then flips the second the midterms hit. Pure survival grift,” a user said on X.

Another added: “The back peddling gonna be legendary with every passing week that Trump gets even less popular.”

#10420. Top 6 AI Image Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer who once spent an afternoon searching stock libraries can now describe the picture they want and have four versions in under a minute. AI image generators have moved from curiosity to everyday tool for blog headers, product mockups, ad concepts, game art and slide decks. The quality gap between tools has narrowed, but the differences that remain matter a lot: how well each follows a detailed prompt, whether it can render readable text, how it handles edits, and what you are allowed to do with the result.

This guide compares six of the best-known options, what each does well and where each falls short. Models, plans and limits change often, so check each vendor's own pages before you commit to a workflow.

OpenArt’s AI image generator puts several image models behind one interface. Its generator page lists models including GPT Image 2.5, Grok Imagine, Qwen Image 3, Nano Banana 2 and Seedream 5.0 Pro, and lets you create images from text prompts or reference photos. It also lists tools for camera angle control, upscaling, collages and a Character Builder for keeping characters consistent across scenes, with 4K export.

Best for: Creators who want to compare several models on the same prompt without juggling separate subscriptions and accounts.

Limitations: The page offers a free start but does not state credit amounts or plan details, and it does not address commercial use, so check the pricing and licensing pages before using results for client work. Output quality depends on which model you pick.

OpenAI's image generation in ChatGPT works inside the chat interface, which makes iteration natural: generate an image, then ask for changes in plain language. OpenAI highlights accurate text rendering, the ability to follow prompts with many distinct objects, and consistent characters across multi-turn edits. You can also upload reference images and have the model transform them. It handles both photorealistic and stylized looks.

Best for: Marketers and writers who want to describe, refine and re-describe an image without learning a new tool.

Limitations: OpenAI notes that tall images such as posters can be cropped too tightly, and that dense small text, multilingual text and precise graphs can still go wrong. Images can take up to a minute to render. All generated images carry C2PA metadata identifying their source.

Gemini's image generation runs on Google's Nano Banana models. Google highlights character consistency, text rendering in many languages, combining several photos, precise editing and 2K resolution. A free tier covers image generation and editing, while the most advanced model requires a Google AI plan. You start from the "Create images" option in the Gemini app, then prompt or upload a picture to edit.

Best for: People who need to keep the same subject across several images, or who want to edit existing photos quickly.

Limitations: Usage limits apply, and the strongest model is behind a paid plan. Images include an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible AI label, which is good for transparency but may not suit every use.

Midjourney is an independent research lab known for images with a strong, painterly look. Its official site points users to a sign-up flow and to a Discord community and help center for support. Many creators choose it for concept art, mood boards and illustration where style matters more than literal accuracy.

Best for: Artists, art directors and game designers who want rich, stylized results and enjoy exploring variations.

Limitations: I could not confirm current pricing, trial terms or feature details from Midjourney's public pages, so check them yourself before subscribing. The workflow can feel less like a normal app than the others on this list, and it is not the easiest route for precise, text-heavy graphics.

Adobe Firefly is built for professionals who already work in Creative Cloud. Adobe states that its Firefly models are commercially safe, trained on licensed Adobe Stock and public domain content, and outputs carry Content Credentials that show AI involvement. It offers text-to-image, image-to-image, sketch-to-image and style transfer, plus editing tools such as background removal, object removal, upscaling and generative expand. It connects with Photoshop, Illustrator, Lightroom and Adobe Express, and also lets you use partner models from other companies.

Best for: Designers and brand teams who need clear licensing and want AI inside the apps they already use.

Limitations: Plans work on a credit system, so heavy use adds up. Adobe lists a free tier with limited daily generations and paid plans starting at about $9.99 a month, but confirm current terms on its site.

Ideogram built its reputation on a problem that trips up many generators: readable text. Its current models are presented as producing clean typography in different layouts, with attention to prompt fidelity and reliable editing. It also offers an API with access to a catalog of models, and has released open weights for one earlier model for local use and customization under its license.

Best for: Posters, social graphics, logo concepts and anything where words are part of the image.

Limitations: The pages I checked did not describe a free plan, so look at current plans before relying on it. Like every generator, it still needs a human to proofread the text it produces.

Start with the job. If you want to compare several models side by side, try OpenArt. If you want to talk your way to a finished image, try ChatGPT or Gemini. If you need images that will sit inside a branded design workflow, Firefly is the safer fit. If you are chasing a distinctive look, Midjourney deserves a trial. If text must be perfect, test Ideogram.

Run the same prompt in two or three tools before paying. Differences in composition, hands, faces and text show up quickly, and they matter more than any feature list.

Reviews only go so far, because your own use case is what counts. Pick one realistic job, for example a 16:9 header image for a blog post about web performance, and run the same prompt through two or three tools. Write the prompt once and paste it unchanged, so the comparison is fair:

Then score each result on five questions: Did it follow every instruction? Are there odd details in hands, edges or objects? Is any text spelled correctly? Does it still look good when cropped to a square? Could you license it for your project? Ten minutes of this teaches more than any feature list, and it shows how much editing each tool needs before you can publish.

Most readers of this guide will put these images on web pages, where a few habits matter more than the generator you choose.

There is no single best AI image generator. The right pick depends on whether you value conversation, style, licensing, control or typography. Test two or three on a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final choice. Whatever you generate, review it for errors and label AI-made images clearly where your audience would expect it.

Are AI-generated images free to use commercially? It depends on the provider and your plan. Read the licensing terms for the tool you use, and consider vendors that publish how their models were trained if you work for clients.

Which AI image generator is best for text in images? Ideogram and ChatGPT both highlight text rendering, and Gemini lists it as a feature. Test each with your own wording and proofread the result.

Do AI images have watermarks? Some do. OpenAI adds C2PA metadata, Google adds an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible label, and Adobe attaches Content Credentials.

Can I use more than one image model without separate accounts? Yes. Platforms such as OpenArt put several models in one interface, which makes side-by-side testing easier. Check the licensing terms for each model you use.

Should I add alt text to AI-generated images? Yes, when the image carries meaning. Describe what it shows or why it is there. Purely decorative images can use an empty alt attribute, following the W3C guidance.

#10417. Dads want to do more caregiving. But here’s how workplaces hold them back

The tension between caregiving obligations and work responsibilities is often framed as a women’s issue. But it’s not just working mothers who find themselves in a bind: Plenty of men say they not only value caregiving but want to do more of it. So what’s holding them back?

A new report from the Better Life Lab at the think tank New America—which publishes research on workplace equity and family policy—indicates that men face some of the same hurdles that working women do, from limited flexibility or autonomy to a lack of structural support. 

Compared to 2019, men are taking more time off from work or reducing their work hours due to caregiving responsibilities, especially those who earn less or have children with special needs. At the same time, they continue to grapple with the kind of cultural stigma that can keep them from taking paid leave even when they’re entitled to it. 

The report’s findings suggest that many men actually want to spend more time on caregiving: In fact, a surprising number of fathers—six in ten—expressed an interest in staying home full-time. Men who helped care for adults or children with special needs were even more interested in being home. Over two-thirds of the men surveyed claim to think caregiving is as important as paid work, and that both parents should contribute equally. But the reality is quite different: Only about 40% of respondents who are caregivers and parents said they actually split those responsibilities equally.  

There are a number of potential explanations for this disparity. Over a quarter of dads said there are barriers to being the kind of parent they want to be, from their job to their financial situation; male caregivers of adults or children with special needs reported feeling this at similar rates. Men with higher-paying jobs—earning over $100,000—were even more likely to report experiencing those barriers. 

As the Better Life Lab points out, however, the data seems to suggest that men are actually underreporting the extent to which work obligations actually impact their ability to show up as a parent or caregiver. 

A third of fathers said they have “no control” over their schedules; when men reported having more control over their hours, that usually meant they couldn’t dictate which days they worked, and vice versa. The majority of parents and caregivers—74%—said they would prefer to work remotely or with a hybrid arrangement if they could, but only 40% of them currently do. Whether they were parents or caregivers, only about a fifth of respondents expressed a desire to work out of an office (though men are more likely to have a preference for the office.) Nearly two-thirds of fathers also said work obligations interfered with their family time. 

Perhaps the most interesting data point is that men are missing work or slashing their work hours at higher rates—a sign that men may be facing the same pressures that women have for many years. Among men who care for adults or children with special needs, the share who missed work due to care obligations rose from 64% in 2019 to 76% as of this year. About half of men also reported cutting back on their work hours, up from 42% in 2019.

As Fast Company reported recently, there has also been a noticeable shift in how men take time off when they become parents. Men are finally taking more parental leave, in part due to the rise of paid leave policies at the state level. Until recently, the data indicated that men took little time off, maybe a week at most, and often through some combination of sick days or vacation days or unpaid time off. 

But recent research shows that dads are filing just as many leave claims as moms in states like California, Colorado, and Washington, which have robust paid leave programs. In states that have had these programs for many years, leave claims from men have only increased, while states that introduced paid leave more recently have seen higher uptake from the start. 

In other words, it seems clear that when men have good access to paid leave, they’re more likely to take it—a pattern that also lines up with the Better Life Lab’s findings on how workplace culture can shape the caregiver experience. When fathers took leave, they overwhelmingly cited a supportive workplace as a reason (72%); many also pointed to a paid leave policy through their employer or state policy (63%). 

Still, even as men say they want to be more present, it is still women who bear the greatest burden of caregiving strain. Women are more likely to sacrifice their careers due to caregiving responsibilities, and this happens at higher rates when they are mothers. As the report’s findings suggest, women are also more quick to self-identify as caregivers and place more value on care work. 

While the culture around caregiving has shifted to some degree—especially with more men now taking leave and stepping up to care for aging parents—it’s clear that both men and women aren’t happy with the status quo.

A former CIA agent says that Jensen Huang had a target on his back, and that other engineers and executives could have also be approached by Chinese spies.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Rep. Brian Mast (R-Fla.), the chairperson of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, has said that he will issue a subpoena to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang if he does not respond to the committee’s request to meet with them on November 18 by Wednesday, November 11, according to ABC News. The House invited him to a “joint, closed-door briefing” with the heads of the House committees on China, Foreign Affairs, and Intelligence, and that they will focus on his company’s “security protocols against foreign adversary espionage.”

Congress first called the embattled CEO on November 7, a few days after rumors started circulating about Huang’s “closeness” to a particular Chinese executive suspected of passing information to Chinese intelligence. Aside from having to face Congress, Jensen Huang is also reportedly being looked into by the FBI.

It remains unclear whether Jensen Huang was targeted by intelligence agencies, but he came into prominence with the AI boom that was arguably fueled by Nvidia’s AI chips. This led to the AI race as we know it today, with the U.S. taking steps to delay China’s advancement for fears that it will use the technology for military purposes. We also don’t know if Huang Le or Alice Huang is actually a Chinese spy or an “innocent” bystander caught in a web of accusations. And, even if she did work with the MSS, the question remains as to whether she specifically targeted the Nvidia CEO or whether Chinese intelligence discovered their indiscretion and used that as a convenient tool for blackmail to extract information from her.

However, former CIA agent Darrel Blocker told ABC News that the authorities should just focus on Jensen. “I think that the biggest concern is that the focus or overfocus on just Jensen Huang and Nvidia risks losing sight of the fact that other people have been targeted by the Chinese. Are they talking to the engineers? Are they talking to other people who might have even more access than even the CEO has? So, this focus should not be singular; it should include everyone in the company, and quite frankly, every tech company out there should be looking at all the contacts that their officers and employees have.”

“It’s definitely not a one-time thing. When you run a trillion-dollar company, you know that you have a bullseye on your back; whether it’s from competition or rather from nation-states, so this is absolutely not a one-off. Jensen Huang is not the only person at Nvidia that has access to the crown jewels, so I would be looking at the other people in terms of the access that they enjoy and their proximity to Chinese nationals.”

#10413. Walmart raises prices on its entire Google TV streamer lineup

Walmart’s ultra-cheap Google TV streamers were never going to remain cheap in the age of RAMageddon, and now we’re seeing the second big price hike of the year with the whole Onn Google TV lineup seeing higher prices today.

Quietly rolled out over the past few days, Walmart has raised the price of its Google TV streaming devices by $10 a piece. The lineup now starts at about $40, up from just $15 prior to this year. Walmart’s streamers started getting higher prices back in March.

The price hikes have hit everything from the base 1080p streaming stick up to the Onn 4K Pro that rivals Google’s own TV Streamer.

Walmart’s price hikes still leave every device in its lineup as a great deal in the broader scheme of things, but it’s a huge increase compared to past generations.

For the time being, Walmart’s Onn-branded Google TV sets remain at their launch price, starting at around $250.

#10412. 18 leaders on supporting diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs

The workplace doesn’t work equally well for everyone. Some workers face obvious physical challenges that require accommodation. Others have diverse physical, cognitive, and learning needs that can stay hidden, overlooked, or brushed aside. When those needs are unmet, workers can struggle to do their best work. When their needs are understood and addressed, workers can flourish and so can the workplace.

We asked members of the Fast Company Impact Council what workplaces should consider doing to better support people with diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs. Here, 18 of those leaders share insights on what does and doesn’t help when it comes to supporting all people.

1. DESIGN WITH PEOPLE AND LIVED EXPERIENCE

The goal should be to design with people, not simply for them. Lived experience can reveal barriers that may be invisible to those creating the workplace and, when incorporated into the design, often leads to solutions that are more intuitive, flexible, and effective for everyone. That means involving people with diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs in shaping the workplace itself from the beginning—from the physical environment and technology to communication, policies, and organizational practices. Accessibility should not be an afterthought but a fundamental design principle. — Gail Zauder, AbleFly Inc.

2. MAKE COMMUNICATIONS ADJUSTMENTS

I have an employee with autism, and she has taught me a great deal about how differently people process information. When I provide clear direction, specific responsibilities, and complete expectations, she performs exceptionally well. I’ve learned to be patient and adapt my communication style to what works best for her. She is genuinely one of my best employees and consistently exceeds expectations. As leaders, we need to recognize that everyone works and learns differently. Sometimes small adjustments in how we communicate make an extraordinary difference. — Kim Wileman, No Makeup Makeup

3. DON’T MAKE PEOPLE DISCOVER THEIR ACCOMMODATIONS

Proactivity and clarity are a great starting place. Our Neurodiversity at Work Survey found that 70% of neurodivergent employees don’t know what accommodations they’re entitled to, and 60% don’t know who to ask. Leaders can change that by making accommodations known from the start, rather than something employees have to uncover or disclose to get. Tell candidates what’s available during interviews, when they join, and throughout their tenure. Flexible workspaces, meeting pre-reads, and transcription tools can make a big difference. These are things that benefit everyone and are especially helpful to those with cognitive differences. — Nathan Friedman, Understood.org

4. UPDATE POLICIES AND LISTEN

Most policies exist because of something that happened before there was a policy. So when a new hire asks why something works the way it does and the answer is “that’s how it’s always been,” that’s a good sign it’s time for an update. Beyond that, listen. Not a survey whose feedback sits untouched for months, but active listening in one-to-ones, Slack channels, team meetings, and the break room. A generic response that ignores what people said is worse than no response at all. So listen, then be clear about what can change, what can’t, and why. — Colby Proffitt, Seekr

5. DESIGN WITH FLEXIBILITY FROM THE START

Most workplaces were designed around a narrow definition of how people should think, communicate, and perform. That model is outdated. The better approach is to design for flexibility from the start, rather than forcing people to ask for exceptions just to do their best work. — Ran Mullins, Psympl

6. ASK PEOPLE WHAT HELPS THEM DO THEIR BEST WORK

Ask people what they need to do their best work, then listen. It’s harder than it sounds and worth the effort. Share agendas ahead of time, let people weigh in live or afterward, and caption and record meetings. One of my team’s culture intentions is “Clear is Kind.” Clarity takes effort and sometimes courage, and it drives better outcomes. — Michele Walsh, UNICEF USA

7. RETHINK WHO GETS TO LEAD

Living with a vision disability has taught me that systems designed for the “average” person leave talent behind. That insight fuels my commitment to inclusive leadership, and my belief that accessibility and inclusion are drivers of innovation. Across education, technology, executive search, and leadership advisory, we need to challenge organizations to rethink who gets to lead, how leadership is defined, and how different perspectives create stronger organizations. Difference is a strength, not a barrier. — Meredith Rosenberg, NU Advisory Partners

8. MAKE IT COMFORTABLE TO ASK FOR HELP

I think workplaces should spend less time trying to make everyone work the same way. People think, learn, communicate, and solve problems differently, and that can be a real strength. Leaders should be clear about the outcome they need while giving people flexibility in how they get there. It’s also important to create an environment where people are comfortable asking for what they need without feeling singled out. Some of the best ideas come from people who see a problem differently than everyone else. If we build workplaces around only one way of thinking or working, we shouldn’t be surprised when we get the same kinds of answers. — Yuping Huang, Quantum Computing Inc.

9. MAKE FLEXIBILITY THE DEFAULT

Workplaces are strongest when they embrace diverse perspectives, experiences, and ways of thinking. Innovation often comes from people who see challenges differently. By fostering an open-minded, inclusive environment and providing flexibility for different problem-solving approaches, organizations can unlock new possibilities and empower everyone to contribute at their highest potential. — Chris K Bailey, Bailey Brand Consulting

10. LOOK AT CULTURE AND POLICY TOGETHER

First, people with diverse cognitive, physical, and learning needs bring real strengths. Many colleagues with dyslexia or ADHD are standout big picture thinkers, and colleagues with autism often catch patterns others miss. Next, look at culture and policy together. Do your rigid schedules, meeting norms, or accommodation process bring out their best, or get in the way? Written agendas and flexible hours go a long way. Finally, partner with Understood.org, 2Gether-International, and Easterseals, whose research and expertise can help everyone contribute at their best. — Muneer Panjwani, Engage for Good | The Halo Awards

11. FOCUS ON HOW PEOPLE LEARN

Having someone on our team with severe depression made us rethink our interview process and how we work. We focused more on how people learn and apply what they know and asked what support they need to do that well. Workplaces should give people different ways to demonstrate their skills and flexibility in how they do the work. That can mean written instructions, accessible tools, or time to process information before responding. Ask the person, then follow through. — Andra Vaduva, SafeSpace

12. ADJUSTMENTS SHOULD BE STANDARD PRACTICE

Start by asking people how they work best and then action the answer. Intentional process can cover most of what is typically needed: agendas sent before meetings, written follow-ups after them, captions turned on, and a choice between written and spoken updates. These changes cost almost nothing and help everyone, including people who will never ask. Adjustments can be standard practice for the whole team. — James Chester, WVN

13. MAKE ROOM FOR DIFFERENCES

Make flexibility a core part of your workplace culture. Some people hit their productivity stride at 6 a.m., others at 4 p.m. Some respond quickly and others need time to digest the information for a bit before offering an idea that strengthens the plan. Some work better in silence, others with background noise or music in their headphones. Don’t put roadblocks in front of talent by thinking there’s only one way of working. Make room for those differences so everyone has an opportunity to do their best work. Make sure people feel comfortable requesting the tools or accommodations they need to thrive. — Matt Mandrella, City of Huntsville, AL

14. UNDERSTAND HOW YOUR PEOPLE WORK

The best place to start is recognizing that there is no single way people do their best work. Some need schedule flexibility, some process information differently, and others need different tools or environments to stay focused. That puts responsibility on managers to understand how their people work and create room for those differences. It should be easy for an employee to say, “Here’s what helps me do my best work,” without feeling they have to justify it. When you give people the flexibility to play to their strengths, you build a better workplace for everyone. — Matt Conlin, Fluent

15. DON’T TREAT FLEXIBILITY LIKE AN EXCEPTION

Workplaces should start by making flexibility normal instead of treating it like an exception. That means offering different ways to communicate, contribute, process information, and structure work. The goal should be to judge people by the quality of their output, not by whether they work in the exact same style as everyone else. — George Kailas, Prospero.ai

16. IDENTIFY BARRIERS AND DEVELOP PRACTICAL SOLUTIONS

I believe workplaces should focus on adapting their environments, communication, and training to different cognitive, physical, and learning needs. This matters because providing the same resources to everyone is not always enough. Employees may need accessible technology, flexible schedules, or different ways to receive information and demonstrate their abilities. Businesses should involve their teams in identifying barriers and developing practical solutions. Today, workplace inclusion should not be treated simply as a compliance issue. It should be part of how organizations develop talent and support performance. — Manuel Freire-Garabal, Gioya HEI

17. DON’T WAIT FOR AN ACCOMMODATION ASK

The best workplaces don’t wait for someone to ask for an accommodation. They design the workplace with differences in mind, recognizing that people process information, learn, and communicate in unique ways. When we reduce barriers, we enable people to spend less energy navigating the workplace and more energy contributing their talents, hopefully making the workplace better for everyone. — Namrata Yadav, Truist Foundation

18. FOSTER A CULTURE OF EMPATHY AND OPEN ACCESS

Supporting diverse needs requires shifting from only reactive accommodations to intentional workplace design. At The Trevor Project, accessibility is central to our core value of belonging. Part of why we have embraced remote work is that we’ve learned it is invaluable for accommodating disabilities, chronic illness, and learning differences. While no organization can proactively anticipate every requirement, leaders must foster a culture of empathy and open access where employees feel comfortable and empowered to request the individualized support they need to thrive. — Jaymes Black, The Trevor Project

#10410. What separates the co-CEOs that work from those that don’t?

David Ellison’s appointment of Ynon Kreiz as co-CEO of the merging Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery empire is a timely reminder: the lone chief-executive model isn’t the only way to run a company. 

Rather, in an environment characterized by complexity, speed and pressure, sharing the CEO role can be a strength that delivers increased shareholder returns.

Analysis from Harvard Business Review supports this, finding that companies with two CEOs delivered an average shareholder return of 9.5% from 1996 to 2020, outperforming the 6.9% return from single-leader companies during the same period.

The model has been adopted by several corporations, including Spotify, Comcast, Netflix and KKR. But while some have created value by having two people at the helm, others have floundered. Approached in the wrong way, it can even lead to crisis—notably at the mobile phone pioneer Research in Motion, which later became BlackBerry.

What separates the models that work from those that fail is clarity. Co-CEOs can work every bit as effectively as a single CEO when there is clarity of vision, strategy, and action, and a leadership team fully aligned behind the plan.

Here’s how to get that right—no matter how many names are at the top.

Know why you’re doing it

Research from Calgary University’s Haskayne School of Business finds that the dual-CEO model works best when there’s a specific context and clear strategic reason for doing it. This might involve overseeing different verticals or regions or, as in Lululemon’s case, bridging the transition between two single CEOs with trusted, interim co-CEOs while the board ran its search, eventually appointing Heidi O’Neill to the top job.

The news that James Gunn and Peter Safran are stepping down as co-heads of DC Studios, four years after being hired to rebuild the franchise, shows why the “why” matters. Their pairing was created for a specific mandate under a previous owner. With the studio now inside a merged Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, and Marvel veteran Jeremy Latcham taking over as president and chief creative officer, it’s the context that has shifted—not necessarily the model.

A co-CEO structure is only as durable as the strategic reason behind it, so that reason needs revisiting whenever the business around it changes.

Secure leadership alignment

Above all else, leading a company requires clarity of vision, strategy and action. Whether you have one chief executive, two, co-CEOs, or a multi-CEO model, that simple fact doesn’t change.

That involves the whole leadership coming together and getting fully aligned on the unified plan. In my experience, skipping that stage is where many companies fall down: not in the ambition of the plan, but the absence of genuine buy-in around it. Alignment will bring clarity to decision making, influence culture, and motivate people to deliver against the vision.

I recall interviewing the executive team of a major UK retailer in the early 2000s, following a major strategy review and build by McKinsey. Of the 12 executive members I spoke with, only five were fully on board with the new CEO’s strategy. Without leadership alignment from the word go, the organization struggled and was eventually acquired and swallowed up into a larger organization.

Complementary skillsets

Often, pairing two CEOs brings complementary skills that, when combined, strengthen the business. Get this right and tactical decisions also move faster, because nobody is waiting for sign-off on someone else’s patch.

At Netflix, for example, Ted Sarandos was promoted to co-CEO alongside Reed Hastings in 2020. When Hastings stepped back in 2023, Greg Peters replaced him—with Sarandos owning content, marketing and legal affairs and Peters owning technology, product and operations.

Seven years in, it’s one of the model’s starkest successes and compelling evidence for a deliberate split with distinct demarcation of roles.

Invest in the relationship

A co-CEO relationship is a strategic asset and needs the same deliberate investment that goes into any critical partnership. This means regular, structured time together and an agreed process for resolving disagreement long before one arises.

Most importantly, build up enough trust in advance that neither party has to perform confidence they don’t feel. In the early months of our partnership at Enterprise IG, [my co-CEO] Jim and I spent an enormous amount of time together. While it wasn’t always easy, it was critical to understanding each other’s strengths and weaknesses.

Mutual trust at the top has a ripple effect; it is the cultural foundation of the organization. Conversely, when leaders have vastly different personalities or lack experience working together, dual leadership structures can become strained and ultimately collapse.

The real question isn’t how many CEOs you have

Lululemon’s board turned to co-CEOs for continuity in trusted leaders while it searched for its next chief executive at a challenging pivotal moment for the business. The lesson for any organization weighing up the model is that the structure isn’t the strategy, it’s the vehicle for one.

The biggest misconception is that a single CEO automatically delivers clearer leadership. One name at the top can mask a divided executive team just as easily as two names can expose one. I’ve watched solo chiefs try to preside over strategies that half their leadership never bought into. The missing piece of the puzzle was alignment, not authority.

Co-CEOs don’t get to skip that step. From day zero, they have to define the vision, agree the plan, and draw hard lines around who owns what. That forced clarity is exactly the thing many companies assume they already have and rarely test.

#10409. Motorola accidentally confirms its ‘Razr Flex’ wide foldable is coming in 2027

Following a past teaser, Motorola has mistakenly posted an early support page that not only confirms the name of its wide foldable as the “Razr Flex,” but also confirms it will be launching in 2027.

The wide foldable trend continues to expand, with Motorola set to launch the “Razr Flex” as its iPhone Duo/Galaxy Z Fold 8 competitor. While there’s been no official announcement, Motorola did directly tease the device around Apple’s launch event, showing it in a silhouette (pictured above).

Now, Motorola has, presumably by accident, confirmed the name and release window of that wide foldable.

A support page on Motorola’s website lists the name of the device as “Razr Flex,” a name we’ve previously heard in connection with this wide form factor, while the page also directly says that the device is a 2027 release. There’s no firm release date, but it’s almost certainly not arriving before the end of the year, despite initial leaks around Motorola’s wide foldable hinting at a 2026 release.

The Motorola “Razr Flex” is expected to have a very compact form factor and, based on Motorola’s initial plans, will likely have 3D glass and at least wants to have a creaseless display, seemingly with just two cameras on the back similar to Apple and Samsung’s wide foldables. The outer display might be a mere 4.97-inch panel, much smaller than others.
