#10406. Want to move? Homeowners in these 6 U.S. cities could see their mortgage payments more than double

The housing market is locked up—and by looking at one key number, it’s easy to see why.

High mortgage rates are the main force freezing existing U.S. homeowners in place, with many would-be home sellers reluctant to part ways with much lower rates from a bygone era. But just how much more would they be paying in today’s real estate ecosystem?

It turns out, the answer is a lot. Today, the average homeowner would pay $1,100 more every month compared with existing mortgage holders for a median-priced home, according to a new report from Realtor.com. High rates, combined with stubbornly high home prices, have created a perfect storm in the market, dissolving the dream of homeownership for some while keeping existing homeowners tethered, even when they’d rather relocate.

“Home sales have been stuck for several years, and this research helps illuminate one of the major friction points for today’s homeowners who might like to move,” Realtor.com chief economist Danielle Hale said in the report. “For the typical mortgage holder, buying a home at today’s median price and current rates would mean finding about $1,100 more each month for principal and interest, before property taxes and insurance.”

On average, the cost of buying a home in the U.S. has risen from $1,332 to $2,468 per month—an 85.3% change. Those numbers are based on a median-priced home with a 14.3% down payment and a 7.32% mortgage rate, typical for the current market, compared with the 4% average rate attached to outstanding mortgages. 

Hale notes that lower rates would offer some relief, but because the cost of buying a home today has gone up so much, an incremental drop in rates wouldn’t do the trick. “Even at 6%, the typical payment would still be more than 60% higher,” she said. “For many families, that is money they simply don’t have to spare.”

Monthly costs soar 

The average national numbers are painful enough on their own, but in some U.S. cities the situation is even more dramatic, with homeowners extremely locked in to affordable monthly payments and unable or unwilling to move.

In Portland, Maine, the typical current mortgage holder pays $1,270 a month at a 4% interest rate. Buying a new home there today with 21% down costs an average of $3,392 a month—a monthly budget hit of more than $2,000. That 167% percentage increase in monthly mortgage costs under current rates is the highest in the nation, but other cities aren’t far behind. 

In the greater San Jose area, the cost of a monthly mortgage has ballooned from $2,659 for current mortgage holders to $7,039—a massive 165% increase, even for homeowners with a market-typical 26% down payment. Los Angeles; Albany, New York; Providence, Rhode Island; Boise City, Idaho; and Worcester, Massachusetts, are among the U.S. cities where homeowners looking to move would also face monthly mortgage payments that have more than doubled. In many cities where monthly payments would double under current rates, existing mortgage holders enjoy rates lower than 4%.

The lock-in effect is weakest in cities where home prices are lower and existing homeowners hold mortgages with relatively higher rates. Pittsburgh leads the pack with the smallest percentage increase in monthly mortgage payments across the 100 largest U.S. metro areas. “More affordable markets offer homeowners a smaller payment jump, not an escape from higher costs,” Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones observed in the report. 

In Pittsburgh, a current typical mortgage holder pays $1,005 a month with a 4.1% interest rate, compared with the $1,441 a new homebuyer would be expected to pay. That’s still a 43% increase in what homebuyers would need to pay on a monthly basis, but in this real estate market, it passes for very good news. In Baltimore, Buffalo, New Orleans, Detroit, and Birmingham, Alabama, the percentage increase for a monthly mortgage payment also hovers under 60%, according to Realtor.com’s new data.

Jones believes the home affordability crisis will persist, even if mortgage rates shed a few percentage points. “Lower rates alone would not erase the affordability gap,” she said. “Slower home-price growth and rising incomes would also help narrow the difference between what households pay now and what it would take to buy.”

#10402. Cognichip reveals how its new AI-assisted program can slash chip design times from months to days — automating labor-intensive tasks cuts design time down, Chief Product Officer explains

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

We speak to Cognichip's Chief Product Officer, Stelios Diamantidis, about its new AI products

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Amid the AI gold rush, one particularly fruitful vein of research is the emergence of AI-assisted workflows in electronic design automation (EDA) tools like Cadence Maestro. One such company developing such foundational models is Cognichip, which in September launched Artificial Chip Intelligence (ACI), proprietary AI models developed specifically for semiconductor engineering. ACI deploys the 'industry’s largest training dataset' across semiconductor physics and chip design principles.

ACI Enterprise is Cognichip's deployable platform, which combines these specialized models with agentic workflows, project memory, and computing infrastructure for AI inference. The company describes ACI's approach as a self-learning flywheel, a continuously evolving design environment that brings together specifications, register-transfer level (RTL), and verification results to build a design that meets power, performance, and area (PPA) targets to Pareto limits. We sat down with Cognichip to learn more.

"We have surpassed, I think, over ten million training samples in our datasets that are either things that we designed ourselves directly or things that have been generated through our synthetic technology," says Stelios Diamantidis, Chief Product Officer at Cognichip, in an interview with Tom's Hardware Premium. "We have invented new ways of creating synthetic training samples for semiconductor design. And very importantly, now that we have all these data samples, characterized data samples, we also had to invent new models so that we can capture the data, and we also had to invent new pipelines for training so that we can map the training data to the models themselves."

Cognichip says ACI can analyze specifications, explore microarchitectural options, generate RTL, verify designs, identify and fix functional problems, and optimize designs to meet PPA targets. The system can also integrate existing IP modules and even produce FPGA implementations. In principle, ACI enables engineers to focus on product requirements and architectural decisions while ACI handles much of the logic implementation and verification work.

"Build the spec, build the RTL, estimate PPA, perform optimizations, run verification, everything," Diamantidis told us while explaining how ACI can develop and evaluate multiple architectural alternatives before engineers select the preferred design. "We do not try to turn the designer into a verification engine; our system is self-verified, so we want the designer to design, and we want our system not only to do the tasks but also verify them."

Because ACI operates as a self-learning flywheel, Cognichip claims engineers can revisit earlier architectural decisions and modify their designs without restarting the entire development process from scratch.

"Architecture, microarchitecture, RTL, all abstractions in the cognitive system are aligned, so the change in microarchitecture is essentially automatically reflected in RTL, and vice versa," Diamantidis explained. "Changes in RTL are automatically reflected in microarchitecture."

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Cognichip says it has participated in more than 40 customer engagements, including projects at Renesas and SiTime. According to the company, these engagements span a wide range of chip designs, from microcontrollers and encryption engines to networking processors, RISC-V CPUs, and AI accelerators.

To illustrate ACI's capabilities, Cognichip points to an evaluation conducted with an unnamed leading fabless semiconductor company. The customer provided an existing 55-page microarchitecture specification, which ACI used to explore architecture options, develop RTL from scratch, and integrate middleware and other components. The system also generated SystemVerilog testbenches, ran simulations, identified and corrected errors, and achieved functional verification closure with over 90% UVM coverage, the company claims. According to Cognichip, the entire process took just 10 days and required only one engineer. By contrast, doing the same job using conventional methods would require a full front-end design team and approximately 20 weeks, Cognichip says.However, there are several important caveats. First up, the customer had already provided a detailed 55-page microarchitecture specification, so the platform did not begin with a blank sheet. Secondly, Cognichip has not disclosed the complexity of the design, which makes it difficult to assess how much work has been done (or rather, processed). Finally, the reported acceleration applies to front-end engineering rather than the entire chip development process, which also includes physical implementation, tape-out, manufacturing, and silicon validation.

There is a substantial difference between developing functionally, conducting verified RTL, and prepping a chip design for manufacturing. Although Cognichip's ACI can develop microarchitectures, generate RTL, and optimize designs for PPA, the platform does not perform physical implementation. Tasks such as floorplanning, placement and routing, clock-tree synthesis, physical verification, and signoff still require conventional electronic design automation (EDA) tools from companies like Cadence, Synopsys, and Siemens EDA.

"Today we do not do physical implementation," Diamantidis said. "So, place and route […] is done outside of our system."

This distinction matters because an architecture that meets its estimated PPA targets may fail to achieve them after physical implementation. For example, a design expected to operate at 1 GHz while consuming 15 W could achieve only 800 MHz at 20 W once engineers account for interconnect delays, clock distribution, buffering requirements, congestion, and other physical effects. Such discrepancies can require changes to RTL or even fundamental architectural elements, followed by another round of verification and physical implementation. In some cases, these iterations can consume weeks or months of engineering time.

"That cycle is very expensive in terms of time," Diamantidis said. "Because you are crossing design boundaries, and you are running very long runtimes and all that."

Cognichip claims that ACI can reduce these costly iterations by predicting the physical consequences of architectural decisions before implementation begins. According to Diamantidis, the company has trained its models on internally developed and characterized semiconductor designs, which enables ACI to estimate PPA characteristics and identify potential architectural trade-offs during front-end development.

"We have trained models with full physical stack visibility understanding so that the models can predict the PPA during the front-end design phase." "We can identify those trade-offs before going to physical implementation."

For example, ACI could recommend replacing two independent FIFOs with a single time-multiplexed FIFO to reduce power consumption at the expense of performance. Diamantidis used this hypothetical scenario to illustrate how the platform can evaluate architectural alternatives before engineers commit to a particular implementation.

"If you want to save power here at the cost of 10% of frequency, take these two FIFOs that you have for two different tasks, merge them, make one FIFO, and then time multiplex the two functions over one FIFO," Diamantidis explained.

Cognichip claims that ACI can estimate not only the immediate PPA consequences of such changes but also their potential impact on physical implementation.

"These trade-offs become visible now because we can tell the designer essentially in real time what some of these impacts are going to be to downstream parasitics and downstream congestion and all those kinds of effects."

The general idea behind ACI is to resolve potential architectural problems before RTL reaches physical design engineers rather than wait for implementation results and revise the architecture afterward.

"Our expectation is that there will be no loopbacks to the designer in terms of 'let us do this again because we did not meet this goal or because we ran into congestion issues,'" said Diamantidis.

However, the accuracy of these predictions is critical. Cognichip claims its models can predict certain PPA characteristics with errors of approximately 10% relative to results obtained from conventional EDA tools at the pre-route stage of physical implementation.

Cognichip evaluated its technology using 1,200 open-source designs and compared its predictions with those produced by general-purpose AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Opus and Google's Gemini. According to him, general-purpose models produced errors of approximately 50% – 100%, depending on the metric, whereas Cognichip's models reduced prediction errors to around 10%.

"[General purpose models] do not do well when it comes to predicting delays," Diamantidis said. "So, we take that down to within 10%. Again, 10% because think about it, we are running it at RTL. We do not have much physical information at all, right?"

There is an important distinction, though. Cognichip's reported accuracy refers to pre-route physical implementation results, not final post-route characteristics. Actual routing introduces interconnect and power delivery parasitics as well as other physical effects that can alter timing and power consumption. Consequently, the reported 10% prediction error does not necessarily mean that ACI-generated designs will achieve their estimated PPA targets after physical implementation and signoff.

Another question concerns the range of architectures and manufacturing technologies for which ACI can deliver such accuracy. Although Cognichip says its models incorporate semiconductor physics and account for process-dependent characteristics, it has not disclosed which foundry nodes it supports or how prediction accuracy varies across them.

Diamantidis explained that some architectural decisions are largely independent of manufacturing technology, whereas others become increasingly dependent on the target process node as development progresses.

"[Think about chip development as a tree], at the top of the tree, there are decisions that are not really foundry-specific; they are closer to microarchitecture," he explained. "Then, of course, as you start working your way down the tree, at some point you branch off because the decision may be targeted towards the TSMC node A versus TSMC node B, which will result in different prediction things. But there is a big trunk of decisions that are not really related to the node and are more related to the architecture and the way that the architecture is implemented. What we try to do is we try to think of this whole tree and provide a lot of feedback to designers, especially in a node-agnostic mode, and then, of course, at some point we have to become more node-aware."

This distinction is exceedingly important for complex CPUs, GPUs, and AI accelerators, where physical constraints tend to have substantial impact on architectural decisions. Meanwhile, Cognichip does not claim that ACI must develop such processors as monolithic designs. Instead, the company says its technology can work with individual IP blocks and subsystems, which engineers can subsequently integrate into larger chips. Of course, integration of these individually optimized IP blocks into larger chips is a challenge.

Although Cognichip says ACI can handle subsystems containing millions of logic gates (i.e., circuits that implement Boolean functions), combining multiple such designs into a complex SoC introduces additional constraints related to interconnect delays, clock distribution, shared memory bandwidth, power delivery, and floorplanning. Consequently, an architecture that achieves Pareto-optimal PPA characteristics as a standalone subsystem may no longer be optimal once integrated into a larger chip. According to Cognichip, ACI can account for these system-level interactions by aligning and optimizing individual blocks against global SoC and subsystem constraints.

“Yes, ACI can align and optimize individual blocks to global SoC and subsystem constraints. It can be applied to both flat and hierarchical optimization problems,” Diamanditis said.

Cognichip is not proposing a fundamentally new SoC development methodology, but rather automating substantial portions of conventional front-end workflows. Its ACI platform supports both flat and hierarchical optimization and can drive the partitioning and propagation of design constraints from the top level to individual blocks. According to Cognichip, ACI can decompose system-level constraints into requirements for individual components and then re-aggregate them as the blocks are integrated.

“ACI does not require a flat design methodology; it can drive hierarchical design from the top or subsystem-top down, ensuring that constraints are decomposed going into components but then re-aggregated when blocks are integrated,” Diamanditis explained.

According to Cognichip, ACI can handle individual IP blocks and subsystems containing millions of logic gates, which can subsequently be combined hierarchically into larger SoCs. The company emphasizes that the complexity of individual partitions, rather than their size, determines how effectively ACI can handle them. However, it remains to be seen how effectively ACI can coordinate architectural decisions and optimize PPA across an entire complex SoC in practice. After all, a Pareto-optimal CPU core, memory controller, and interconnect do not necessarily form a Pareto-optimal SoC, as their interactions introduce additional timing, bandwidth, power, and physical constraints.

Then again, developers of complex SoCs already address these challenges with hierarchical design methodologies, system-level optimization, and integration, so ACI’s focus on individual subsystems does not necessarily represent a significant limitation.

One of the questions that normally occurs when we talk about a tool that is only aimed at the front end of chip development is how it can handle feedback loops from physical implementation (more on this later). Such feedback loops occur when placement and routing reveal problems with timing, power consumption, die area, or routing congestion that cannot be resolved through physical design optimizations alone. In more serious cases, engineers must revise RTL, pipelines, memory hierarchies, or other architectural elements, which requires another round of functional verification, synthesis, and physical implementation. These iterations can take plenty of time and involve multiple engineering teams, which makes them particularly expensive.

Cognichip says ACI can use physical implementation results to revise RTL and microarchitecture if necessary, although this is not the company's primary focus. Instead, it aims to prevent costly iterations between front-end and back-end design teams by predicting potential problems before physical implementation begins.

"We are still focused very much on what we can do in the front end to prevent these situations rather than fix them."

If physical implementation reveals problems that require changes to RTL or microarchitecture, engineers can return to ACI with the relevant feedback (Cognichip does not specify with which file format that feedback must be submitted) and use the platform to revise their designs. Yet, ACI cannot resolve issues that require changes to the physical implementation itself, such as clock-tree synthesis or placement and routing.

"They can come back to our environments to provide additional guidance, additional prompting, additional information," Diamantidis said. "Then based on that information, we can of course perform an adaptation of the design […] if the issue can be addressed in the design itself, then of course we can do that."

Since feedback loops are expensive, yet necessary parts of semiconductor chip development flow, I had to ask that question explicitly, and the answer was that there would be no need for feedback loops with ACI. As the question and response require a lot of minutiae, we've added them in full below.

Anton Shilov: Can ACI ingest physical implementation reports from Cadence, Synopsys, or Siemens EDA tools, identify architectural bottlenecks, revise the microarchitecture, and RTL, and repeat the process until the design meets its PPA targets?

Stelios Diamantidis: Actually, this is where we depart from conventional approaches. We do not need to wait until physical implementation to identify these issues. We have trained models with full physical-stack visibility, which enables them to predict PPA during the front-end design phase.

For example, suppose you have one design that operates at a certain frequency and consumes a certain amount of power, and another that runs perhaps 20% slower but consumes significantly less power. We can identify these trade-offs before physical implementation.

Ultimately, the changes you want to make involve RTL and design constraints. Traditionally, engineers develop the RTL and constraints and hand them over to the physical implementation team. That team implements the design and reports the results. If the design fails to meet its targets, the front-end team must make changes, and the process starts again.

These iterations are very expensive in terms of time because they cross design boundaries and involve tools with long runtimes.

By training our models on the full physical stack, we can now provide immediate feedback to front-end designers. For example, we might tell an engineer: 'If you want to reduce power consumption at the cost of 10% of your frequency, take two FIFOs that serve different functions, combine them into one, and time-multiplex the two functions over that FIFO.'

That is just one example of a microarchitectural decision. We can make these trade-offs visible to designers in essentially real time because our models can predict their impact on downstream parasitics, routing congestion, and other physical characteristics.

As a result, engineers can make these decisions in a closed loop during front-end development. Once they have resolved the microarchitectural trade-offs and produced well-structured RTL, they can hand the design over to the physical implementation team. Of course, the design still has to go through physical implementation tools.

Our expectation is that there will be no need to return the design to the front-end team because it failed to meet its targets or encountered congestion problems. That is the fundamental change we aim to bring to the transition between front-end and back-end chip development.

Modern SoCs typically combine proprietary logic with third-party IP, including processor cores, memory controllers, interface IP, and other reusable components. However, developers do not necessarily have access to the RTL of licensed IP blocks, and licensing agreements may restrict how such information can be used. This raises the question of how ACI can integrate third-party IP without access to its internal implementation.

According to Cognichip, this is not a problem. ACI does not need to examine proprietary RTL to integrate an existing IP block into a larger design. Such IPs provide relevant interface specifications, including pin connections, signals, and their characteristics. ACI can then proceed with necessary integration logic and connect the IP to other subsystems.

"Most people want to take […] third-party licensed IP and optimize the integration of the IP," claims Diamantidis. "We ourselves do not ingest the guts of the IPs, nor do we try to train on any kind of data related to the IPs."

The CPO of Cognichip stressed that ACI Enterprise protects customers' IP within the company's firewalled data centers and prevents it from entering third-party AI training pipelines. The platform provides customer-controlled access to sensitive information, multi-tenant isolation, and security controls validated through a SOC 2 Type II examination. However, these protections do not supersede the licensing restrictions imposed by third-party IP providers.

Cognichip's ACI technology could substantially improve chip development productivity by automating labor-intensive tasks such as architectural exploration, RTL development, verification, and PPA optimization.

The company's benchmark with a major chip designer suggests that the potential gains could be considerable, although the complexity of the project remains undisclosed. Such capabilities could be particularly valuable for startups and newcomers to the semiconductor industry, which tend to lack engineering resources and extensive IP portfolios of established chipmakers. By reducing the time, costs, and expertise required to develop custom silicon, ACI could lower the barriers to entry and enable smaller companies to pursue a custom silicon strategy that would otherwise be prohibitively expensive.

However, Cognichip has yet to demonstrate autonomous development of a complete, physically validated chip, let alone a sophisticated CPU or GPU. The company does not perform physical implementation, and its expectations of eliminating costly feedback loops depend on the accuracy of its PPA predictions. It also remains to be seen how effectively ACI can optimize complex SoCs that combine numerous proprietary and third-party IP blocks under common power, performance, and area constraints.

Ultimately, the Cognichip technology's value for leading-edge chip developers will depend not only on how quickly it can produce verified RTL, but also on how closely its predictions match the results of physical implementation and how much engineering work it can eliminate across the entire development cycle.

Anton Shilov is a contributing writer at Tom’s Hardware. Over the past couple of decades, he has covered everything from CPUs and GPUs to supercomputers and from modern process technologies and latest fab tools to high-tech industry trends.

#10401. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella: Workers in AI-heavy jobs are having an identity crisis

For workers in AI-heavy professions, the central challenge of the AI era might not be technical—it might be psychological.  

Because AI can instantly replicate skills that once took years to master, it has left professionals in some fields struggling with their professional identities, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella said in a recent interview with French news outlet Le Point.

“In many cases, our work was built around using a set of tools to achieve certain goals,” he said. “And now suddenly we have a very powerful new tool that displaces a little bit of that sense of identity.”

Indeed, recent studies show that this kind of anxiety is common among workers impacted by AI.

In September, a survey of 2,500 knowledge workers found that 46% were frustrated that tasks requiring years of expertise can now be done by almost anyone using AI. Another 23% felt their personal expertise has been devalued.

Another recent study found that patent attorneys and staff at a firm introducing AI were also struggling with changing expectations around autonomy, expertise, and client relationships.

Anthropic itself has drawn attention to this issue. In December 2025, it conducted an internal survey of 132 of its engineers and researchers in which some said they missed writing code and wished they still had more opportunities for mentorship and collaboration.

Nadella said he hopes workers can preserve their “sense of identity and dignity” even as their jobs change dramatically. He pointed to software engineering: while engineers’ jobs have been transformed, the profession is more critical than ever, because AI’s work still needs to be verified and deemed trustworthy.

“It needs to be empowering humans and should be in human control,” he said. “If not, it should be, by definition, rejected.”

#10395. United Airlines just made a big change to flight bookings. Here’s why Delta and American aren’t following

United Airlines is banning personal AI assistants from booking and modifying tickets on its system, a change that could see other airlines follow suit.

The Chicago-based carrier updated its contract of carriage on Wednesday, prohibiting the use of AI agents to “make, modify or service a ticket, and/or purchase any service or product.” The airline cited concerns that third-party, AI assistants can expose the sensitive information of passengers and that actions taken by these tools may result in unintended purchases or account changes, it said in a statement. United also noted that automated AI actions could potentially violate Federal Aviation Administration or Department of Transportation regulations—noting, for example, that flight check-in requires passengers to acknowledge that their bags don’t contain any banned items.

The carrier, it seems, may be taking its cue from other industries as it cited that corporate leaders of banks and retail companies have taken similar actions regarding AI agents. “We encourage others to consider measures that preserve the data and security of their customers and strengthen safety,” it said.

As is often the case for the industry, other airlines may follow in United’s path, though none of the other major U.S. carriers have similarly banned AI agents—yet. Just last month, Southwest Airlines announced a plugin partnership with ChatGPT to “offer a simpler, more direct path from inspiration and discovery to booking options” with the airline.

Here’s how the other major carriers are currently approaching AI tools for booking.

DELTA AND AMERICAN: ‘EVALUATING’ AI

United is one of the three largest carriers serving the U.S., along with Delta Air Lines and American Airlines. Both of those airlines indicated to Fast Company that some AI tools continue to have booking privileges on their systems, though they’re both evaluating the landscape.

A spokesperson for Fort Worth, Texas-based American said that because AI-assisted travel shopping and booking are evolving quickly, the airline will continue to evaluate how to best serve customers. 

“Some AI tools are ​able to facilitate the purchase of American Airlines tickets,” the company said in a statement. “At the same time, protecting our ​customers and their experience remains our priority. We actively monitor traffic across our website ​and app and ​can investigate, manage ​or block automated traffic when necessary ​to protect our ​customers and their experience.”

A spokesperson for Delta told Fast Company that if an AI tool can authenticate a passenger’s credentials, then it can complete a booking on their behalf. But the company noted that even if passengers use AI tools, they’re ultimately responsible for overseeing any actions and authorizing any transactions taken by these assistants on their behalf. 

“We currently support certain interactions with AI assistants when they can be appropriately authenticated, while maintaining additional safeguards for sensitive transactions,” the company said in a statement to Fast Company. “We’re continuing to evaluate how new technology, including external AI agents, could enhance the way customers engage with Delta, always with security and the customer experience in mind.”

Representatives for Southwest, JetBlue AirWays, Alaska Airlines, and Frontier Airlines didn’t immediately respond to requests for comment from Fast Company.

Shares of United fell 1.8% on Wednesday while the broader S&P 500 Index rose 1.3%.

#10393. Which MCM Has the Best Revshare? A Developer’s Guide to Evaluating Ad Partners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An ad partner’s revenue share looks easy to compare: keeping 90% sounds better than keeping 80%. But the percentage does not tell a developer what will happen when the integration reaches production. Extra JavaScript, unstable ad slots, opaque reporting, and difficult rollbacks can change the value of an apparently attractive deal.

For teams maintaining an advertising-funded website, choosing a Multiple Customer Management (MCM) partner is partly an engineering decision. The integration affects page templates, consent handling, third-party requests, account access, and monitoring. Those changes deserve the same scrutiny as a new analytics SDK or payment integration.

This guide explains how to compare revenue shares on a consistent basis, review integration requirements, and test an ad setup without sacrificing usability. The goal is a repeatable evaluation process, rather than a universal winner.

Google Ad Manager’s MCM system lets a parent publisher manage a child publisher’s account or delegated inventory. The commercial agreement defines the partner’s services and compensation. Before comparing offers, ask which revenue streams the quoted percentage covers and whether the reporting figure is before or after that fee.

Use three separate lines in your evaluation spreadsheet:

Revenue base: record the amount to which the partner’s percentage applies. Advertiser spending, exchange-reported revenue, and publisher earnings are different figures. Do not treat them as interchangeable.

Partner compensation: identify the percentage, fixed charges, and any optional services. Ask whether the same terms apply to every demand source and ad format.

Adjustments: distinguish estimated revenue from finalized earnings, including invalid-traffic deductions and other corrections. Document when adjustments appear and which party bears them.

A common mistake is applying the partner fee twice because a dashboard already displays net revenue. Label each exported metric before using it in a comparison.

The two delegation models have different implications for access, reporting, and payments. Google’s documentation is the starting point for checking the proposed setup.

Manage Account gives the parent edit access within the child’s account while the child retains access and permissions. Payment facilitation can allocate eligible earnings from the child’s primary Ad Exchange account, but it does not automatically cover every third-party revenue stream. Confirm the invitation’s payment percentage and the separate commercial agreement.

Manage Inventory lets the parent manage delegated inventory in its own account, without access to the child account. Reporting for that inventory sits in the parent account. Google pays the parent for the relevant inventory, and the parent pays the child according to their agreement. Ask how you will obtain reports and reconcile payments.

Before implementation, document who can change tags, ad units, consent settings, and demand configuration. Agree on access for debugging and exporting historical data. A developer should be able to trace a production request to its configuration owner.

Use this table as a request-for-information checklist. It does not rank providers or assert that any named service supports a particular implementation. Obtain current terms and technical documentation for the product you are considering.

Apply the same standards to every proposal. The following examples show what a developer should ask during evaluation; each question is relevant across providers.

MonetizeMore offers publisher monetization products, including PubGuru and Traffic Cop. When evaluating its proposal, connect the commercial discussion to concrete integration questions: which scripts will load, who owns the configuration, and how will reported earnings be reconciled?

Request a sample report showing the revenue base, partner fee, and deductions as separate fields. Check whether the proposed plan includes every service shown in the sales demonstration.

Ask for a written ownership map covering Ad Manager access, tag deployment, configuration changes, and incident response. Identify who can restore the previous setup if delivery fails.

During a trial, compare representative templates and devices. Record script requests and main-thread activity alongside net revenue, using the same measurement definitions for the existing setup.

Decision criterion: choose on measured results and documented responsibilities, rather than assuming that a quoted split predicts final earnings.

For any proposal involving a header-bidding wrapper, including one from Setupad, ask how the wrapper fits into your existing page lifecycle.

Review bidder configuration, auction timeouts, and when the ad server receives targeting information. A missing bid and an unfilled ad slot are different problems.

Check whether your team can inspect the active configuration and reproduce an issue on a test page. Agree on who approves changes to bidders or timeout values.

Measure the complete sequence: consent readiness, auction start, bid responses, ad request, and rendering. Avoid attributing every delay to the same component.

Decision criterion: the implementation should be observable enough to explain failures, with commercial terms verified separately.

When assessing Publisher Collective or another managed partner, establish how the proposed setup could be tested on a limited, agreed scope.

Ask whether rollout can be restricted to selected templates or an eligible traffic cohort. Do not assume partial testing is available until the partner confirms it.

Record device, geography, page type, and consent state so differences in traffic mix do not masquerade as a revenue improvement.

Keep a dated deployment log connecting each configuration version with the reporting period.

Decision criterion: a measured comparison should separate implementation changes from changes in audience or demand.

A proposal from AdPushup or another optimization provider may involve changes to ad placement. Review the actual proposed changes with both design and engineering.

Check spacing, responsive behavior, keyboard navigation, and whether sticky placements obscure content or controls.

Reserve space for expected ad dimensions and test what happens when no ad is returned.

Compare layout stability before and after the change.

Decision criterion: additional earnings should be assessed alongside usability and performance, with explicit acceptance criteria.

For an Ezoic proposal, as for any managed platform, start with the exact integration method offered to your site.

List required changes to scripts, plugins, caching, DNS, or delivery infrastructure, if any. Do not assume that every deployment uses the same method.

Test interactions with your consent manager, content security policy, caching rules, and existing analytics.

Document how to remove the integration and restore the prior configuration.

Decision criterion: the operational cost should be visible before the first production rollout.

When reviewing a Monumetric proposal, establish which implementation work belongs to your team and which belongs to the provider.

Ask who creates ad slots, updates templates, and validates responsive sizes.

Request a support escalation process with the diagnostic information required for an incident: URL, timestamp, device, consent state, and affected slot.

Confirm onboarding costs and payment terms in writing.

Decision criterion: routine changes and production failures should have a clear owner.

Chrome DevTools helps trace third-party requests in the Network panel and investigate long tasks in the Performance panel. Capture a baseline before deployment, then repeat the same navigation and interaction sequence after deployment. Use comparable device and network settings.

Google Publisher Console helps investigate Google Publisher Tag ad slots and delivery issues. For a Prebid-based setup, use Prebid’s debugging tools to inspect the auction path. These tools answer different questions: whether the auction behaved as expected and whether the ad server delivered the requested slot.

PageSpeed Insights provides performance diagnostics; distinguish its lab results from available field data. Add web-vitals to your own monitoring when you need measurements associated with a deployment or cohort. Track LCP, INP, and CLS alongside revenue instead of treating a single Lighthouse score as the complete user experience.

Reserve responsive space for ad slots before content arrives, and investigate shifts when empty slots collapse. SitePoint’s guide to web performance optimization explains related techniques for loading JavaScript, reducing layout work, and managing long tasks.

With the same traffic volume, revenue definition, and deduction rate, the break-even increase in pre-partner revenue is: (current publisher share ÷ new publisher share) − 1. Express shares consistently as fractions or percentages. The table below shows the required lift; it is not a forecast these thresholds only describe the arithmetic. Different ad density, audience mix, deduction rates, or reporting bases can invalidate the comparison. Normalize to the same denominator, such as revenue per thousand pageviews, and compare matched periods or properly assigned concurrent cohorts.

Assume two hypothetical setups each receive one million pageviews. Here, RPM means revenue per thousand pageviews before the partner fee. For this simplified example, deductions reduce that revenue before the publisher share is applied.

Partner A: $10 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.90 after deductions × 0.90 publisher share = $8,100 net revenue.

Partner B: $12 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.98 after deductions × 0.80 publisher share = $9,408 net revenue.

The difference is $1,308 for the period despite Partner B’s lower share. These are illustrative inputs, not provider performance claims. Faster payment improves cash-flow timing; it does not, by itself, increase the revenue earned.

Use Playwright with mocked responses or test creatives in a controlled environment to check slow loading, empty slots, blocked scripts, and navigation between routes. Verify that the page remains usable when the ad system fails. Automated tests should not click live advertisements or generate artificial monetized traffic.

Separate unit tests for configuration logic, integration tests for component boundaries, and browser tests for user-visible behavior. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing provides a useful foundation. Define rollback criteria before launch, keep the control configuration available, and review finalized revenue alongside performance data before expanding the deployment.

There is no universal answer from the percentage alone. Compare current written offers using the same revenue base, then evaluate finalized net earnings, performance, access, and implementation effort.

Not necessarily. Reports may differ in timezone, currency, transaction coverage, fee treatment, and finalization date. Reconcile those definitions before treating a discrepancy as an integration bug.

Ask how renewals, volume tiers, optional services, and rate changes are handled. Record the applicable terms with the configuration and reporting period used for your evaluation.

No. If deductions and the revenue base are otherwise comparable, an 80% share needs 12.5% more pre-partner revenue to match a 90% share. Measure whether that lift exists rather than assuming it.

Do not treat any protection claim as a guarantee. Ask how suspected invalid traffic is handled, what evidence is available, and how later adjustments are reflected in your reports and payments.

Choose the setup that produces verifiable net value while meeting the site’s performance and operational requirements. A good evaluation leaves your team with reproducible measurements, understandable reports, and a practical rollback plan—not just a favorable percentage.

#10392. Can anyone really stop the AI data center boom? What could happen next now that voters have spoken

On Election Day, Americans made their opinions about the AI data center boom clear.

Now, it’s up to those newly elected legislators to rein in the country’s high-speed data center development. And there’s a lot they can actually do—particularly on the local level.

Data centers were “dealt a resounding blow in the midterm elections,” Mandy DeRoche, director of emerging issues at Earthjustice, said in a press event on Wednesday. 

Across 10 states, residents overwhelmingly voted for ballot measures to pause development, require public approval for projects, restrict data center electricity use, and link financial assurances to tax incentives. 

Congressional and state-level candidates who promised to address AI data center development also won in large numbers. The total number of governors supporting moratoria will now represent states with 41% of the country’s planned data center developments, according to Earthjustice. 

How developers fast-tracked data center projects

The data center industry has a history of limiting public information around its projects and a track record of skirting regulations and oversight.

Developers have demanded that government officials sign non-disclosure agreements, denying the public information—and a chance to weigh in.

They’ve also split their projects into smaller permits, sometimes applying for one project’s permits under multiple corporations. That strategy, called “segmentation,” can fasttrack approval and conceal the true scope of a project, allowing developers to sidestep more rigorous environmental reviews. 

Still, activists who have been working to protect communities from the harms of data centers are hopeful.

“I’ve never been part of a movement like this before,” said Sarah Friedman, co-founder of Better Data Center Project, an advocacy group that works with communities facing data center development. 

The effort to push back on rampant data center development has “created new alliances, new support, and I think new awareness of big tech and the role it plays,” she said. 

It’s also been a bipartisan issue, DeRoche notes, and there’s precedent for action.

“Harmful data center development has been reined in when a state regulator acts, and acts in its community’s interest,” she said. “And I’m optimistic that that will increasingly happen after last week, with new agency heads, with new governors.”

What can actually be done?

So what can the midterm winners actually do about data centers? 

On the state level, lawmakers can ensure that project info is public and accessible by banning NDAs and allowing for community engagement. For projects that are building their own power, lawmakers can require clean energy that meets “new, near, and now standards.”

“That means that they’re building new clean energy resources, not taking existing clean energy resources that would otherwise benefit the grid,” said Callie Rabinovitz, research and policy analyst with Earthjustice’s Clean Energy Program. “They must be online when the data center comes online to ensure there’s no fossil fuel bridge power, and they should match the data center’s load 24/7.”

State legislators can require “meaningful” environmental reviews, she added, that look at the cumulative impact of these data centers and their electricity generation. That means considering what pollution already exists in a region, and ensuring that a data center’s base power is not reviewed separately from its backup generators. 

State legislators can repeal tax incentives for data centers that have cost municipalities hundreds of millions of dollars. They can require that data center developers cover decommissioning costs, as well as the costs for any new grid infrastructure that may be needed if an off-grid data center eventually does connect to the grid, “which we expect most of them will,” Rabinovitz said. 

And they can make sure data center moratoria are actually effective. 

“Not all moratoria are created equal,” DeRoche said.

When outgoing Texas Governor Greg Abbott issued a data center pause, for example, state regulators at the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) still moved forward with 35 permitting actions for data center projects. 

Now, Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa, who campaigned on a data center pause, could appoint at least two new commissioners to TCEQ. The state also has a new Democratic commissioner for its Texas Railroad Commission, which regulates the state’s oil and gas industry, after the midterm elections. 

That commissioner “was not the oil and gas industry’s pick to win the seat,” Commission Shift, a nonprofit watchdog group, told local outlet KERA News. Texas also elected a new District Attorney who has spoken out against data centers and in support of more local involvement in those projects. 

Much of the permitting decisions for data center projects is made at the state and local level, so those legislators have lots of opportunities to regulate this industry. Congress can still take action, too. 

In the House and Senate, there’s room to establish guardrails around data center development on public lands, and require more transparency around energy and water use, and overall emissions from these projects. Congress can also pass enforceable ratepayer protections that do not incentive more gas development, and therefore more pollution.

With Democrats controlling both chambers, they can establish stronger oversight of agencies like the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), to ensure that they’re protecting communities from harmful environmental impacts with laws that are already on the books.

And because so much of the permitting happens at the local level, Congress can make sure that those local rights to regulate are protected. 

With all these changes in legislation, it could create an opportunity, Meg Slattery, staff scientist at Earthjustice said, to reframe the entire data center conversation.

“We are hoping that this creates an opportunity to refocus on addressing the climate crisis and building a just clean energy transition,” she said, “rather than orienting the government towards accommodating a hyperscale data center buildout at any cost.”

#10389. Nintendo unleashes DMCA takedown wave against open-source Switch modchip firmware, 144 tools disabled

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Even open-source isn't safe from Nintendo's legal team

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nintendo has set its famously litigious sights on the open-source firmware that powers hardware modchips on Nintendo Switch. Earlier this week, GitHub posted a DCMA request from Nintendo of America confirming that an entire network of 144 total repositories, including the parent repository, was taken down. Mod chip firmware for hardware including SX Core, SX Lite, Picofly, and HWFLY was targeted.

Early original Nintendo Switch consoles shipped with an unpatchable hardware exploit found in the device's recovery mode, which has since been patched in later units such as the Switch Lite and Switch OLED (the modchip hack doesn't work on Switch 2). As such, running custom software on patched Switches requires hardware modification, soldering a microchip like a Picofly, HWFLY, or SX Core to your Switch's motherboard. These chips require firmware, specifically code that can perform the voltage glitching required during boot to hack the Switch.

Rather than playing whack-a-mole with vendors of off-the-shelf microcontrollers, Nintendo has instead issued a DCMA takedown notice to the entire family of GitHub repos hosting this firmware. "The reported repositories offer, link to, or otherwise provide access to mod chip firmware including but not limited to SX Core, SX Lite, Picofly, or HWFLY," the DCMA request from Nintendo reads. "The firmware allows Nintendo Switch modchips to illegally circumvent Nintendo’s TPMs in order to run illegal copies of Nintendo Switch games. Specifically, during operation, the mod chip firmware at the reported repositories circumvents the technological protection measures put in place on the Nintendo Switch console by bypassing the boot sequence.

Nintendo went on to state that because these repositories are designed "primarily" to unlawfully circumvent technological measures controlling access to work protected under the DCMA, distributing mod chip firmware "constitutes unlawful trafficking in technology that is 'primarily designed or produced for the purpose of circumventing a technological measure that effectively controls access' to copyrighted works." In support of this argument, Nintendo cited criminal proceedings brought by the US Department of Justice against Team-Xecuter, a maker and seller of SX Pro, SX Core, and SX Lite modchips.

While these modchips can be (and are) used for game piracy, they unlock a host of features including running homebrew and custom applications, emulation, alternative operating systems, and more. They also enable game modding, custom themes, and even Switch overclocking. Naturally, Nintendo has chosen to focus on their utility in accessing pirated games and bypassing DRM.

Because much of this hardware and firmware is already out in the wild, the move won't have any impact on existing Switch owners who have a modchip installed. However, this will prevent firmware updates to existing modchips, as well as firmware flashing and reflashing. Naturally, it will also prevent future potential hardware modders from hacking their Switch in the same way.

Nintendo is famous for its fervent prosecution of copyright law, but developments in AI are turning this into a drastically more complicated battle. Nintendo's Zelda remake was dumped online on the same day it launched on Switch 2, while earlier this year one developer used AI to bring the original Nintendo 64 version to iOS. In July, Mario Kart Wii was recompiled for PC in 4K with over 200 tracks.

#10386. Hunter is killing Google Home, Alexa controls for its smart ceiling fans

Hunter, a major brand for ceiling fans in the US, is pulling the plug on its Google Home and Amazon Alexa bridge for controlling “HunterSMART” models in just a few weeks.

Effective January 1, 2027, Hunter will pull support for the cloud-reliant “HunterSMART” connection used for Google Home and Amazon Alexa controls. Hunter’s app and the included remote will continue to work, but connections to smart home ecosystems will not.

Beginning January 1, 2027, the legacy cloud-to-cloud connections used for app linking between the HunterSMART™ mobile apps and Google Home and/or Amazon Alexa will no longer be supported.

After this date, Google home and Alexa functionality that relies on this legacy cloud connection will no longer work for HunterSMART fans, including voice commands, scenes, and automations.

This bridge is going away in favor of a Matter connection, with Hunter having been upgrading select models to be Matter-compatible over the past year. But this is only available to “eligible” models, not everyone who was already using Hunter’s existing bridge. A list of compatible models is available on Hunter’s website, with firmware updates rolling out via the HunterSMART app.

Some models, such as the one I reviewed a few years ago, require updated hardware. Besides that, the one I have can’t even be re-connected to Wi-Fi to check for an update as Hunter’s Android app consistently crashes during the setup process.

Once the fan has been updated, it won’t be available via the existing link and will instead require re-pairing via your smart home app of choice, whether that’s Google Home or another.

…Hunter is transitioning eligible HunterSMART™ products to Matter, an industry standard designed to support broader smart-home compatibility.

If your fan is eligible for Matter-compatibility, you will be able to continue using all smart functionality within the Google Home and/or Alexa ecosystems after completing the applicable Matter migration & setup.

Once your HunterSMART fan is Matter-enabled, connecting your fan to Google and Alexa will work differently than it does today. Instead of linking your HunterSMART account to Google Home or Alexa, you will need to setup your Matter-enabled fan directly into these compatible ecosystems using the Matter setup available via the Google Home and/or Alexa apps. Matter-enabled devices do not use the legacy cloud linking service for smart controls that is being discontinued.

How can I migrate my HunterSMART fan to Matter?

Depending on your fan model and hardware configuration, a firmware update or updated hardware components may be required for Matter compatibility. Available Matter upgrade options and instructions will vary by product. You can visit our HunterSMART™ Matter resource page for the latest information about Matter compatibility, available upgrade options, and next steps.

#10385. Top 6 AI Video Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A year ago, most AI video clips lasted a few seconds, had no sound and drifted off-model by the final frame. Today several tools produce 1080p or even 4K clips with synchronized audio, camera control and reference-based consistency. The market also moves quickly: OpenAI announced it was shutting down Sora in March 2026, so the list of tools worth using has changed.

This guide compares six AI video generators that developers, designers and marketers can use today. For each one you will see what it does well, where it falls short and who it suits. Plans, credits and model names change often, so confirm details on each vendor’s site before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI video generator puts a range of video models behind one interface. Its page lists models such as Veo 3.1, Kling, Seedance, Wan, Hailuo and PixVerse, so you can run the same prompt on different engines and compare the results without managing separate accounts.

Beyond text-to-video and image-to-video, it offers start and end frame control, video-to-video restyling, motion transfer from a reference clip, a character builder for consistency, lip sync and conversational editing for tasks such as swapping a background or relighting a scene. Resolution goes up to 4K on many models, with 1080p as the default, and clips are usually up to 15 seconds, with longer limits on a few models.

Strengths: model variety, a single credit pool, editing tools alongside generation and a shared library for your clips.

Limits: credit use varies by model, so it is worth testing short clips first. Commercial-use rights depend on your plan and on the model you pick, so check the pricing and terms pages.

Best for: creators and small teams that want to compare models quickly instead of committing to one.

Veo is Google DeepMind’s video model. Veo 3.1 generates sound effects, ambient noise and dialogue together with the video, supports image-to-video, first and last frame transitions, reference images for characters or objects, and outputs 1080p and 4K. You can reach it through the Gemini app, Google Flow and the Gemini API.

Strengths: audio generated with the picture, strong prompt adherence and an API that fits into developer workflows.

Limits: base clips are short, around 8 seconds, so longer scenes rely on scene extension. Dialogue quality in short segments is still being refined, and access and quotas depend on your Google plan.

Best for: developers and marketers who want audio and video from one prompt, or who plan to automate generation through an API.

Runway has been a staple of AI filmmaking since its early Gen models. Its pricing page lists Gen-4.5 and Aleph alongside partner models, and the paid plans include all video and image models, 4K upscaling and parallel generations. Higher tiers add features such as voice clones, brand kits and HDR or ProRes output.

Strengths: polished editing tools, video-to-video workflows and a professional output pipeline that suits post-production teams.

Limits: credits are consumed quickly on premium models, and on the lower paid plans they do not roll over from month to month.

Best for: video editors and agencies that need control over shots and want AI to fit into an existing editing process.

Kling from Kuaishou has built a reputation for natural motion and expressive characters. The current release, Kling 4.0, sits alongside tools such as Motion Control, a canvas workspace, an agent and MCP or CLI batch generation for teams that want to automate production.

Strengths: realistic movement, motion transfer and batch tools for producing many short clips.

Limits: the main site does not spell out all clip-length and resolution limits for each model, so check the model page you plan to use. Pricing and free credits vary by region and plan.

Best for: social video, product shots and character-driven clips where motion quality is the priority.

Luma’s Dream Machine is built on the Ray model family. Its current page lists Ray3.2 and Ray3.14, along with a “Modify video” tool that restyles footage, extends duration and reframes aspect ratios. It also lists camera-motion controls and high-quality EXR export for professional pipelines.

Strengths: reshooting or restyling existing footage, camera-motion control and pipeline-friendly export.

Limits: paid plans start at around $30 per month (or less when billed yearly), and heavy use pushes you into the higher tiers.

Best for: filmmakers and designers who want to modify and extend footage rather than only generate new clips.

Adobe Firefly offers text-to-video and image-to-video with Adobe’s own Firefly Video Model. It also provides partner models, including Google Veo 3.1, Luma Ray3, Kling and Runway Gen-4.5, so you can compare outputs from inside one product. An Assembler and AI video editor let you trim, reorder, extend and add voiceovers, music and sound effects.

Adobe states that the Firefly Video Model is trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public domain material, and describes its output as safe for commercial use. Partner models may carry different terms, so read the details for whichever model you choose.

Strengths: a clear licensing story for the native model, integration with Adobe’s creative apps and an included editor.

Limits: most clips run up to 8 seconds, with some models reaching 15. Credits are allocated monthly, and premium partner models can use them faster.

Best for: brand and in-house teams that care about provenance and already work in Adobe tools.

The table summarizes what each vendor’s own pages say at the time of writing. “Entry paid plan” figures are US prices billed annually where the vendor offers that option.

Credit counts are hard to compare, so convert them to clips. Runway’s pricing page lists Gen-4.5 at 60 credits per 5-second clip, which means the 625 monthly credits on the Standard plan cover roughly ten clips. Adobe says its Standard plan’s 2,000 credits equal up to 40 five-second videos, and OpenArt’s Starter plan lists about 50 videos for 4,000 credits. Premium models cost more: the same Runway page lists Aleph 2.0 at 140 credits per 5 seconds.

Plan for waste. If you keep one usable clip out of every three attempts, your real cost per finished shot is about three times the sticker price. Test with short, low-resolution drafts, then render the winner at full quality.

Because every tool outputs short clips, the real work is assembly. A typical workflow is: write a shot list, generate two or three takes per shot, pick the best, normalize them to one format, join them and add captions and audio. FFmpeg handles the joining step from the command line:

Matching resolution, frame rate and codec before joining avoids glitches at the cut points. For character consistency across shots, reuse the same reference image in every prompt and keep descriptions of the subject identical.

If the clip ends up on your own site, treat it like any other media. Provide a poster image, load metadata only until the visitor presses play, and include captions so deaf and hard-of-hearing users, and anyone watching without sound, can follow along. The W3C’s guidance on captions and subtitles explains what to include, and the MDN video element reference covers the attributes below.

Also write a text description or transcript near the video. Search engines and screen reader users cannot see the footage, so the surrounding text is what explains it.

Short, structured prompts beat long, vague ones. Describe the elements in the order a director would:

Change one element at a time between takes so you can tell which change improved the result.

Marketing demos show the best of a tool, so run your own test. Use the same prompt, duration and aspect ratio on each tool, generate a few takes and score them against a short checklist. Keep notes on how many credits each usable clip costs.

Run the same test again with an image-to-video input if your workflow starts from product photos or illustrations. That often exposes differences in consistency that text prompts hide.

Which AI video generator is best? There is no single winner. Veo is strong for audio, Runway for editing, Kling for motion, Luma for modifying footage and Firefly for licensing clarity. A multi-model tool such as OpenArt helps you compare them.

Can AI video generators make long videos? Not in one pass. Most models create short clips, so longer videos are built by extending or stitching clips together.

Is Sora still available? OpenAI announced in March 2026 that it was shutting down the Sora app and API, so it is not a safe choice for new projects.

Can I use AI videos commercially? Often, but it depends on the plan and the underlying model. Read the current terms before publishing.

How do I keep a character consistent across clips? Reuse the same reference image, keep the subject description identical in every prompt, and use character or reference tools where the platform offers them.

Should I add captions to AI-generated video? Yes. Captions help accessibility and viewers watching without sound, and they give search engines text to index.

Do I need a powerful computer? No. These services run in the cloud, so a browser and a stable connection are enough.

The best AI video generator depends on what you make. Start with a short test prompt, compare a few tools, and keep the one that gives you usable clips at a sensible credit cost. If you are not sure where to begin, a multi-model option such as OpenArt lets you try several engines before you settle on one.

#10383. Googlebook apps built by Googlers add macOS-like status bar shortcuts, keyboard commands

Googlebooks are a bit starved of desktop-class features at launch, but one of Google’s pitches around this platform is how apps can be built for it, and the company’s own employees are showing off what that looks like with some clever apps that just so happen to add features that feel a lot like they’re replicating macOS staples.

In a Reddit thread, three Googlebook apps built by Googlers through Antigravity are shown off, and they’re pretty cool, regardless of how you might feel about the platform itself.

“BentoBox” is a utility that gives you control over the status bar on Googlebook, enabling you to fill the status bar with shortcuts, mini-apps, and more. It’s full of glancable information such as weather conditions, battery charging speeds, and more, but also includes useful tools such as being able to access a clipboard history, set a timer, and more. The app requires permissions including accessibility overlays, but it works like a charm.

I had no idea the status bar on Googlebook could even be used like this, and it really makes that space a lot more useful. Of course, you have to draw a comparison here to macOS, which also uses this space for app shortcuts and tools, though BentoBox offers a different type of customization.

BentoBox is available for download via Google Play for free.

The other Mac-like app here is “Booklight,” a launcher that’s basically Apple’s Spotlight, just for Googlebook. It has a transparent design and lets you search “apps, settings, and the web.”

Technically, Googlebook already can do this in its built-in search bar (in the app drawer), but Booklight has more customization and functionality, including being able to write out shorthand commands all in one sentence with the app then completing the command in the appropriate apps, as well as having a clearer UI for experiences such as typing in quick math equations. It’s also good at finding symbols (so you don’t have to remember the U+ codes that carried over from ChromeOS). Not one I found myself wanting to use, but the potential is there.

Both BentoBox and Booklight were developed by Alexander Kuscher, VP over Laptops & Tablets at Google.

The other app highlighted in Google’s thread was Wayfinder, a file manager app described as a “desktop-grade file manager.” The big bonus here is network file access for NAS boxes and other uses, but the app also just has a more advanced UX compared to Googlebook’s built-in Files app.

Googlebook’s app gap is a real problem at launch, but these apps do show there’s a lot of potential in the platform, it’s just a matter of getting developers on board – which itself will require Google and its partners to actually sell some of these laptops… classic chicken-and-egg situation.

#10380. Fitbit Sense & Versa losing 3rd-party watch faces this month, existing downloads remain

Google announced today that Fitbit Sense and Versa smartwatches will soon lose the ability to “install or update third-party apps and clocks.”

This change to the Fitbit App Gallery applies to the Versa, Versa 2, Versa 3, Versa 4, Sense, and Sense 2.

Existing “third-party apps or clock faces installed on your Fitbit device” will remain available, but you can no longer download new ones (or update them) starting November 28. As such, “if you choose to delete an app or clock face, or factory reset your device, you will no longer be able to re-install it.”

Instead, the Fitbit App Gallery will “exclusively feature apps and clock faces created by Fitbit and Google.”

The November 28 deadline also applies to third-party developers. Google will “no longer support uploading new apps and clock faces or updating existing ones.”

Google winding down Fitbit OS for smartwatches is not particularly surprising as the company has been all-in on Wear OS and the Pixel Watch. That said, the Sense 2 and Versa 4 should probably no longer be on sale.

#10379. 7 E-commerce UX Tips for Selling Car Accessories and Home & Kitchen Products Online

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Here is a question a product page should answer in about five seconds: “Will this magnetic phone mount hold my phone with the case still on?” When the answer is hard to find, shoppers tend to open another tab instead of contacting support.

Stores that sell both car accessories and home & kitchen products face an extra challenge: two very different catalogs share one site. A car accessories buyer cares about fit, mounting and compatibility. A home & kitchen buyer cares about size, capacity and how easy the product is to clean. A single generic product page frustrates both.

This guide covers seven practical e-commerce UX tips that designers and developers can apply right away, with code snippets and a checklist at the end.

A car mount and a vegetable peeler should not share the same spec table. Keep a single reusable product page component and change only the fields based on category. The layout stays consistent, while each shopper sees the details they need.

In code, this can be a config object that your template reads from:

Showing “Not specified” instead of hiding a missing field is a small habit with a real benefit: it makes gaps in your product data visible to your own team.

Shoppers look for deal-breaker information near the price and the purchase button: fit, size and compatibility. Do not bury it in an “Additional information” tab halfway down the page.

A short comparison table also works better than three paragraphs of marketing copy, because shoppers scan rather than read. Baymard Institute’s product page research consistently finds that unclear or hard-to-find specifications are a common reason shoppers hesitate.

A responsive layout is the starting point, not the finish line. People compare car accessories in a parking lot and browse kitchen gadgets while standing in the kitchen, often with one thumb.

A phone mount on a white background says nothing about how it attaches to a dashboard. Show it in a real car, from the driver’s point of view. For home & kitchen products, place the item next to something familiar, like a coffee mug, so people can judge its size.

Good alt text matters for accessibility and for image search. Compare “phone mount” with “Magnetic phone mount fixed to a car dashboard, holding a phone in landscape mode”. The W3C alt text decision tree is a useful guide for deciding what to describe.

Keep images fast, too. Responsive sources with srcset and sizes, explicit dimensions and lazy loading help prevent layout shift and slow pages:

One caveat: do not lazy-load the main product image at the top of the page. It is usually the Largest Contentful Paint element, so web.dev advises loading it eagerly.

Shoppers often search for a problem rather than a product. Few people type “seat gap filler” the first time; they type “stuff falling between car seats”. Your search, synonyms and category names should handle both.

Mixed catalogs show why this matters. Stores that cover both vehicle and household products, such as VNSPORT.org with its separate product collections, keep the two audiences apart so that someone looking for a dashboard mount never has to scroll past kitchen tools. The principle applies to any store with more than one audience: group by the shopper’s intent, not by your supplier list.

Decorative “100% secure” badges do little. What builds trust is information shoppers can check for themselves:

At checkout, show the full cost, including shipping and taxes, before payment, let people fix mistakes easily and offer guest checkout. In Baymard’s checkout usability research, unexpected extra costs and forced account creation are among the top reasons shoppers abandon a cart.

If your support inbox gets the same question every week, the product page has a gap. Answer it on the page, close to where the decision happens, instead of on a generic help page three clicks away.

You can mark up those answers with FAQPage structured data:

A reality check: since 2023, Google shows FAQ rich results only for well-known government and health websites. Do not add FAQs just to chase a snippet. Add them because they answer real questions and help shoppers decide.

What are the most important e-commerce UX tips for a car accessories store? Show compatibility and mounting details next to the price, use in-car photos, and make sure the mobile layout works with one hand.

How should product pages differ for car accessories and home & kitchen products? Use the same layout but different spec fields. Car accessories need fit, mounting and power details. Home & kitchen products need dimensions, capacity, materials and cleaning instructions.

How big should buttons be on a mobile product page? WCAG 2.2 sets a minimum target size of 24 × 24 CSS pixels. For primary actions such as “Add to cart”, around 44 pixels is more comfortable.

Do FAQ sections still help SEO? Mostly indirectly. FAQ rich results are now limited, but clear on-page answers can match long-tail searches, reduce returns and keep shoppers on the page.

How can I reduce cart abandonment? Show total costs early, offer guest checkout, display delivery times and return terms before payment, and keep the checkout form short.

Good e-commerce UX is not about clever animations. It is about answering the shopper’s question before they have to ask it. Clear specs, honest photos, thumb-friendly controls and real FAQs turn an ordinary product page into one that helps people buy, whether they are shopping for car accessories or home & kitchen essentials.

#10376. Montech King 95 3D Pro case review: The King has returned
